Rather than force a false hierarchy onto inconsistent heading levels, every `<h2>`–`<h6>` found in `#section-content`, in document order, is treated as one flat `PinellasSection` boundary (heading, raw tag-name `level`, joined text, links) — the same "don't force a shape that isn't cleanly and consistently there" call DECISIONS #45/#50 made for stpete.org's un-modeled FAQ block and stpeteha.org's heading-level ambiguity, now applied to heading *level* rather than heading *meaning*. A `<table>` (whether wrapped in a `figure.wp-block-table` or a `wpDataTables`-plugin `div`, both confirmed server-rendered with real rows, not JS-only) is treated as an ordinary block. A bare `<a href>` not wrapped in `<p>`/`<ul>`/`<ol>`/`<table>` — e.g. the hurricane page's "Apply Now" button linking to the Neighborly Software application portal — is captured into the enclosing section's `links` rather than silently dropped, since (unlike DECISIONS #50's CTA-button/heading-span collision) there's no risk of a bare anchor being mistaken for a heading in this template.

Real, current dollar figures and eligibility data are confirmed live on multiple authorized pages, independent of the `pinellas.gov/news/...` article DECISIONS #48 already flagged as unauthorized: `home-repair-loan-program` states "Fully forgivable loan up to $75,000" directly (**the exact figure DECISIONS #48 asked this round to check for on an authorized page — confirmed present**, not only on the unauthorized news article) plus a $20,000 Independent Living cross-reference, and states the program is now CLOSED as of 5/4/2026, captured verbatim rather than treated as a reason to skip the page. `pinellas-county-hurricane-home-repair-program` states "Up to $30,000 grant" plus a full 120%-AMI income table by household size ($87,600 for 1 person), and is also confirmed CLOSED ("reached capacity... New programs are expected to open this fall"). `lealman-residential-improvement-grant-program` and `lealman-commercial-improvement-grant-program` state real $15,000/$100,000 funding caps and AMI-tiered match tables. `independent-living-program` states a real $10,000 grant cap. `community-development-neighborhood-stabilization-program` states 3 real historical NSP-round funding totals ($4.6M/$8M/$17.8M) tied to no currently-open program. `florida-state-housing-initiatives-partnership-program` and `home-investment-partnerships-program` state real eligibility percentages/purpose prose but no dollar figure tied to a current program. The 2 Spanish-language pages (`ayuda-pago-inicial`, `programas-de-prestamo-...`) were built identically to the other 8, not skipped or thinned — both confirmed to carry the same real dollar figures as their English-language counterparts (`ayuda-pago-inicial` states real $50,000/$75,000 down-payment tiers by AMI band; `programas-de-prestamo-...` states the same $75,000/$20,000 figures as `home-repair-loan-program`, confirming its "revision-in-progress" URL slug does not mean thin/placeholder content).

**1 "department overview" page** (`department/housing-and-community-development/`) is confirmed live to be a structurally distinct template — its own `<h1 class="title">` hero (outside `#section-content`) plus a `div.description` mission paragraph, then a real `#quick-container` holding two `<h2>`-labelled card-decks ("Quick Facts": a real "grow by over 30,000 permanent residents" stat and a real "the County has committed $23.4 million toward 1,197 units that include 884 affordable homes" stat — **the department page independently corroborates the population/investment figures DECISIONS #48's recon quoted**; "Accomplishments": 3 narrative cards, 2 of which link off `pinellas.gov` entirely to `homesforpinellas.org`/`plan.pinellas.gov`, hosts not in `ALLOWED_SOURCE_HOSTS` and never fetched). These are modeled as `PinellasStatCard` — real, page-owned prose.

Beyond `#quick-container`, the same `#section-content` region has 5 more `<h2>` sections — "News & Stories", "Events", "Services", "Information & Resources", "Programs" — and live recon confirmed **every one is a hub, not owned content**: "News & Stories" links to 5 distinct `pinellas.gov/news/...` article pages, including the exact `pinellas.gov/news/pinellas-reopens-home-repair-program-offering-up-to-75000-in-assistance/` URL DECISIONS #48 already named as unauthorized — confirmed reachable from this hub, still not fetched. "Events" links to individual event pages; "Services" links to `/services/apply-for-*` permit/application forms; "Information & Resources" links to NOFA/work-plan documents; "Programs" links back to 7 of this round's own 10 program pages (a real subset — confirmed it never links outside them). None of these 5 sections' links are followed; each is captured as a `PinellasHubSection` of `(title, url)` pairs only — the same link-only, never-fetched shape `StpeteGrantsCrawler.parse_tiles_page()` established for hub tiles (DECISIONS #27/#32), deliberately not the full `PinellasSection` prose shape, so a hub link can never be mistaken for owned page content.

**Blocker for a future DECISIONS entry**, same hard-stop pattern as DECISIONS #27/#30/#32/#50/#51: if any of the department page's "News & Stories" article pages, "Events" pages, "Services" application-form pages, "Information & Resources" documents, or the 2 off-host "Accomplishments" links (`homesforpinellas.org`, `plan.pinellas.gov`) are wanted, they need their own explicit URL naming and recon before any crawler follows them.

`published_date` stays `None` at the `Attribution` level for every page in this module (nullable per `.claude/rules/data.md`) — no page attests one canonical effective/publication date; real dates that do appear (the hurricane program's closure note, the department page's dated news/event headlines) remain in `text`/hub-link `title` verbatim, same discipline as DECISIONS #35/#45/#50.

Fixtures (`tests/fixtures/pinellas_hcd/*.html`, 11 files) recorded live via a generic fetch during this session's recon, then confirmed parseable through this module's own parser — `pinellas.gov`'s `robots.txt` is a real 200 Yoast-plugin response (no restrictions on `/programs/*` or `/department/*`), same "real 200, no restrictions declared" shape DECISIONS #50 confirmed for `www.stpeteha.org`. Rate-limited, honest User-Agent, no further change to `BaseCrawler` beyond DECISIONS #52's host addition. 43 new tests added (10 real-fixture shape checks parametrized across the program pages, 10 page-specific dollar-figure/eligibility content assertions including the bare-anchor "Apply Now" link and both Spanish pages, 1 department-page real-fixture shape check, 3 department-page content assertions including the DECISIONS #48 unauthorized-URL confirmation and the Programs/Services hub-link scope-boundary assertions, 14 fail-loud structure-failure cases across both page shapes, 2 end-to-end mocked `crawl()` cases).
Why: The task's own instructions anticipated the department overview page would likely be structurally different from the program pages and told this round to confirm live rather than assume — recon confirmed exactly that, plus confirmed the county's own filtered-index scoping (DECISIONS #48) held: no program page or the department page links to a 12th `pinellas.gov` housing/CD page outside the named 11. The flat, level-agnostic section model for program pages and the link-only hub-section model for the department page are both the same "match what's actually there, don't guess" discipline DECISIONS #45/#50/#51 already established, now applied to a third distinct CMS.
Date: 2026-08-20

## #54 — Recon confirms Legistar exposes per-member roll-call votes as structured HTML; a "council accountability" vote ledger is a structured-crawl problem, not an NLP-extraction problem
Decision: Live recon against `pinellas.legistar.com` (the same Legistar instance already crawled by `app/crawlers/legistar.py` per DECISIONS #11/#22), plus a local REPL exercising the existing `LegistarCrawler`, confirmed per-commissioner roll-call vote data is available as real, structured HTML — not only as prose inside minutes/agenda text. This unblocks a future accountability-tracker feature (a deterministic `council_members`/`council_votes`/`agenda_items` vote ledger sitting alongside the RAG chunk store) as a structured crawl extending the existing Legistar chain, rather than requiring LLM/NLP extraction from unstructured minutes.

Confirmed chain, four hops deep:
1. `People.aspx`/`PersonDetail.aspx` (member pages) carry roster/bio/committee data only — no voting history. Dead end for "vote history by member"; votes must be assembled by walking meetings → items, not members.
2. `MeetingDetail.aspx` (per-meeting agenda grid) lists each agenda item with a summary `Result` ("Pass"/blank, not a roll call) and a File # link to `LegislationDetail.aspx?ID=<item_id>&GUID=...`.
3. `LegislationDetail.aspx` has a History table (`#ctl00_ContentPlaceHolder1_gridLegislation_ctl00`) whose "Action details" cell is not a plain `href` — it's a Telerik modal `onclick="var obj = radopen('HistoryDetail.aspx?ID=<action_id>&GUID=...', ...)"`, requiring a regex over the `onclick` attribute rather than an href read.
4. `HistoryDetail.aspx?ID=<action_id>&GUID=...` is the payload: mover/seconder, a text result, and a per-member roll-call table (`#ctl00_ContentPlaceHolder1_gridVote_ctl00`, "Person Name"/"Vote" rows). Confirmed live on two real items from the 2024-12-17 BCC meeting — File #24-2000A (Indigo Apartments housing-bond resolution, a standalone motion, "Votes (7:0)") and File #24-2091A (a bundled Consent Agenda minutes-approval item, "Consent Votes (7:0)") — both returning the same 7-commissioner roll call (Peters, Scott, Eggers, Flowers, Latvala, Nowicki, Scherer). This directly resolves the main risk going in: **consent-agenda bundling does not hide individual votes** — every agenda item, whether individually moved or bundled into consent, gets its own full per-member roll-call table.

The crawl chain a future build would add, layered on top of the existing `Calendar.aspx` grid parse (DECISIONS #22's postback replication for past date ranges): `Calendar.aspx` (existing) → `MeetingDetail.aspx` [new: parse agenda-item grid, collect `LegislationDetail` links] → `LegislationDetail.aspx` [new: parse the History table, regex the "Action details" onclick for `HistoryDetail`'s ID+GUID] → `HistoryDetail.aspx` [new: parse `gridVote_ctl00` into one `council_votes` row per commissioner]. Two new fetch hops per agenda item beyond today's crawler, both plain GET-able static HTML (no JS rendering needed — the onclick is only how a real browser triggers the modal, the target URL itself is directly fetchable), both keyed on stable ASP.NET control element IDs — fitting the existing fail-loud-on-missing-element-id pattern already used in `legistar.py`'s `parse_calendar`.

**Two items this recon leaves explicitly open/unverified — do not lock a `vote_value` enum or any schema decision on these until resolved:**
- Every roll call sampled (both examples above) was a unanimous 7-0 "Aye." The actual string Legistar uses for a dissenting vote (e.g. "Nay" vs "No" vs something else) is unverified — a real split vote has not yet been found and checked live.
- Volume/rate-limit exposure is materially higher than today's calendar-only crawl: this adds 2 extra fetches per agenda item, and a single BCC meeting can carry dozens of items, so a multi-year backfill means significantly more requests against `pinellas.legistar.com`. DECISIONS #15's existing 2.0s/host rate limit still applies and bounds this, but the volume increase is named here so it isn't a surprise when a full backfill is scoped.

**Explicitly not decided by this entry — each is its own future DECISIONS entry, not implied or pre-committed here:**
- The `category_tags` taxonomy (energy equity/Duke Energy, housing & affordability, economic development, infrastructure investment, environmental justice, public safety, youth & education) needs deciding with the coalition, not invented solo.
- The actual `council_members`/`agenda_items`/`council_votes` schema, and whether/how it FK-links to the existing chunk/embedding store, is a separate future build decision.
- Grounding-contract tightening specific to this feature (never characterize motive beyond what the record states; every claim traces to a specific Legistar item/vote; structured vote data visibly distinguished from AI-synthesized commentary) is a forward pointer for whenever the RAG-side integration is actually built, not decided here.

No crawler or schema code is authorized by this entry. This is recon-to-decision only, same discipline as every prior DECISIONS #11/#48 round: source/scope changes get an entry before code.
Why: Amber wants a deterministic vote-count answer path (e.g. "how many housing votes did Givens cast against") instead of a RAG "probably most of them" guess, which requires structured per-member vote data rather than prose summarization. The open question was whether Legistar exposes that structurally or only inside minutes/agenda prose — if only the latter, this feature would need NLP extraction with all the grounding risk `.claude/rules/rag.md` exists to guard against. Live recon on the real site, two real confirmed examples including the specific consent-agenda-bundling risk that would have been the feature's biggest silent-failure mode, settles the question: it's structured HTML, keyed on stable element IDs, fetchable with the existing crawler's own scope/rate-limit/fail-loud conventions. That's enough to justify green-lighting a future crawler-build entry; it is not itself that entry.
Date: 2026-08-21

## #55 — DECISIONS #54's accountability-data recon extended to city/county/state: four more sources, each a real but structurally distinct decision-log, none authorizing crawler or schema code yet
Decision: Per Amber's direction, DECISIONS #54's "does structured accountability data exist here" recon question was extended beyond Pinellas BCC/Legistar to four more scopes, confirmed by live raw HTTP fetches (Bash + Python `requests`/BeautifulSoup/`pypdf`/`pdftotext`), not taken on faith from any single AI summary. Findings, thread by thread:

**Thread 1 — St. Petersburg City Council.** NOT a Legistar instance (all plausible subdomain guesses returned Legistar's generic "Invalid parameters!," which is inconclusive either way). The real path is `stpete.org` → "Council Minutes & Action Taken" → per-meeting PDFs at `Government/Agendas and Documents/Council Action Taken/YYYY/YYYY-MM-DD Numbered-Action Taken Agenda.pdf`. Directly verified against Amber's own real-world test case: the 2026-08-13 PDF confirms Councilmember Givens as the lone "Nay" on Ordinance 644-H (sanitation rates) and 646-H (stormwater fee), with Floyd the lone "Nay" on the related Ordinance 645-H (water/sewer) instead — matching Amber's account exactly. Every motion follows one rigid, regex-extractable template (`Roll Call. Ayes. [...]. Nayes. [...]. Absent. [...]`) against a fixed, closed 8-person roster (Driscoll, Figgs-Sanders, Floyd, Gabbard, Givens, Hanewicz, Harting, Gerdes), present even on unanimous procedural motions — not freeform prose requiring NLP, but not structured HTML like Legistar's tables either. A free integrity check exists: Ayes+Nayes+Absent(+other) must sum to 8. **Open/unverified:** the sampled 2026-08-13 PDF's Consent Agenda A/B sections were empty that week, so whether bundled consent items get individual roll-call blocks (like Legistar) or one blanket vote is unconfirmed — needs checking against a week with real consent items. Archive backfill depth (how far back these PDFs go) was not investigated this round.

**Thread 2 — Florida Public Service Commission.** Refines DECISIONS #11's deferred finding rather than reversing it. `www.floridapsc.com`/`www.psc.state.fl.us` are confirmed genuinely JS-rendered (raw `requests.get()` returns a bare Angular SPA shell, `<app-root></app-root>`, zero inline content) — the docket-search UI still needs a headless browser, as #11 said. `robots.txt` is not a real robots file; Angular's client routing serves the same SPA shell for any unmatched path (`/robots.txt`, `/api/dockets`, `/sitemap.xml` all identical), so there are no real Disallow rules to honor or violate. The refinement: `/pscfiles/library/Orders/` is a completely separate, real, static IIS directory listing — no JS, confirmed via raw GET — holding one subfolder per year of every PSC order from 1987 through 2026, each order paired as `NNNNN-YYYY.pdf` + a `.docx` carrying its descriptive title. This is a genuine bypass of the SPA that reaches real order documents with no headless browser needed. **Open/unverified:** filenames are order-number-keyed, not utility-keyed — identifying which orders are actually Duke Energy Florida rate-case decisions (vs. the thousands of other dockets PSC handles) was not done this round; would need full-text PDF search or a docket-number cross-reference (Duke's typical PSC utility code is "DEF"). Also unconfirmed: whether PSC orders record individual commissioner-level votes at all — an order's internal vote/signature structure was not opened and checked, only the archive's existence and browsability.

**Thread 3 — Florida State Legislature.** Current district reps confirmed live: Senate District 16 = Darryl Rouson (D, `flsenate.gov/Senators/s16`); House District 62 = Michele K. Rayner (D) and House District 60 = Lindsay Cross (D), both via `flhouse.gov` (note: `myfloridahouse.gov` now redirects to `flhouse.gov`). Bill detail pages (e.g. `flsenate.gov/Session/Bill/2022/741`) show only an HTML aggregate tally ("YEAS 24 NAYS 15") under "Vote History," linking out to real, extractable per-chamber PDFs (`SenateVote_...PDF`, `HouseVote_...PDF`) — confirmed via `pdftotext -layout` on a live example, HB 741 (2022, a net-metering bill), where both Rouson and Rayner (under pre-redistricting district numbers 19 and 70) voted against; DeSantis later vetoed it. These PDFs are a fixed multi-column seating-chart grid, not one-row-per-legislator — deterministic but needs column-aware positional parsing, not simple table parsing. **Critical negative finding:** no per-member voting-history page exists anywhere on `flsenate.gov` or `flhouse.gov` member pages (House pages only expose "Sponsored Bills") — same dead end shape as DECISIONS #54's `PersonDetail.aspx` finding for the county, meaning a legislator's full voting record can only be assembled by walking every bill they plausibly voted on and pulling each PDF individually. **Open/unverified, flagged as a real trap:** district numbers in older vote PDFs don't match current district numbers (redistricting), so any future crawler mapping "Rouson" → "district 16" across years needs an explicit renumbering table, not an assumption of stability.

**Thread 4 — four other Pinellas County elected offices**, assessed honestly per office rather than forced into a BCC-style per-member roll-call shape:
- **Sheriff (PCSO)**, `pinellassheriff.gov` — strongest of the four. `/administrative-referrals` and `/cases` each carry 50+ dated, real per-item PDF links (one PDF per disciplinary/administrative-investigation case) — a genuine decision-log equivalent, PDF-bodied rather than table-bodied.
- **Property Appraiser (PCPAO)**, `pcpao.gov` — a plain `requests` call with an honest, non-spoofed User-Agent got a bare `403 Forbidden` (AWS ELB-level block); only a browser-spoofed UA succeeded. Per `.claude/rules/crawler.md` ("don't spoof a browser to evade blocking — that's a signal to stop and flag, not circumvent"), **this is flagged as a stop-and-flag blocker, in the same category as DECISIONS #11's GovTrack/PSC-SPA blockers — not a green light to crawl with a disguised UA.** What's visible behind it (`/tools-data/tax-roll/annual-reports`) is thin: once-a-year tax-roll certification PDFs only.
- **Tax Collector**, `pinellastaxcollector.gov` — confirmed essentially no accountability-relevant structured data; the office is a transactional service counter. Only `/budget/` and `/annual-reports/` exist (one PDF/year each). Honest finding: no real vote-ledger equivalent exists here, not worth building against.
- **Supervisor of Elections (SOE)**, `votepinellas.gov` — `/296/Results-Data` has a dated, multi-year per-election results index confirmed live back to 2021, plus a public calendar with dated Canvassing Board meeting/certification events (the Canvassing Board is the body that actually certifies results — the closest analog to a formal "decision" event this office has). `robots.txt` exists and disallows a few paths (`/search*`, `/currenteventsview*`, `/map*`), none overlapping pages checked. **Open/unverified:** whether individual election-results pages expose downloadable structured (CSV/precinct-level) data or only a summary page — one results page was inspected but its file links were not confirmed either way.

**No crawler or schema code is authorized by this entry.** Same discipline as DECISIONS #54 and every prior #11/#48 round: this is recon-to-decision only. Any of the four threads above becoming an actual crawler target requires its own future DECISIONS entry naming exact URLs/hosts (`ALLOWED_SOURCE_HOSTS` additions, per DECISIONS #17/#31/#52) before any code touches them — and PCPAO specifically stays a flagged blocker, not a pending build item, unless a non-spoofing access path is found.
Why: Amber asked to extend DECISIONS #54's "does structured accountability data exist" question across every level of government her coalition work actually touches (city, county beyond just the BCC, state legislature, and the state regulator that rules on Duke Energy rates) before deciding what — if anything — gets built next. Scoping this to a recon-only entry, with confirmed findings kept strictly separate from open/unverified ones per thread, keeps the same "verify against reality, don't guess" discipline as #19/#20/#22/#54 rather than letting an AI-summarized impression of four unfamiliar sites quietly become the basis for a future build decision.
Date: 2026-08-21

## #56 — St. Petersburg City Council vote/agenda data: crawler build authorized off DECISIONS #55 Thread 1's recon; DB ledger schema explicitly still out of scope
Decision: Amber authorized building a crawler against DECISIONS #55 Thread 1's confirmed source: `stpete.org`'s "Council Minutes & Action Taken" index page (`government/mayor___city_council/city_council/council_minutes_action_taken.php`, served under the already-allow-listed `www.stpete.org` host per DECISIONS #26/#31 — no `ALLOWED_SOURCE_HOSTS` change needed) and the per-meeting PDFs it links (`Government/Agendas and Documents/Council Action Taken/{YEAR}/{YYYY-MM-DD} Numbered-Action Taken Agenda.pdf`). This is the same "index page + linked per-item PDFs, both named, both authorized together" shape as DECISIONS #48's SPHA/pinellas.gov grant, not a two-step index-then-blocker pattern like #27/#32 — the PDFs *are* the content this source exists to capture, there's no thinner index-only fallback worth shipping instead.

**What this entry authorizes:** the crawler module only — fetch the index page, enumerate/fetch its linked meeting PDFs, and parse each into attributed structured Python objects (one item per numbered agenda item, carrying its roll-call breakdown and outcome label), same shape as `app/crawlers/legistar.py`'s `LegistarMeeting`/PDF-fallback path. Tests with recorded fixtures, per DECISIONS #23's no-live-network-at-test-time convention.

**What this entry does NOT authorize** (Amber's explicit boundary, restated so it isn't silently assumed later): the `council_members`/`agenda_items`/`council_votes` database ledger schema, or any FK-link to the RAG chunk store. DECISIONS #54 punted that to its own future decision and that punt still stands — this entry only extends the crawl-layer scope, not the storage layer.

**The two items DECISIONS #55 left open are resolved by live-data investigation during this build, not assumed:**
1. Whether bundled Consent Agenda A/B items get individual per-item roll calls or one blanket vote (the one PDF sampled in #55 had empty consent sections that week) — resolved by finding a real past meeting with populated consent-agenda items and checking directly.
2. How far back the PDF archive goes — no bound was investigated in #55. If a backfill bound is introduced here, it is itself a decision, stated and reasoned (not a silent default, unlike DECISIONS #12's Granicus bound which had an explicit "why 12 months" behind it).

Both findings get their own follow-up DECISIONS entry once resolved (mirroring how #32/#33/#34 followed #30's authorization) — this entry authorizes the build and states the boundary, it doesn't pre-resolve the open items.

Why: DECISIONS #55 Thread 1 already did the hard verification work — real PDF checked against Amber's own known real-world vote (Givens/Floyd dissents on the 2026-08-13 ordinances), rigid regex-extractable template, closed 8-person roster, free integrity check (roll-call counts sum to 8). That's enough confirmed structure to justify moving from recon to build, same bar DECISIONS #30/#48 used. Keeping the DB ledger/schema question explicitly out of this entry prevents the crawl-layer build from silently absorbing a much bigger, undecided storage-and-RAG-integration design question that DECISIONS #54 deliberately deferred.
Date: 2026-08-21

## #57 — `app/crawlers/stpete_council_votes.py` built; DECISIONS #56's two open items resolved with real data; a 4th vote category found
Decision: `StpeteCouncilVotesCrawler` (module `app/crawlers/stpete_council_votes.py`) parses the DECISIONS #56 index page and its per-meeting PDFs into `CouncilAgendaItem` (one per roll-call block — a lettered/numbered outline item, or the single blanket "C. Consent Agenda" vote) and `CouncilConsentLineItem` (one per numbered line under a Consent Agenda A/B listing, carrying no vote of its own). Both open items from #56 are resolved with live data, not assumed:

1. **Consent-agenda bundling.** Checked 4 real meetings with populated consent sections (2026-02-05, 04-16, 06-11, 07-23): every one shows exactly one roll-call block on "C. Consent Agenda" that blanket-approves the whole bundle — individual line items don't get their own vote. 2026-07-23 additionally shows the pull mechanism live: Consent Agenda B item 6 ("CB-6," a Prison Rehabilitative Industries office-paper contract) was pulled via 3 separate, separately-moved, separately-labeled motions (reconsider / approve-without-CB-6 / defer-CB-6), each with its own full 8-count roll call. `CouncilConsentLineItem` has no vote field by design; a pulled item's separate motion surfaces as its own `CouncilAgendaItem` with the "CB-N" label visible in description text — cross-referencing the two is left to the caller, not done in this crawl layer.
2. **Archive depth.** The index page's individually-linked, one-PDF-per-meeting structure only goes back to 2026-01-08 (21 links total, live-enumerated). Everything earlier is a structurally different shape — one PDF bundling an entire year's agendas ("...Combined.pdf"), format unverified. No number was invented (unlike Granicus's reasoned 12-month bound, DECISIONS #12): `parse_index` recognizes and skips "Combined" links (logged, not fail-loud — a known, differently-shaped, expected link) and the crawler naturally stops where the individual links stop. Reaching further back requires new parsing logic for the yearly-combined shape and is out of this build's scope — flagged, not followed, same DECISIONS #27 discipline.

**New finding beyond what #55/#56 anticipated:** the vote vocabulary is a confirmed 4-category superset, not 3. "Recused" appears live twice, in two distinct textual shapes: as an in-sentence category keyword (2026-05-14, Harting: `...Absent. Recused. Harting.`) and as trailing prose entirely outside the fixed-template sentence (2026-04-16, Harting again, a different item: a 7-name Roll Call sentence plus `(Council Member Harting recused himself.)` immediately after). `VOTE_CATEGORIES` is now `("Ayes", "Nayes", "Absent", "Recused")`; the prose-recusal case is handled by a narrow, forward-only 150-char window search directly after the roll-call sentence — not a general fuzzy fallback — specifically to avoid misattributing a recusal note to the wrong motion. No "Abstain" was observed in the 10-meeting sample checked (2026-01-15 through 2026-08-13). An unrecognized vote-category keyword (anything outside this 4-set) still fails loud, per #55/#56's instruction, rather than being silently absorbed.

Two implementation choices worth naming: outcome-label capture (`_extract_outcomes_by_order`) uses `pypdf`'s `extraction_mode="layout"` rather than `legistar.py`'s plain-text extraction, because plain extraction shoves the left-margin outcome column ("Approved"/"No action"/"Refer to HLUT"/a resolution number) to the end of each page, out of reading order — layout mode preserves it. `item_label`/`description`/`outcome` are nullable, best-effort fields (indentation-depth heuristics, not a stable element ID the way Legistar's HTML has one) explicitly separated from the roll-call vote-count contract, which stays the rigid, fail-loud part. Consent-line-item resolution numbers are not captured this round — that column is also scrambled by plain-text extraction and reconstructing it reliably wasn't verified; flagged rather than guessed at.

Tests: `tests/crawlers/test_stpete_council_votes.py`, 15 tests — 2 index-page fail-loud cases, 5 roll-call vote-count contract cases (full-Ayes, in-sentence Recused, prose-recusal fallback, unexplained-mismatch fail-loud, unrecognized-category fail-loud), 1 kerning-normalization unit test, 6 real-fixture full-meeting-PDF parses (2026-08-13's known Givens/Floyd dissents per #55, 2026-02-05's blanket consent vote, 2026-07-23's consent-pull case, 2026-05-14's in-sentence Recused, 2026-04-16's prose recusal + kerning artifact), 1 end-to-end mocked `crawl()` case. Fixtures (`tests/fixtures/stpete_council_votes/`, 6 files: the index HTML + 5 real meeting PDFs) recorded live 2026-08-21, no live network at test time, per DECISIONS #23's convention. No `ALLOWED_SOURCE_HOSTS` change — `www.stpete.org` already covers this per DECISIONS #26/#31.
Why: Both #56 open items needed real data, not a guess, before the parser's shape could be trusted — consent bundling determines whether the crawler needs one roll-call-per-line-item or one-per-block (getting this wrong would silently misattribute votes), and archive depth determines whether a hardcoded backfill number would have been fabricated versus discovered. The 4th vote category is exactly the kind of thing `.claude/rules/crawler.md`'s fail-loud rule exists to catch: a hardcoded 3-category enum would have either silently dropped Harting's recusal or crashed opaquely on the first real recused vote, rather than surfacing a clean, understood 4th category.
Date: 2026-08-21

## #58 — `app/chunking/base.py`: shared `Chunk` shape and deterministic chunk-ID scheme (PLAN.md Phase D)
Decision: A new `app/chunking` package holds the chunking module (PLAN.md Phase D — chunking only, not embeddings/pgvector/retrieval/generation, those are Phases E/F/G). `app/chunking/base.py` defines the one `Chunk` dataclass every source-specific chunker (Legistar, stpete.org pages, the AMI table, Pinellas CF, ARPA) produces: `chunk_id`, `doc_type` (a discriminator string, e.g. `"legistar_agenda_item"`, `"stpete_program_detail"`, `"ami_threshold_table"`, `"pinellas_cf_grant"`, `"arpa_funding_allocation"`), `text`, `section_label: str | None`, `attribution: Attribution` (reused verbatim from `app/crawlers/base.py`, never reinvented), and two nullable extension-point fields, `start_seconds: float | None` / `end_seconds: float | None`.

`chunk_id` is computed by `make_chunk_id(*identity_parts: str)`: a sha256 hash of the identity parts joined with the ASCII unit-separator character (`\x1f`, not `|`, so a field value that happens to contain `|` can't create a collision), truncated to 32 hex chars. Every chunker derives its identity parts from stable fields — source URL plus a section/row/item key (an `<h2>` heading, an agenda item number, a program name, a table column-program identity) — and explicitly never from the chunk's own text content, a random UUID, or an incrementing counter. This is `.claude/rules/data.md`'s idempotency principle applied to chunking, and PLAN.md Phase D's own "idempotent re-run" requirement: re-running the chunker against the same crawled data reproduces the same ID for the same logical chunk, so a re-run can upsert by ID rather than duplicate rows, even if the source prose is lightly reworded between crawls (the ID is stable to that; a content-hash ID would not have been).

`Chunk.__post_init__` rejects empty/whitespace-only text — a chunk with nothing to embed is a bug in the calling chunker, not a legitimate output, so this fails loud rather than letting a blank row reach Phase E.

`start_seconds`/`end_seconds` are a documented but unbuilt extension point: no crawler in this codebase produces transcript data yet (Granicus, PLAN.md Phase C, hasn't been built), so no chunker populates these fields this cycle. They exist so a future transcript-with-timestamps source can carry a chunk's position within meeting audio without a schema change to `Chunk` itself.
Why: A single shared shape (rather than a per-source chunk type) lets Phase E/F/G code treat every source uniformly, and keeps the attribution/citation contract (DECISIONS #6/#7, `.claude/rules/crawler.md`'s mandatory source attribution) enforced in exactly one place rather than re-implemented per source. The unit-separator join and text-content-independent ID derivation are the two concrete design choices that make "idempotent re-run" true rather than aspirational — both were deliberately chosen over the more obvious alternatives (naive `"|".join()`, hashing the chunk's own text).
Date: 2026-08-20

## #59 — `app/chunking/legistar.py`: agenda-item chunking with a stub-merge pass, whole-meeting fallback
Decision: `chunk_legistar_meeting()` chunks a `LegistarMeeting`'s `agenda_text` (the only free-text field the crawler populates — `minutes_pdf_url`/`accessible_minutes_html_url` are stored as URLs but their content is never extracted, so there is nothing else to chunk yet) by agenda item, not by fixed length. The item-boundary signal is a line holding only an item number (e.g. `"10."`) confirmed by a Legistar file-number line (e.g. `"25-177"`, pattern `\d{2}-\d{2,4}`) within the next 3 lines — requiring both, not just the bare number, so a numbered sub-list inside an item's own recommendation text doesn't produce a false boundary.

This was verified against the real recorded fixture (`tests/fixtures/legistar/accessible_agenda.html`, run through `LegistarCrawler._extract_html_text()` — the same extraction a live crawl performs) before being written into the module, not designed in the abstract. That verification surfaced a real problem: Legistar's own consent-agenda table layout (item #, file #, version, description as separate table cells) sometimes flattens under BeautifulSoup's `get_text()` into item-number cells, file-number cells, and description cells each grouped consecutively rather than interleaved per item — e.g. items 2, 3, and 4's file numbers and descriptions all land inside what the boundary scan sees as item 3's block, leaving items 2 and 4 as "stub" boundaries (a marker line with nothing else before the next boundary). A naive implementation would have emitted several near-empty item chunks (violating `.claude/rules/rag.md`'s "don't split mid-thought" concern from the opposite direction — over-splitting into content-free fragments). `_merge_stub_items()` folds each stub forward into the next boundary that actually carries content, labeling the merged chunk with the full number range it covers (e.g. `"Items 4-6"`) so no item number is silently dropped from the citation even though its content couldn't be separately isolated. Verified live: the real fixture's 66 raw item-marker boundaries reduce to 61 real, non-empty item chunks after merging, with zero stub chunks surviving.

If no item boundary is detected at all (a differently-formatted agenda, or PDF-fallback-extracted text with different line breaks — not yet observed live but not ruled out), `chunk_legistar_meeting()` falls back to one whole-meeting chunk (`doc_type="legistar_agenda_whole"`) rather than forcing item structure onto text that doesn't have it, or fixed-length-splitting a dense meeting-agenda document mid-clause.

Chunk IDs fold in `source_url` + `body_name` + `meeting_date` + the item label, not `source_url` alone — DECISIONS #21's "not viewable by the public" meetings share the bare calendar URL as their fallback `source_url`, so two different such meetings' "item 1" chunks would otherwise collide on the same ID.
Why: The item-boundary + file-number-confirmation heuristic and the stub-merge pass are both empirically justified against real data, not assumed — the alternative (fixed-length chunking of an 80,000-character single-meeting agenda blob) would produce chunks with no coherent single-item context, actively hostile to a query like "what did the Board decide on item 12." A whole-document fallback for undetected structure, rather than a forced or fixed-length split, follows the same "don't chunk what you can't verify the structure of" discipline the crawler-domain DECISIONS entries (#21, #37) already established for parsing.
Date: 2026-08-20

## #60 — `app/chunking/stpete_pages.py`: every `<h2>` is its own chunk regardless of DECISIONS #37's ambiguity; tile/freeform items chunk 1:1
Decision: `app/chunking/stpete_pages.py` covers every stpete.org page-shaped source: index/hub-page tiles (`StpeteGrantCategory`, name + optional caption — DECISIONS #27/#32), `for_south_stpete.php`'s freeform `<h3>`/`<h4>` program blocks (`StpeteGrantProgramDetail` — DECISIONS #33), the Sunrise St. Pete tile grid (`StpeteSunriseProgram`, name + eligibility + description — DECISIONS #34), and the h2-sectioned per-program detail pages (`StpeteProgramDetailPage` — DECISIONS #35/#37/#38/#39/#41). The first three are each already one coherent, self-contained unit — one item, one chunk, same reasoning as DECISIONS #62's ARPA/Pinellas CF row-based chunking. Tiles with an empty caption (the observed live state for every hub-page tile) still produce a chunk from the category name alone: thin, but dropping them would leave that part of the site's category structure completely unindexed, and a query like "what business grants does the city offer" is exactly the kind of navigational question a bare category name can still answer.

The h2-sectioned pages are the harder case. DECISIONS #37 found `<h2>` means "one generic section of a single program" (`Overview`/`Eligibility`/`How To Apply`) on most of these 25 pages, but "a distinct named program/credit/RFP-round in its own right" on a few (`for_developers.php`, `for_property_owners.php` — 13 of them, `solar.php`, `stormwater_utility_fee_credits.php`, `tax_incentives.php`), with **no reliable structural signal** (heading text pattern, `<h1>` wording) to tell the two cases apart — DECISIONS #37 deliberately left this unresolved at the crawler level rather than guess.

`chunk_stpete_program_detail_page()` resolves it at the chunking level by treating every `<h2>` as its own chunk regardless of which case it is: a query about one specific program's deadline on `for_property_owners.php` shouldn't need the other 12 programs' text in the same chunk to be answered — the exact context-stuffing risk `.claude/rules/rag.md` and the `rag-review` charter's point 2 warn about. On a genuinely single-program page, this means one chunk per `Overview`/`Eligibility`/`Documents` section instead of one chunk for the whole program — a normal, well-scoped chunk boundary, not an error, and arguably still better than one giant per-page chunk mixing eligibility rules with document-checklist text. Every chunk carries the page's `<h1>` title (and intro paragraph, if any) as a context prefix, and folds in every `<h3>`/`<h4>` subsection nested under that `<h2>` (`StpeteProgramSection.text` is only the direct-under-h2 paragraph content — subsection text lives separately and has to be explicitly included) — so a chunk pulled off a 13-program page still self-identifies which page it came from rather than reading as an orphaned fragment, even without knowing which of DECISIONS #37's two cases produced it.

Tradeoff acknowledged: this duplicates the page title (and intro, where present) across every section-chunk on a page, and on the ambiguous "N programs in one page" pages, a query broad enough to want "all of `for_property_owners.php`'s programs" now needs the retrieval layer (Phase F, not built yet) to gather multiple chunks rather than getting them pre-assembled. Both are judged acceptable: the alternative (guessing which of the two cases a page is, and building two dataclasses/chunking paths for it) risks silently misclassifying an ambiguous page like `multi-family_rental_loan_program.php` (3 `<h2>`s that read either as 3 RFP rounds of one program or 3 distinct RFPs) — a wrong, confident split is worse than a uniform, slightly-coarser-than-ideal one. If a future retrieval-quality review shows the per-section chunks on genuinely single-program pages are too fine-grained (e.g. an "Eligibility" chunk retrieved without its sibling "How To Apply" chunk producing an incomplete answer), the fix is at the retrieval layer (multi-chunk grouping by page_url) or a future DECISIONS entry revisiting this boundary — not a silent workaround here.
Why: Per the task's own framing, a chunk boundary that depends on a semantic distinction the crawler layer already proved it cannot reliably make from markup alone (DECISIONS #37) should not be re-attempted at the chunking layer either. Chunking on the one structural signal that IS reliable — the `<h2>` boundary itself — and compensating for the resulting ambiguity with an explicit context prefix, is more honest than a guess, and directly serves the "don't need the other 12 programs' text" retrieval goal regardless of which of DECISIONS #37's two cases applies.
Date: 2026-08-20

## #61 — `app/chunking/ami_table.py`: income_limits.php's 120 `AMIThreshold` rows chunk by program, not 1:1 per row
Decision: `chunk_ami_thresholds()` groups DECISIONS #44's 120 `AMIThreshold` rows (8 household sizes x 15 program-column pairs) by `program` and produces one chunk per group — 7 chunks total against the real fixture: 6 named programs (SHIP, HOME, NSP, CDBG-DR, TIF, WFH) plus one group for the bare `100% AMI` column that has no program name at all. Each chunk is a compact table of every `(household_size, ami_percent) -> dollar_amount` pair that program applies to, e.g. the SHIP chunk covers all 8 household sizes across the 30%, 120%, and 140% AMI tiers SHIP appears at on the real page (confirmed live — SHIP is not confined to a single percent column).

The real query shape this is designed to serve, named directly in the task, is "what's the income limit for a household of 4 under SHIP" — a (program, household_size) lookup. Grouping by program puts everything needed to answer that in one chunk, without retrieval (Phase F, not built yet) having to reassemble scattered per-cell fragments. Grouping by household size instead (8 chunks, one per size, covering all programs/percents for that size) was considered and rejected: it would equally answer the household-size half of the query, but `published_date` is a per-program attribute (DECISIONS #44 — SHIP/HOME/NSP/CDBG-DR each have their own stated effective date; TIF/WFH have none) with no single clean per-household-size date to attach — grouping by program keeps that attribution exact (each chunk reuses its group's first row's `Attribution` verbatim, since every row in a program's group already shares that program's own effective date) instead of forcing a group-level date that doesn't cleanly exist for the household-size axis.

1:1 per-row chunking (120 chunks) was rejected as the default alternative: near-identical numeric-only rows with no prose context and heavy cross-row redundancy (the same dollar amount often shared across household sizes' adjacent tiers) is exactly the context-stuffing risk `.claude/rules/rag.md` and the rag-review charter's point 2 name directly. A single one-chunk-for-the-whole-table alternative was also rejected: it would bury a program's own effective date next to five other programs' different dates in one chunk, undermining exact per-program citation.
Why: The AMI table's whole reason for existing (DECISIONS #43) is to let other stpete.org pages' AMI-percent references resolve to real dollar figures — chunking it in a way that doesn't actually serve a program-and-household-size lookup would defeat that purpose while still being technically "chunked." Group-by-program is the one grouping that keeps both the real query shape answerable from a single chunk and the per-group effective-date attribution exact.
Date: 2026-08-20

## #62 — `app/chunking/row_based.py`: Pinellas CF grants and ARPA allocations chunk one row per chunk
Decision: `chunk_pinellas_cf_program()` and `chunk_arpa_allocation()` each chunk one row of their respective source 1:1 — a `PinellasCFGrantProgram` (DECISIONS #11) or an `ArpaFundingAllocation` (DECISIONS #45). Unlike the AMI table (DECISIONS #61) or the h2-sectioned stpete.org pages (DECISIONS #60), neither source needed a grouping or splitting judgment call: each row is already exactly the unit a citation should point at — one named grant program with its own application timeline and award-distribution date, or one named funding allocation with its own dollar amount and description — per the task's own framing that these sources are "already coherent, self-contained units."

Row identity (for `chunk_id`) is not the same field for both sources, chosen per what's actually unique in the real data rather than a default like "row index": Pinellas CF uses `program_name`, confirmed unique across all 7 real rows even where 3 rows share one underlying program name prefix and detail_url ("Senior Citizens Services Grants: Housing"/"...: Wellness"/"...: Support" — 3 distinct funding-cycle rows, each with its own timeline/distribution text baked into the name itself). ARPA uses `category` + `amount_text` together, since no single `ArpaFundingAllocation` field is independently guaranteed unique (`category` repeats across up to 6 allocations; `amount_text` could in principle repeat across categories) — confirmed unique in combination across all 10 real allocations live.
Why: Using the row's own most-specific real-data-confirmed identifying field(s), rather than a positional index, keeps chunk IDs stable if a future site update reorders rows without changing their content — an index-based ID would silently reassign IDs (breaking the idempotent-upsert contract DECISIONS #58 exists to guarantee) the moment row order changed for a reason unrelated to content.
Date: 2026-08-20

## #63 — Accountability tracker formalized in PLAN.md as a parallel, non-load-bearing track
Decision: `PLAN.md` gains a new Section 6, "Accountability Tracker — Parallel Track," recording that the council-vote-ledger feature (recon'd DECISIONS #54-#55, first crawler built DECISIONS #56-#57) is explicitly parallel to, not part of, the original Day 1-9 critical path. It does not block and is not blocked by the load-bearing Day 1-5 path (Tier-1/1.5 crawlers → chunking → embeddings/pgvector → retrieval/grounding) or the Day 5 go/no-go checkpoint, and it sits below Section 3's existing fallback-cut order rather than competing with any item already ranked there. Two touchpoints are named as opportunistic, not scheduled: the ledger DB schema/insert pipeline could ride along Day 4's schema/index/insert-pipeline work only after pgvector is solid that day, and a read-only query endpoint fits Day 7's FastAPI phase only after `/query`/`/health`/`/documents/upload` are working. The remaining backlog (DB schema + insert pipeline, Sheriff crawler, FL Legislature crawler, FL PSC crawler, read-only query surface) gets no day assignment — each still requires its own future DECISIONS build-authorization entry before any code, per this repo's closed-scope discipline.
Why: This feature is real, coalition-relevant work, but it emerged mid-build and was never part of the original 9-day scope that Section 2's schedule and Section 3's fallback order were built around. Leaving its placement implicit risked exactly the failure mode the Day 5 checkpoint and Section 3 already guard against — real-but-unplanned scope quietly competing with or displacing the load-bearing path. Writing the placement down explicitly, in the same document that already governs cut-order discipline, makes the boundary something Amber can point to and defend rather than something inferred after the fact.
Date: 2026-08-21

## #64 — `app/chunking/stpete_commitment.py`: closes the ACCC action-item chunking gap; positional identity accepted as the lesser of two flawed options
Decision: `chunk_stpete_commitment_action_item()` chunks DECISIONS #47's `StpeteCommitmentActionItem` (16 real items on the fixture — 10 Building Sector, 6 Transportation Sector — confirmed live, not assumed) one item per chunk. Verified against the real fixture first: each item is a self-contained, independent sentence ("Deep energy efficiency retrofits and retro-commissioning of municipal facilities (F)"), not a near-duplicate numeric fragment like the AMI table's rows — so the row-based reasoning of DECISIONS #62 applies, not #61's grouping.

The identity-field question was the real decision here. `StpeteCommitmentActionItem` has no field independent of `text` that names a specific item — no item number, no name, unlike Pinellas CF's `program_name` or ARPA's `category`+`amount_text`. Two flawed options were on the table: (1) hash or prefix `text` itself, rejected outright — this is exactly what DECISIONS #58 forbids (identity derived from the chunk's own text content), and it would defeat the point of a stable ID, since a light prose edit (a typo fix) would mint a brand-new `chunk_id` instead of updating the existing chunk; (2) positional identity, `(sector, ordinal-within-sector)` — the same *shape* of ID DECISIONS #62 explicitly rejected for Pinellas CF/ARPA rows, on the grounds that a source reorder unrelated to content would silently reassign IDs.

This module uses option 2, but does not treat #62's rejection as automatically transferring — the two sources differ in a way worth naming rather than eliding: Pinellas CF/ARPA are listing-style tables where a reorder (a CMS re-sort, alphabetization) is plausible and purely presentational, unrelated to content. `st_petes_commitment.php` is one static, hand-authored HTML page — changing its order means hand-editing that page's markup, making an incidental, content-unrelated reorder far less likely. The honest caveat, stated in the module docstring rather than silently accepted: inserting one new item mid-sector (a real, plausible edit to a living work plan) would still shift every later item's ordinal and silently reassign their `chunk_id`s, even though those items' own text never changed. This is a real, known limitation, not a solved problem — accepted because no better identity field exists on this dataclass, and flagged explicitly rather than picked without comment.
Why: A chunking decision that quietly reuses a prior rejection's conclusion without checking whether the reasoning behind it actually transfers is exactly the kind of "looks considered, isn't" move `.claude/rules/rag.md`'s discipline (and this project's whole decisions-log habit) exists to catch. The two sources differ in a real, checkable way (arbitrary sortable listing vs. hand-authored static page), so the same *shape* of ID is defensible here for a different, weaker reason than it was rejected there — and the weaker reason is worth writing down so a future reviewer isn't misled into thinking this is a clean, solved case.
Date: 2026-08-21

## #65 — `app/chunking/spha.py`: closes the SPHA program-page and news-index chunking gap; per-section call reasoned fresh against DECISIONS #60, not copied
Decision: `chunk_spha_program_page()` chunks DECISIONS #50's `SphaProgramPage` (8 pages) one chunk per `SphaProgramSection`, including the `heading=None` lead section (its own chunk, labeled "Introduction" — confirmed live it occurs at most once per page, right after the crawler's own `flush()` logic) and `performance-report`'s degenerate zero-heading case (one `heading=None` section covering the whole page, producing exactly one chunk under the same rule, not a hand-carved special case).

This mirrors DECISIONS #60's per-`<h2>` answer for stpete.org's program-detail pages in outcome, but the reasoning was checked, not inherited: #60's per-heading chunking exists specifically because DECISIONS #37 found `<h2>` means two structurally different things on different stpete.org pages (a program's own subsection vs. a distinct named program), with no reliable markup signal to tell which — chunking on the heading boundary was a hedge against that unresolvable ambiguity. SPHA's shape does not carry that ambiguity: live recon (reconfirmed against all 8 real fixtures) shows every page is about exactly one program/topic, with FAQ-style subtopic headings ("Paying Rent", "Eligibility", "Renting a Unit" all under `public-housing-clients`) — there's no "is this heading a subsection or a whole new program?" question here at all. The per-section call instead rests on ordinary query-scoping: "how do I pay rent" and "what's the eligibility" are different, specific questions, and keeping them separately retrievable serves precision without a citation dragging in a page's unrelated FAQ/Recertification/Scholarship sections.

`chunk_spha_news_item()` chunks DECISIONS #51's `SphaNewsIndexItem` (20 items on the fixture, this CMS's default page size) one row per chunk, same row-based reasoning as DECISIONS #62. Unlike the commitment action items (DECISIONS #64), this source has a real non-text identity field: `item_url` (e.g. `/news-view?id=688`), a structural per-item CMS article ID, confirmed unique across all 20 real fixture items — no DECISIONS #58 tension here. Because the CMS server-side-truncates `title` (confirmed baked into the raw HTML, not a CSS ellipsis), the chunk text says so explicitly and cites `item_url` for the full article rather than presenting a truncated fragment as if it were complete — the same "cite a summary-only source honestly" discipline DECISIONS #60 applied to thin category tiles.
Why: Reusing DECISIONS #60's conclusion without re-checking its premise would have been a coincidence dressed up as a decision — SPHA's pages don't share the ambiguity that #60 exists to hedge against, so the per-section call needed its own justification (query-scoping) to actually be defensible in an interview, even though the code shape ends up nearly identical. The news-index chunker's honest handling of CMS-truncated titles keeps the "never claim content that wasn't fetched" discipline intact across a source that, unusually for this project, does carry a real per-item date.
Date: 2026-08-21

## #66 — `app/chunking/pinellas_hcd.py`: closes the Pinellas HCD chunking gap; flat per-section chunking reasoned fresh, stat-card count corrected from 2 to 6, hub sections deliberately excluded
Decision: `chunk_pinellas_program_page()` chunks DECISIONS #53's `PinellasProgramPage` (10 program pages) one chunk per `PinellasSection`, in document order, regardless of the section's raw heading level (`h2`-`h6`). This is the third source in this codebase to face a "chunk by heading boundary" judgment call (after stpete.org's h2 pages, DECISIONS #60, and SPHA, DECISIONS #65), and it was reasoned fresh rather than inherited from either: like SPHA and unlike DECISIONS #60's stpete.org ambiguity, each pinellas.gov program page covers exactly one program — `home-repair-loan-program`'s "How to Apply"/"Eligible Improvements" headings are subsections of that one program, not disguised sibling programs. This source's real complication is different in kind: heading *level* isn't semantically consistent across pages (one page uses h3/h4/h5 with no h2 at all; another uses h2/h3/h4; some headings nest inside a styled callout `<div>` rather than sit as its sibling). DECISIONS #53 already made the crawler-layer call to flatten every `h2`-`h6` into one non-hierarchical `PinellasSection` sequence rather than invent a nesting structure that isn't reliably there. Chunking follows that same flattening (one chunk per section, regardless of level), justified by SPHA's query-scoping logic (a specific "how do I apply" question shouldn't need a whole page's other sections in the same chunk) — not a re-litigation of the crawler's own flattening call.

`chunk_pinellas_department_page()` produces one chunk for `page.description` (the hero mission paragraph, real substantial prose independent of any one stat card) plus one chunk per `PinellasStatCard`, identified by each card's own heading text (confirmed unique across all 6 real cards on the fixture). **Correction to the DECISIONS #48/#52 recon quote, confirmed against the live `department.html` fixture during this build:** the department page actually carries 6 real stat cards, not the 2 ("Quick Facts"'s "Our Population is Growing" / "Affordable Housing Found Here" pair) that recon quote focused on — "Our Mission" and 3 "Accomplishments" cards (Countywide Affordable Housing Program, Pinellas County's Comprehensive Plan Update, Unincorporated Pinellas 1st Form Based Code) are also real, page-owned prose from the same `#quick-container` card-decks, and all 6 get their own chunk. The description was given its own chunk rather than folded as a prefix into all 6 stat cards (would duplicate substantial prose six times over — over-fragmentation in the opposite direction) or dropped (would lose real content the page actually states).

`PinellasHubSection`/`PinellasHubLink` get **no chunker at all** — a considered exclusion, not an oversight. All 5 of the department page's hub sections (News & Stories, Events, Services, Information & Resources, Programs) are link-only by the crawler's own dataclass shape (`PinellasHubSection` has no `text` field), pointing at pages the crawler deliberately never fetches. A chunk built from a heading plus a handful of link titles would look citable in a retrieval hit while representing zero page-owned knowledge — misrepresenting what this system actually knows. Leaving them unchunked matches the "don't follow, don't pretend to have" discipline the crawler layer already applied by never fetching them (DECISIONS #27/#30/#32/#50/#51/#53).
Why: Treating "chunk by heading boundary" as a single, transferable pattern across 3 different sources would have papered over a real difference between them (a semantic-meaning ambiguity at stpete.org vs. a structural heading-level inconsistency at pinellas.gov) — writing out why the same code shape is right for a different reason each time is what keeps this defensible, not just consistent-looking. The stat-card correction matters because a wrong count silently propagating from an old recon quote into what actually gets embedded and cited would have been exactly the kind of undocumented drift `.claude/rules/crawler.md`'s fail-loud discipline exists to prevent, even though this is chunking-layer, not crawler-layer, code. The hub-section exclusion keeps a bare navigational link from ever posing as retrievable knowledge.
Date: 2026-08-21

## #67 — rag-review pass on DECISIONS #64-66: contentless-heading chunks fixed in `pinellas_hcd.py`, stat-card ID collision guard added, positional-identity risk confirmed acceptable and left as-is
Decision: `rag-review` ran an adversarial pass on the staged #64-66 diff before commit, per this repo's global pre-commit gate. Two findings were applied; one was reviewed and confirmed as an accepted, already-disclosed tradeoff rather than something to fix.

**HIGH, applied — contentless-heading chunks in `chunk_pinellas_program_page()`.** The reviewer ran the real fixtures through the actual crawler+chunker and found 4 of the 10 pinellas.gov program pages (`home-repair-loan-program`, both `lealman-*` pages, the Spanish `programas-de-prestamo-...` page) produce a `PinellasSection` with a real heading (e.g. `<h3>Eligible Improvements</h3>`) but no text or links of its own — its real content lives in the next section(s), which the crawler's flat, level-agnostic model (DECISIONS #53) records as siblings, not children. The pre-fix chunker turned that heading into its own chunk: non-empty (passes `Chunk.__post_init__`, since the heading text itself fills it) but substantively empty, and worse, it would score highly on embedding similarity for exactly the query it can't answer (e.g. "what improvements are eligible") while carrying none of the real content — a chunk that actively misdirects retrieval rather than just being thin.

Fix: a contentless heading now forward-merges into the single next section that does carry content, producing a compound label (e.g. `"Eligible Improvements › Home Repair Loan Program"`). A level-based alternative (merge into every subsequent section at a deeper heading level) was considered and rejected during the fix, not just the original build: `home-repair-loan-program`'s "Eligible Improvements" (h3) is followed by 2 real h4 children *and then 2 more h4 siblings unrelated to it* ("Required Documents", "Application details and timeline") at the same nominal depth — a level-based merge would have falsely folded those two unrelated sections under "Eligible Improvements" too, fabricating a hierarchy the source page doesn't actually have, exactly what DECISIONS #53 already declined to do at the crawler layer for the same reason (heading levels aren't semantically consistent here). The accepted tradeoff: only the immediate next section gets the compound label — matching the single-hop forward-merge precedent DECISIONS #59 already used for Legistar's stub agenda items — so a true second/third child (e.g. "Independent Living Program," right after "Home Repair Loan Program" absorbs the merge) doesn't also carry the parent heading's label. An unmerged trailing contentless heading (page ends before real content follows it — not observed live) still emits as its own thin, heading-only chunk rather than being silently dropped, matching DECISIONS #60's "a bare label chunk is still legitimate, if thin" precedent for category tiles. New test: `tests/chunking/test_pinellas_hcd.py::test_contentless_heading_forward_merges_into_next_section_only`; the pre-existing section-count assertions were updated to reflect the new, lower chunk count (11 sections → 10 chunks on `home-repair-loan-program`).

**MEDIUM, applied — stat-card chunk-ID collision guard.** `chunk_pinellas_department_page()`'s stat-card loop used `card.heading` directly as chunk-ID identity with no disambiguation, unlike `chunk_pinellas_program_page()` two functions above it in the same file, which already guards heading-derived IDs with an occurrence counter for the (unobserved but structurally possible) case of two same-named headings. Fixed by reusing the identical occurrence-counter pattern for stat cards. New test: `tests/chunking/test_pinellas_hcd.py::test_stat_card_duplicate_heading_disambiguation` (hand-built two-card collision case, mirroring `test_stpete_pages.py`'s existing `test_h2_section_duplicate_heading_disambiguation` precedent for exactly this class of defensive case).

**MEDIUM, reviewed, no code change — `stpete_commitment.py`'s positional identity.** The reviewer worked through the alternatives independently and confirmed DECISIONS #64's conclusion: `StpeteCommitmentActionItem` genuinely has no better identity field (tier_code/tier_label are shared by 3-5 items per sector; links are populated on only 3 of 16 items), so `(sector, ordinal-within-sector)` is the least-bad option, already honestly disclosed in the module docstring and #64. The reviewer's one addition: nothing in the crawler layer (`app/crawlers/stpete_commitment.py`, already committed, out of this diff's scope) would detect a mid-sector item insertion — it only asserts the Duke Energy item is present, never a stable per-sector count — so the accepted "unlikely" risk is currently also an "undetectable" one. **Deferred, not fixed here:** a future DECISIONS entry should add a `len(items) == 16` / per-sector-count assertion to `StpeteCommitmentCrawler.parse_commitment_page()` so a future reorder at least fails loud instead of silently reassigning IDs. Logged here rather than silently dropped, per this repo's "deferrals get a DECISIONS.md note, never a silent skip" rule.

Full suite after fixes: `pytest tests/ -q` → 315 passed (313 pre-fix + 2 new defensive-case tests for the applied findings).
Why: Both applied findings were real, fixture-confirmed failure modes, not speculative — the contentless-heading case actively produces a misdirecting chunk (worse than a merely-thin one), and the stat-card gap was a real inconsistency within the same file's own established pattern. The commitment-identity finding didn't change the code because the reviewer's independent pass reached the same conclusion #64 already reasoned through — re-confirming a hard call from a second angle before shipping it is itself the point of the review gate, not just a formality to pass.
Date: 2026-08-21


## #68 — Local dev Postgres: docker-compose + `pgvector/pgvector:pg16`, pinned, not `:latest`
Decision: `docker-compose.yml` (repo root, uncommitted pending Amber's review) brings up one `db` service on `pgvector/pgvector:pg16` (resolves to Postgres 16.15 + pgvector 0.8.6, verified by pulling and running it live) — Postgres with the pgvector extension pre-available, for Phase E's embeddings work. Port bound to `127.0.0.1:5432` only, not `0.0.0.0` — this is a dev-only DB with a throwaway local password; no LAN/container-network exposure is needed since no app container exists yet in this repo (FastAPI is a later phase) and the only intended client is a host-run Python process. Data persists in a named volume (`pgdata`), reset via `docker-compose down -v`.
Why: `.claude/rules/deploy.md` bars `:latest` on anything meant to be reproducible; pinning the major version (`pg16`) matches how the official `postgres` images are conventionally pinned. Binding to localhost-only rather than all interfaces is the minimum-exposure default for a credential-light dev database, cheap to widen later if a real reason shows up (e.g. a future app container needing to reach it over a compose network, which would use the compose network directly rather than the host-exposed port anyway).
Date: 2026-08-21

## #69 — Local dev DB credentials: new `LOCAL_DB_*` env vars, deliberately distinct from `RDS_MASTER_USERNAME`/`RDS_MASTER_PASSWORD`
Decision: `docker-compose.yml` reads `LOCAL_DB_USER`, `LOCAL_DB_PASSWORD`, `LOCAL_DB_NAME`, `LOCAL_DB_PORT` from `.env` (gitignored, unchanged in structure — existing `RDS_MASTER_USERNAME`/`RDS_MASTER_PASSWORD`/`OPENAI_API_KEY` entries left untouched, new vars appended alongside them). A committed `.env.example` documents all required variable names with placeholder values. `.gitignore` gained a `!.env.example` exception carved out of its existing `.env.*` glob, since that glob was silently blocking the very template file meant to ship.
Why: `RDS_MASTER_USERNAME`/`RDS_MASTER_PASSWORD` are a specific AWS RDS credential pair for a different environment (the Fargate/RDS deploy path, `infra/`'s ECS task-def/trust-policy files). Reusing those names for a local throwaway Postgres password would conflate two secrets that must never be the same value, and would make a future prod/dev mixup (e.g. accidentally wiring compose to read prod creds, or vice versa) structurally easier instead of harder — same "make scope creep/mixups structurally awkward" discipline DECISIONS #17 already applied to the crawler host allow-list, applied here to credential naming.
Date: 2026-08-21

## #70 — pgvector extension enabled via init-script mount, not left for app code to assume
Decision: `infra/local-db/init-pgvector.sql` (`CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS vector;`) is mounted read-only into the pgvector image's `/docker-entrypoint-initdb.d/`, so the extension is guaranteed present the moment the container finishes first-time init — verified live via `psql -c "\dx vector"` against the running container. The embed-and-insert pipeline (rag-pipeline, next) does not need its own extension-creation logic.
Why: `.claude/rules/deploy.md`'s idempotency/pre-check discipline ("check if it already exists before creating") applies conceptually here too — the right layer to guarantee a piece of database setup is the layer that owns the database's lifecycle (compose + init script), not scattered as a defensive `CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS` some app code has to remember to run on every cold start. `IF NOT EXISTS` still makes the script itself idempotent against a re-run.
Date: 2026-08-21


## #71 — `chunks` table schema, dimensionality, HNSW index, and idempotency mechanics (Phase E core pipeline design)
Decision: `app/db/schema.py` defines one `chunks` table (not separate `chunks`/`embeddings` tables) with an explicit `CHUNK_COLUMNS` constant used by every INSERT/SELECT this pipeline writes (`.claude/rules/data.md` — no `SELECT *` anywhere). Column shape: `chunk_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY` (DECISIONS #58's stable sha256), `doc_type`, `chunk_text` (renamed from `Chunk.text` to avoid reading ambiguously next to Postgres's `text` type), `section_label` (nullable), `source_url`, `published_date` (nullable), `retrieval_timestamp`, `start_seconds`/`end_seconds` (nullable, unused by every current source), `embedding VECTOR(1536)`, `embedding_model`, `embedded_at` — the last three nullable, `NULL` until the embed step runs, per `.claude/rules/data.md`'s nullable-over-sentinel rule; every other column is `NOT NULL` since `Chunk`/`Attribution`'s own constructors already guarantee those fields are present by the time a `Chunk` exists.

1536 is `text-embedding-3-small`'s real output length, confirmed via one live OpenAI embedding call on 2026-08-21, not assumed from memory.

Idempotency key is `Chunk.chunk_id` alone (`app/embeddings/pipeline.py`'s `chunk_already_embedded()`), pre-checked via row existence before ever calling the paid embeddings API (`.claude/rules/deploy.md`'s idempotency-key-and-pre-check-before-any-external-write rule). A row is only ever written once its embedding is already in hand — the pipeline always embeds first (in memory, no DB side effect), then performs one atomic `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT (chunk_id) DO UPDATE`, never an insert-stub-then-update-embedding two-step. That means "row exists" and "row is fully embedded" are the same state by construction — no ambiguous partial-embedded state is possible, and existence alone is a complete pre-check. The one failure mode that remains — embed call succeeds (money spent) but the DB write then fails — is handled by rolling back the local transaction, logging the rollback outcome, and re-raising the *original* exception, never the rollback's own exception (`.claude/rules/data.md`'s best-effort-cleanup rule); there is deliberately no "cleanup" action on the OpenAI side, since the embeddings endpoint is stateless and created no remote resource to delete.

Index: HNSW (`vector_cosine_ops`), not ivfflat. Reasoned specifically against this corpus's real scale (342 chunks across 8 sources as of this build, growing incrementally rather than loaded once upfront) — ivfflat's `lists` parameter needs a representative row count to size meaningfully and the index wants rebuilding/retuning as the table grows; HNSW has neither requirement and degrades gracefully as rows are added. The usual HNSW-vs-ivfflat build-time/memory tradeoff is a non-issue at hundreds-to-low-thousands of rows — the real differentiator at this N is operational simplicity, which favors HNSW outright here. `vector_cosine_ops` matches the cosine-distance `<=>` operator Phase F's retrieval code will use; `text-embedding-3-small` embeddings are documented unit-normalized, so cosine and dot-product rank identically, but cosine gives a bounded distance more legible for a relevance threshold (DECISIONS #7) than an unbounded inner product.

Batch-processing robustness: `embed_and_insert_chunks()` continues past a genuine per-chunk failure (one malformed chunk shouldn't block the other 341, `.claude/rules/crawler.md`'s fail-loud-at-item-granularity spirit) but does NOT absorb a connection-level failure (`psycopg.OperationalError`/`InterfaceError`) into that same per-chunk failure count — a dropped DB connection mid-run aborts the batch loudly instead of replaying as N misleading "failed chunk" entries. Model-output-is-untrusted-input handling (`.claude/rules/rag.md`): `_validate_embedding()` in `app/embeddings/client.py` checks response shape, length, numeric type, AND finiteness (`math.isfinite`) on every value — a NaN/Infinity value passes a bare `isinstance(v, (int, float))` check but would corrupt any downstream cosine-distance computation, so finiteness is checked explicitly, not assumed. A malformed response shape (e.g. missing `.embedding` on the first data entry) raises the module's own `EmbeddingValidationError`, never a raw `AttributeError`.

Failure classification (`.claude/rules/rag.md` — classify before retrying): `get_embedding()` retries only `RateLimitError`/`APITimeoutError`/`APIConnectionError`/`InternalServerError` (bounded, 3 attempts, exponential backoff) — an explicit allowlist, not a blocklist, since "classify before retrying" is safer built as "these specific things are worth retrying" than "everything except these specific things." Everything else (bad request, auth failure) fails immediately with the original error.

Known limitation, disclosed not fixed: `MAX_INPUT_CHARS = 24_000` is a belt-and-suspenders pre-flight guard against `text-embedding-3-small`'s real 8,191-token limit, using a ~4-chars/token conservative-average-for-English-prose heuristic, not real tokenization (`tiktoken` wasn't judged worth a new dependency for one safety-net check — chunk sizing is each chunking module's own job, DECISIONS #59-66, this is a backstop, not the primary control). `rag-review` confirmed this is safe to leave as-is: a chunk that's genuinely too dense (e.g. a numeric/financial-heavy AMI table row) to fit under-counts at 4 chars/token and could still exceed the real token limit, but the failure mode is a loud, non-retried `BadRequestError` from `get_embedding()`'s own classification above, not silent corruption or a wrong embedding. Deferred as a documented heuristic, not treated as a bug.
Why: Each piece above was reasoned against this project's actual scale and actual rules rather than a generic default — a join between two always-1:1 tables buys nothing and doubles the write/partial-failure surface for no benefit; embedding-first-then-atomic-upsert collapses the cleanup surface to near-zero by construction instead of building recovery logic for a self-inflicted two-step write; HNSW's usual tradeoffs against ivfflat don't bind at this corpus's real size, so the tie-break is operational simplicity, not a rule-of-thumb.
Date: 2026-08-21

## #72 — Test-mocking precedents established for the OpenAI SDK and for Postgres-backed pipeline code
Decision: Two new test-strategy precedents, neither previously established in this repo:
1. **`openai` SDK mocking** (`tests/embeddings/test_client.py`, 15 tests): `get_embedding()` takes an `openai.OpenAI` client as a parameter rather than constructing one internally, so tests substitute a `MagicMock` directly for that injected client (mocking `.embeddings.create`) rather than intercepting HTTP traffic the way `responses` does for crawler `requests` calls (DECISIONS #23). Retry-path tests instantiate real `openai.RateLimitError`/etc. exception objects (backed by real `httpx2.Request`/`httpx2.Response` objects, not mocks of those) so the retry-classification logic is exercised against the actual exception shapes the real SDK raises, not a hand-waved stand-in. No live OpenAI call happens anywhere in the test suite.
2. **Postgres-backed pipeline code** (`tests/db/`, 7 tests): runs against the REAL local Postgres from DECISIONS #68-70 (not mocked, not a separate throwaway DB), using a transactional-rollback-per-test pattern — each test gets its own connection, writes with `commit=False`, and rolls back at teardown, verified to leave zero residual rows after a full suite run. Schema (`apply_schema()`) is applied once per test session, not per test, since its DDL is idempotent (`CREATE TABLE/INDEX IF NOT EXISTS`).
Why: The SDK boundary our own code actually depends on is the injected client object, not an HTTP layer underneath it — mocking at that seam is more direct than reconstructing `responses`' HTTP-interception approach for a dependency shape it wasn't built for. For Postgres, this pipeline's entire point is real SQL behavior (upsert-by-id against a real `vector` column, a real index) — a mock would test nothing meaningful about the one part of this code that's riskiest to get wrong, and transactional rollback against the already-running dev DB (rather than standing up a second disposable test database) reuses infrastructure that already exists per DECISIONS #68 instead of adding a third Postgres instance to manage.
Date: 2026-08-21

## #73 — Full 342-chunk real Tier-1 corpus embedded and inserted; validated with 7 real hand-picked queries
Decision: The complete real chunk output of all 8 chunking modules (14 `doc_type`s: Legistar agenda items, stpete.org grant-category/program-detail/tile/freeform pages, Pinellas CF grants, ARPA allocations, AMI income-limit thresholds, SPHA program sections/news items, Pinellas HCD program/department content, Duke Energy commitment action items — 342 chunks total) was run through the embed-and-insert pipeline against the real local Postgres. Confirmed post-run: 342 rows, 342 distinct `chunk_id`s (no collisions across sources), 0 `NULL` embeddings, 0 pipeline failures. Real cost: ~$0.0014 (≈71K tokens at OpenAI's published $0.02/1M-token rate for `text-embedding-3-small` — not independently re-verified against a live billing dashboard this session, but negligible at this volume regardless of proxy-estimate error margin). Run happened in two passes (a 91-chunk validated slice first, then the remaining 251) — the second pass's re-submission of the first 91 cost nothing extra, since the pipeline's `chunk_id` pre-check (DECISIONS #71) skipped them automatically.
