7 real hand-picked queries were run against the live corpus via real cosine similarity (`pgvector`'s `<=>` operator): a SHIP AMI income-limit query correctly top-ranks the SHIP threshold chunk over HOME/TIF (0.72 vs 0.57/0.52); a Legistar agenda-item query for a specific real contract ("Future Partners" destination-marketing agreement) exactly matches the intended item at 0.69 with clean separation from unrelated items (~0.52); a Pinellas CF grant query correctly top-ranks the exact program (0.81) over template-near-duplicate sibling rows (0.69-0.70); a Duke Energy commitment query exactly matches its target action item (0.63); an off-corpus control query (Duke Energy rooftop solar rebates — not actually in the corpus) correctly ranks its best match well below every genuine on-topic hit (0.42-0.46 vs 0.63-0.81), real evidence a relevance threshold around 0.5 will usefully separate real hits from near-misses when Phase F builds retrieval; a SPHA "paying rent" query top-ranks the two real matching sections (0.83/0.82); a Pinellas HCD "eligible home repairs" query returns a real, correct, on-topic program at rank 1 (0.776) but the specific intended chunk (a different, similarly-named real program — "Home Repair Loan Program" vs. "Hurricane Home Repair Program") lands at rank #7 (0.68), still clearly retrievable and not wrong, but not top-ranked by a generic query alone.
Why: PLAN.md's Phase E done-criteria requires similarity search validated "against the real Tier-1 + a sample Tier-1.5 corpus, not synthetic test data" — running the actual full real corpus (not a token subset) and checking real, hand-picked, checkable queries (including a known-target case and a known-negative control) is what makes that claim defensible rather than "the code compiles." The rank-#7 Pinellas HCD finding is logged here rather than treated as a bug: two real, legitimately similar, distinct programs sitting close in embedding space is expected behavior at real semantic density, not a Phase E defect — it's forwarded as a named input to Phase F's relevance-threshold and possible-reranking design, not fixed here.
Date: 2026-08-21

## #74 — `rag-review` pass on the Phase E diff: 2 HIGH/MEDIUM fixed, 3 LOW fixed, 1 heuristic knowingly deferred
Decision: `rag-review` ran an adversarial pass on the full Phase E diff (`app/db/`, `app/embeddings/`, `tests/db/`, `tests/embeddings/`, `scripts/`) before commit, per this repo's global pre-commit gate. Findings and disposition:

**Applied — HIGH:** `_validate_embedding()` didn't reject NaN/Infinity values (`isinstance(v, (int, float))` is true for both, either would corrupt a downstream `<=>` cosine-distance computation if stored). Fixed with an added `math.isfinite()` check; folded into DECISIONS #71's write-up above rather than restated as its own entry.

**Applied — MEDIUM:** (1) `embed_and_insert_chunks()`'s batch loop originally caught every exception identically, meaning a systemic failure (dropped DB connection mid-run) would have replayed as N misleading independent "failed chunk" entries instead of aborting loudly — fixed to abort the batch on `psycopg.OperationalError`/`InterfaceError` specifically, keep continue-and-count behavior for genuine per-chunk failures. (2) `tests/db/test_pipeline.py`'s one real-commit batch test cleaned up its rows via a DELETE placed *after* its assertions — an earlier assertion failure would have skipped cleanup and left permanent junk rows in the shared dev Postgres, skewing the `chunk_already_embedded` pre-check on a later run. Fixed by moving cleanup into `try/finally`.

**Applied — LOW:** `EMBEDDING_DIMENSIONS = 1536` was duplicated in `app/db/schema.py` and `app/embeddings/client.py`; collapsed to one definition, owned by `client.py` (both existing test files already imported it from there, and it's fundamentally a model property). `_validate_embedding()`'s `data[0].embedding` access was unguarded, so a malformed response shape raised a raw `AttributeError` instead of the module's own `EmbeddingValidationError`; guarded. Two follow-up regression tests were added for the NaN/Infinity and malformed-response-shape guards specifically (`tests/embeddings/test_client.py`), since neither had dedicated coverage when first applied — full suite is 337 passing (315 pre-Phase-E + 19 original Phase E tests + 3 follow-up regression tests).

**Deferred, not fixed, per this repo's "deferrals get a note, never a silent skip" rule:** the `MAX_INPUT_CHARS` 4-chars/token heuristic (documented in DECISIONS #71 above) — reviewer independently confirmed this is safe to leave as a documented, loud-failure-mode heuristic rather than adding a `tiktoken` dependency for one pre-flight check. The Pinellas HCD rank-#7 ranking-adjacency finding (DECISIONS #73) — reviewer independently confirmed this is correctly scoped as Phase F's problem, not a Phase E code defect.

`rag-review` also independently confirmed (not just accepted the builder's own claims): no generation prompt exists anywhere in `app/` yet (Phase E is embeddings-only, verified by grep, not assumed); no stray `SELECT *` anywhere in the new code; nullable-over-sentinel followed correctly throughout the schema; the psycopg `%s::vector` cast gotcha (a raw Python list needs an explicit cast outside an INSERT context, since psycopg can't infer the pgvector adapter's target type without a destination column to introspect) is handled correctly in `scripts/validate_retrieval.py` and is now a documented gotcha for whoever builds Phase F retrieval code next; a process-kill between a successful embed call and the DB commit is safe by construction (an uncommitted transaction rolls back server-side on connection loss, so the next run's `chunk_id` pre-check correctly sees no row and just re-embeds that one chunk — re-billing it, not corrupting any state).
Why: Every applied finding was a real, code-confirmed failure mode (a NaN silently corrupting similarity math, a systemic failure misreported as many independent ones, a test leaving junk rows on failure) rather than speculative — this is the same "apply or consciously defer with a note" discipline this project runs pre-commit for every specialist's domain, not unique to RAG code.
Date: 2026-08-21

## #75 — `deploy-review` pass on the docker-compose diff: `httpx2` supply-chain concern resolved as a false alarm; `pg16` reproducibility caveat added; no blocking findings
Decision: `deploy-review` ran an adversarial pass on `docker-compose.yml`, `infra/local-db/init-pgvector.sql`, `.env.example`, and `.gitignore`'s `!.env.example` exception (DECISIONS #68-70) before commit.

**CONFLICT: `deploy-review` vs `rag-pipeline` on `requirements.txt`'s `httpx2==2.12.0` pin.**
- `deploy-review`: flagged HIGH — `requirements.txt`'s comment cites DECISIONS #24 as justification, but #24 actually describes `httpx2`/`httpcore2` as *non-existent* packages from a prior broken install that had to be removed; reintroducing `httpx2` looked like repeating that exact mistake, now mis-cited as intentional.
- `rag-pipeline`: pinned it deliberately, asserting it's a real, distinct package required by `openai==3.3.1`'s actual dependency tree in this environment (confirmed via `pip show`), not a repeat of #24's broken install.
- **Resolution (orchestrator, independently verified):** ran `pip show httpx2`, `pip show openai`, and `pip download httpx2==2.12.0 --no-deps` live against the real `.venv` and PyPI. Confirmed `httpx2` 2.12.0 is a real, independently-published, installable package (`Requires-by: openai`) — genuinely distinct from whatever was broken during DECISIONS #24's incident months earlier, not a recurrence of it. `rag-pipeline`'s pin stands as correct; `deploy-review`'s flag was a reasonable, appropriately cautious read of #24's history given how similar the situation looked on paper, not an error in judgment — the two package names being this close is a real hazard for a future reader, which is exactly why this entry exists: to make the "these are two different `httpx2` situations" distinction a matter of record instead of tribal memory.

**Applied — MEDIUM:** DECISIONS #68's `pgvector/pgvector:pg16` pin is major-version-only, not `pg16.15`/digest-pinned — a future `docker-compose pull` on a fresh clone could silently land a different patch release than the one verified live on 2026-08-21. Accepted as-is for a dev-only stack (deploy.md's literal "no `:latest`" bar is satisfied), but noted here as a known reproducibility gap rather than silently implying byte-for-byte pin-level reproducibility that doesn't actually exist. Revisit with a full point-release or digest pin if this ever needs to be bit-for-bit reproducible (e.g. CI).

**Confirmed clean, no finding:** no hardcoded credential anywhere in `docker-compose.yml` (only `${VAR}` references); `.env.example` holds placeholders only, no real value copy-pasted from `.env`; the init-script's `CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS` correctly runs only on first init of an empty data volume (standard Postgres `docker-entrypoint-initdb.d` behavior, independently confirmed, not just taken on the builder's word); the `127.0.0.1` port bind is valid, correctly localhost-scoped compose syntax, not a YAML mistake that silently widened exposure; `.gitignore`'s `!.env.example` negation is scoped narrowly enough that `.env.local`/`.env.production` remain ignored (verified via `git check-ignore`); the diff touches nothing under `infra/`'s existing AWS/Fargate files, so DECISIONS #1/#8 are undisturbed. A LOW cosmetic finding (the healthcheck's `${LOCAL_DB_USER:-postgres}` fallback is dead code, since the `environment:` block's `${VAR:?...}` already fails fast on an unset var) is noted but left as-is — genuinely harmless, not worth a round-trip.
Why: The `httpx2` conflict is the clearest example this project has yet had of two specialists reasoning correctly from their own vantage point to opposite conclusions — `deploy-review`'s caution was earned by #24's real history, `rag-pipeline`'s claim was independently true. Verifying it myself rather than picking a side by authority is what the orchestrator role is for per CLAUDE.md's precedence rule (specialist judgment doesn't get to unilaterally win a factual disagreement; the orchestrator checks and is the tiebreaker).
Date: 2026-08-21


## #76 — `archive-video.granicus.com` added to `ALLOWED_SOURCE_HOSTS`
Decision: Added the Granicus MP3/video CDN host (distinct subdomain from `stpete.granicus.com`, already allow-listed under #12) to `app/crawlers/base.py`'s closed host list, mirroring the one-line-per-host pattern already used for `www.stpeteha.org` (#49) and `pinellas.gov` (#52). Per-meeting audio resolves to `archive-video.granicus.com/stpete/<uuid>.mp3`, found on each meeting's `MediaPlayer.php` page — this is the host `BaseCrawler.fetch()` would need to reach to actually download that audio. No fetch to this host happens yet (no downloading/transcription is in scope this round); the entry exists so Day 5's worker doesn't need a schema/allow-list change mid-build.
Why: `ScopeViolationError` in `app/crawlers/base.py` enforces DECISIONS #11's closed source list in code, not just by convention (#17) — any host a crawler needs, including a CDN subdomain distinct from the page host that names it, must be listed explicitly before it's ever fetched. This addition is independently correct and narrow regardless of #77's finding below (it doesn't depend on `stpete.granicus.com` itself being fetchable) — the CDN host and the page host are two separate access questions.

`crawler-review` flagged (MEDIUM) that adding this host at all presumes Tier-1.5 continues in some form, in tension with #77's "nothing built speculatively while the block is unresolved" stance — if Amber picks full deferral, this line becomes closed-list residue for a dropped source with nothing tracking that it then needs removal. Accepted as-is rather than reverted: the line is inert (no code fetches this host yet; `ScopeViolationError` only matters at fetch time) and was already implied by #12's original Tier-1.5 description. Flagged here explicitly so it isn't forgotten — if #77 resolves to full deferral, remove this line as part of that cleanup.

## #77 — Granicus Tier-1.5 build BLOCKED: `stpete.granicus.com` robots.txt disallows our honest User-Agent from the entire host
Decision: Recon before writing the RSS parser / MediaPlayer.php extractor (this session, ahead of the planned PLAN.md Day 4 Granicus build) found that `https://stpete.granicus.com/robots.txt` (HTTP 200, real published policy, not a 404) reads:
```
User-agent: Googlebot
Disallow: /JSON.php
User-agent: Slurp
Disallow: /JSON.php
User-agent: msnbot
Disallow: /JSON.php
User-agent: search-one-scgov
Disallow: /JSON.php
User-agent: *
Disallow: /
```
Four named bots (Googlebot, Slurp, msnbot, search-one-scgov) are narrowly restricted from only `/JSON.php`. Every other user agent — including this project's honest, non-spoofed `DEFAULT_USER_AGENT` — falls under `User-agent: *` and is disallowed from `/`, i.e. the entire host, including the RSS feed and every `MediaPlayer.php` page. Verified two ways: independently by me via a direct `curl` with the project's real User-Agent string, and by the `crawler` specialist via `urllib.robotparser` (the exact library `RobotsChecker` uses in production) against `/`, `/ViewPublisher.php`, and `/MediaPlayer.php` — all return `can_fetch() == False`. No page beyond `robots.txt` itself was fetched once this was confirmed.

**No RSS parser, MediaPlayer.php extractor, or jobs-table population is built this round.** The `crawler` specialist correctly stopped before writing any of it — `.claude/rules/crawler.md`: "if a site blocks automated access, that's a signal to stop and flag it... not a reason to circumvent it," and identifying as one of the four exempted named bots would be exactly the User-Agent-spoofing this project has ruled out (`.claude/rules/crawler.md`, DECISIONS #14/#16). Only the independently-correct, non-blocked `archive-video.granicus.com` allow-list addition (#76) landed this round.

This corrects #11/#12's "confirmed working" characterization of the Tier-1.5 Granicus sub-pipeline: Day 1's smoke test (2026-08-19) verified the CDN host's URL pattern, User-Agent/Referer requirement, and Range-request support for the audio file itself — it did not check `stpete.granicus.com`'s robots.txt for the RSS/MediaPlayer pages that resolve *to* that audio. That gap went unnoticed for two days until this recon step caught it, which is exactly the "recon live before building" discipline this project has run for every other source paying off.

**Status: build blocked pending Amber's call on how to proceed** — this is the same shape of decision as #11's PSC/GovTrack deferrals (a source that looked viable hit a real access wall), but for a source PLAN.md had scheduled as load-bearing critical-path work for today (Day 4) and tomorrow (Day 5), not a from-the-start "deferred" source. Options on the table, none chosen yet: (a) investigate whether Granicus publishes a legitimate API/feed under different terms (unverified, same posture as GovTrack's deferred "believed to offer an API" note in #11), (b) seek explicit permission from the City of St. Petersburg / Granicus that would justify a documented User-Agent exception, (c) defer Tier-1.5 entirely for this build cycle, joining PSC/GovTrack/FL Statutes on the deferred list. No code was written speculatively against any of these while the decision is pending.
Why: The grounding contract and source attribution are only as trustworthy as the crawlers that feed them — building a parser against a host that robots.txt disallows would mean either quietly violating this project's own stated scraping ethics or shipping code that can never legitimately run. Documenting the block the moment it's found (rather than after guessing a workaround) is what makes this decision defensible in an interview instead of a silent schedule slip.
Date: 2026-08-21

## #78 — Granicus API-recon (option (a) from #77): no legitimate unblocked path found
Decision: Before committing to a human-input redesign, checked whether Granicus offers a real, unblocked API alternative to scraping `stpete.granicus.com`'s HTML pages, per #77's option (a). Findings, this session:
- **Legistar Web API is a dead end for St. Pete specifically.** A request against Legistar's public Web API using St. Pete's client identifier returned "no Legistar connection configured for client" — a distinct, specific error confirming St. Pete's council does not run on Legistar at all (unlike Pinellas County's BCC, DECISIONS #11's actual Legistar source). This isn't a permissions problem to work around; there is no Legistar backend here to call.
- **Granicus's real MediaManager API exists but is permission-gated.** It requires contacting Granicus support directly to request access — the same permission-based path #77 already named as option (b) (seeking explicit permission from the City/Granicus), not a self-serve unauthenticated alternative.
- **`search.granicus.com`** — the one plausible candidate for an open, unauthenticated search API — is dead: connection timeout, doesn't resolve. Not a live option.
- **Prior art check:** looked at how other open-source civic-tech Granicus scrapers handle this (`legiscrape`, `city-meeting-podcasts`, and similar real projects targeting other cities' Granicus instances). Every one scrapes `ViewPublisher.php`/RSS/`MediaPlayer.php` directly — the same pages blocked for this project. None of them engage with or work around a robots.txt block; none of their target cities' Granicus instances publish one. There is no established open-source workaround pattern to borrow, because no other project examined has had to solve this exact problem.
No code was written against any of these candidates — this was a pure recon pass to close out #77's option (a) before choosing between (b) and (c).
Why: #77 left three options on the table and explicitly deferred picking one until they'd been checked, not guessed at. Confirming (a) is a dead end (not just assumed) is what makes the redesign in #79 a reasoned choice rather than the path of least resistance.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #79 — Granicus discovery redesigned to human-supplied input; supersedes #12's discovery clause only
Decision: Amber's call, given #78's recon: automated *discovery* of St. Petersburg City Council meetings (which meeting has which MP3 URL) is out of scope for this build cycle — permanently, not "pending," absent a future change in `stpete.granicus.com`'s robots.txt or a granted Granicus MediaManager API access (#78's option (b), not pursued now). In its place, a human (Amber, via a real manual browser session — never an automated fetch) discovers a meeting on `stpete.granicus.com`, resolves its `MediaPlayer.php` page down to the real `archive-video.granicus.com` direct MP3 URL herself, and supplies both URLs to a new registration mechanism (#80) that inserts the meeting as a `status='pending'` row in a transcription jobs table (#81).

This does **not** overturn #12. #12's other decisions stand exactly as written: hosted OpenAI `whisper-1` transcription, the async-worker pattern (status column, `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` claiming, stale-row recovery), and the hard 12-month backfill bound are all still correct and unaffected. This entry supersedes **only** #12's discovery-mechanism clause — "direct MP3 links resolved per meeting via Granicus's RSS feed + MediaPlayer pages" — replacing automated resolution with human-supplied resolution. Automated *fetch-and-transcribe*, given an already-known direct MP3 URL, remains fully in scope and fully automated for the Day 5+ worker (not built this round) — `archive-video.granicus.com` was never blocked (#76) and stays reachable via `BaseCrawler.fetch()`.

`stpete.granicus.com` is removed from `app/crawlers/base.py`'s `ALLOWED_SOURCE_HOSTS`. Under this design, no code in the pipeline ever legitimately fetches that host again — the human resolution step happens in a real browser, outside this codebase entirely, and the `MediaPlayer.php` URL a human supplies is recorded as a string (attribution only), never fetched. Leaving the host in the allow-list after this redesign would be exactly the "closed-list residue for a dropped access path" risk #76 flagged and asked to be revisited once #77 resolved — it has now resolved, not to full deferral, but to a design where that host is structurally unreachable by design, which is the stronger version of the same cleanup.
Why: #77 correctly identified that scraping a robots.txt-disallowed host isn't a workaround-able problem, and #78 confirmed no unblocked API alternative exists this cycle. Amber chose to keep the source rather than drop it to the deferred list (PSC/GovTrack/FL Statutes) because the fetch-and-transcribe half of the pipeline — the actually expensive, actually automatable part — was never blocked; only the cheap, five-minutes-per-meeting discovery step was. Removing `stpete.granicus.com` from the allow-list makes the "we never automate-fetch this host" commitment structurally enforced (`ScopeViolationError` on any future accidental call), not just a design intention documented here and hoped to be remembered — the same "structurally awkward to violate, not just discouraged" bar `.claude/rules/crawler.md` sets for the rest of this allow-list.
Date: 2026-08-22


## #80 — Human-input mechanism: a CLI script over an admin UI, single-record flags plus JSON-file batch mode
Decision: `scripts/register_granicus_meeting.py` is a thin CLI wrapper around `app/granicus/register.py`'s `register_meeting()`. Two modes: single-meeting via `--meeting-date/--meeting-title/--mp3-url/--mediaplayer-url` flags, or batch via `--file meetings.json` (a JSON list of objects with the same four keys). Batch mode continues past one bad row rather than aborting the whole file — mirrors `embed_and_insert_chunks()`'s per-item continue-on-failure pattern (DECISIONS #71) — and prints one `OK`/`SKIP`/`FAIL` line per row, exiting nonzero if any row failed. Re-registering an already-registered `mp3_url` is a no-op (`ON CONFLICT (mp3_url) DO NOTHING`), reported as `SKIP`, not an error — safe to re-run the same file twice. Correcting an already-registered row's title/date (a real but rare need — a typo, or a re-check of the actual meeting date) is NOT handled by this mechanism yet; it needs a manual `UPDATE` for now. Not built speculatively here since nothing in this round's scope calls for it and it's a narrow, easily-added follow-up if it turns out to matter in practice.
Why: Amber will use this periodically, ongoing, not as a one-time migration step — a low-friction CLI she can run in ten seconds after resolving a meeting's URLs in her browser fits that better than a web admin UI, which would mean building and maintaining auth (CLAUDE.md already flags the upload endpoint's missing auth as an open stretch item; a second unauthenticated write surface isn't worth adding) and a form UI for a workflow that's really "run one command with four values I just copied from my browser." A structured input *file* alone (no script) was considered and rejected: it would still need something to read, validate, and insert it — that's the script, just without a convenient single-meeting CLI path for the common case of registering one or two meetings at a time. Batch mode exists for the less common case (catching up a backlog) without requiring two separate mechanisms.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #81 — `granicus_transcription_jobs` table: schema, natural key, and worker-shape columns
Decision: `app/granicus/schema.py` defines one `granicus_transcription_jobs` table with an explicit `GRANICUS_JOB_COLUMNS` constant (`.claude/rules/data.md` — no `SELECT *`), matching `app/db/schema.py`'s chunks-table conventions (DECISIONS #71): one owning module, one DDL string, one idempotent `apply_granicus_schema()` entry point.

- **`mp3_url TEXT PRIMARY KEY`** — the direct `archive-video.granicus.com` URL is used as the natural key rather than a synthetic hashed ID (unlike `chunks.chunk_id`, DECISIONS #58). `chunks` needed a hash because a chunk's identity is a *compound* key (source URL + section/row position); a registered meeting's identity is already exactly one stable string — the resource the worker will fetch — so hashing it would add indirection with no benefit.
- **`meeting_title TEXT NOT NULL`**, **`source_url TEXT NOT NULL`** (the `MediaPlayer.php` URL, attribution only, never fetched), **`published_date DATE NOT NULL`** (the meeting date), **`retrieval_timestamp TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL`** (when this system recorded the registration) — the same `source_url`/`published_date`/`retrieval_timestamp` field names `app/crawlers/base.py`'s `Attribution` dataclass already uses everywhere else in this codebase, reused rather than reinvented, per this round's explicit instruction. `published_date` is `NOT NULL` here (unlike `chunks.published_date`, which is nullable because a crawler's date-parse can fail) because a human always supplies the meeting date directly at registration — there's no "maybe unparseable" state for this column the way there is for a scraped page.
- **`status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'pending'`** with a `CHECK` constraint against the closed enum `('pending', 'claimed', 'completed', 'failed')` — DECISIONS #12's async-worker pattern's state machine, enforced at the DB layer so a future bug can't silently write an unrecognized status string.
- **`claimed_at TIMESTAMPTZ`** (nullable — `NULL` until a worker claims the row) supports the Day 5+ worker's stale-row recovery (`WHERE status = 'claimed' AND claimed_at < <cutoff>`), the same shape as the R-EX-derived pattern named in `.claude/rules/crawler.md`.
- **`transcript_text TEXT`** and **`failure_reason TEXT`**, both nullable per `.claude/rules/data.md`'s nullable-over-sentinel rule — neither is knowable at registration time, and a magic placeholder (empty string, `"N/A"`) would be exactly the landmine that rule warns about for a later query that forgot it was magic.
- **Index:** `granicus_jobs_status_idx` on `status` — added now, alongside the table, for the Day 5+ worker's claim query (`WHERE status = 'pending' ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`) and stale-row recovery query, the same "add the index the next phase's known query pattern will need, don't wait" reasoning DECISIONS #71 used for `chunks`' HNSW index.

This round populates the table (inserts `status='pending'` rows via `register_meeting()`) but does not build the claim/process/complete side of the state machine — that's explicitly Day 5+'s worker, out of scope here.
Why: Reusing `Attribution`'s exact field vocabulary keeps this table legible against every other attributed row in this codebase without a reader needing to learn a second naming scheme for the same three concepts. The natural-key choice and the `CHECK`-constrained status enum are both about making an invalid state harder to represent, not just more code to prevent it at the application layer.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #82 — 12-month backfill bound enforced at registration time, not deferred to the worker
Decision: `app/granicus/register.py`'s `register_meeting()` computes `today`'s 12-calendar-months-back cutoff (via a small stdlib-only helper, `_earliest_allowed_meeting_date()` — no new `dateutil` dependency for one call site) and raises `BackfillBoundError` **before any database write** if `meeting_date` falls before it. A meeting dated in the future relative to `today` is also rejected (`RegistrationValidationError`) as an obviously-wrong input, not a backfill-bound question. `today` is an injectable parameter (defaults to `date.today()`) specifically so tests can assert the boundary deterministically without depending on the real calendar date.
Why: DECISIONS #12's 12-month bound is still binding (#79) but its enforcement point moves: the original design assumed the bound would be checked wherever a meeting entered the pipeline, which was going to be the RSS-parsing crawl step. That step no longer exists — registration via `register_meeting()` is now the only entry point a meeting can enter the jobs table through, so it is the correct (and only) place left to enforce the bound in code rather than as a soft convention a human might forget to self-apply when registering an old meeting by hand. Rejecting at registration time (not at worker-claim time) means an out-of-window meeting never becomes a `'pending'` row at all — it fails loud to the person doing the registering, at the moment they'd otherwise wrongly assume it worked, rather than silently sitting in the jobs table until a worker eventually rejects it later.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #83 — `crawler-review` pass on the #79-82 diff: 1 MEDIUM bug fixed, 1 MEDIUM test-coverage gap closed, 1 LOW test gap closed, 1 LOW accepted as-is
Decision: `crawler-review` ran an adversarial pass on the staged diff (`DECISIONS.md` #78-82, `app/crawlers/base.py`'s host-list change, `app/granicus/`, `scripts/register_granicus_meeting.py`, `tests/granicus/`) before commit, per this repo's global pre-commit gate. Findings and disposition:

**Applied — MEDIUM (real bug):** `scripts/register_granicus_meeting.py`'s batch mode looked up `row["meeting_date"]`/`row["meeting_title"]`/`row["mp3_url"]`/`row["mediaplayer_url"]` as call-site argument expressions, outside `_register_one()`'s try/except — a JSON batch file with a row missing a key raised an uncaught `KeyError` that aborted the entire `for` loop, silently skipping every remaining row with no `FAIL` line and no clean exit code. This directly contradicted the module's own documented behavior ("Batch mode continues past one bad row") and DECISIONS #80's stated design. Fixed by moving all four key lookups inside `_register_one()`'s own try/except (now takes a `dict` directly rather than four separate keyword args), so a malformed row reports `FAIL  malformed row (missing key ...)` and the loop continues. Verified live via a 3-row batch file (good/malformed/good): the malformed row now fails cleanly, both good rows still register, exit code is 1 (not a traceback).

**Applied — MEDIUM (test-coverage gap):** `_earliest_allowed_meeting_date()`'s three original "clamp" test cases (`tests/granicus/test_register.py`) never actually exercised the clamp branch — with `BACKFILL_MONTHS` fixed at 12, every month except February has the same day-count in every year, so none of Aug 31/Mar 31/Apr 30 ever hits `except ValueError: day -= 1`. The only day/month combination a fixed-12-month bound can ever actually produce a nonexistent date for is Feb 29 (leap year) → Feb 29 of a non-leap year, which doesn't exist. Added `test_earliest_allowed_meeting_date_clamps_leap_day`, asserting `Feb 29, 2024 → Feb 28, 2023` and `Feb 29, 2028 → Feb 28, 2027` — reviewer independently confirmed the existing code already handled this correctly; this was a missing proof, not a code fix.

**Applied — LOW:** the wrong-host validation was only tested in one swap direction (MediaPlayer.php URL pasted into `mp3_url`). Added `test_register_meeting_rejects_mp3_url_as_mediaplayer_url` for the reverse mistake, since `_validate_host()` is called symmetrically on both fields.

**Accepted as-is, no fix, per this repo's "deferrals get a note" rule:** re-registering an existing `mp3_url` with a different title/date is a silent `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` no-op (reported as `SKIP`, not surfaced as a diff) — reviewer confirmed this is the sharpest edge of DECISIONS #80's already-documented, deliberate tradeoff (no update/correction path this round), not a new finding against this diff.

`crawler-review` also independently confirmed (not just accepted the builder's own claims): no HTTP request primitive (`requests.`, `httpx.`, `urlopen`, `.fetch(`) exists anywhere in `app/granicus/` or `scripts/register_granicus_meeting.py` — grepped directly, the only hit is a comment referencing `BaseCrawler.fetch()`, not a call; the 12-month boundary is correctly inclusive (exactly 12 months back is allowed, per DECISIONS #82's stated intent) and was already proven by a dedicated boundary test; `published_date NOT NULL` on this table (vs. nullable on `chunks`) is correctly reasoned given a human always supplies it directly at registration; no `SELECT *` anywhere in the new code; `claimed_at`/`transcript_text`/`failure_reason` are correctly nullable with no sentinel defaults; removing `stpete.granicus.com` from `ALLOWED_SOURCE_HOSTS` has no unintended effect on any other still-active code path (grepped for other references).

Full suite after fixes: 353 passing (351 pre-review + 2 new tests: the leap-day clamp case and the reverse host-swap case).
Why: The batch-mode `KeyError` was a real, live bug that would have silently truncated any batch file with one bad row — exactly the "fail loud, never silently empty" failure mode `.claude/rules/crawler.md` exists to catch, just manifesting in registration code instead of a page parser. The leap-day gap mattered because the round's own instructions specifically asked for "a test proving" the 12-month bound — three tests that don't exercise the one case that can actually fail isn't that proof, even though the underlying code was already correct.
Date: 2026-08-22


## #84 — Granicus worker: claim + stale-recovery mechanics, 60-minute stale-claim threshold
Decision: `app/granicus/worker.py`'s `claim_next_job()` claims the oldest `status='pending'` row by `published_date ASC` via `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED LIMIT 1`, then `UPDATE`s it to `status='claimed'` in the same transaction (row lock held until commit, so a concurrent claimer's `SKIP LOCKED` genuinely skips it rather than racing it) — `claimed_at` is set app-side via `datetime.now(timezone.utc)`, matching `Attribution.now()`'s existing convention rather than introducing a second DB-side `NOW()` convention. `recover_stale_claims()` resets `status='claimed'` rows back to `status='pending'` (clearing `claimed_at`) where `claimed_at` is older than `STALE_CLAIM_THRESHOLD = timedelta(hours=1)`.

The 60-minute threshold is derived, not a round-number guess: two independent worst-case estimates both land at 20-25 minutes. (A) Under the module's actual design (see #85's early-abort), only a ≤25 MiB/≤~1-hour-of-audio file ever reaches a full pipeline run — download in 8 MiB windows (≤4 requests × ~60s worst case ≈ 4 min) + transcription (up to 3 attempts × 5 min generous bound + trivial backoff ≈ 15 min) ≈ 19 min. (B) Pessimistic cross-check assuming the early-abort design somehow didn't fire: downloading a full 6-hour meeting at 128kbps (~337.5MB, ~43 chunks × 30s worst-case) ≈ 21.5 min to download the entire file even in the scenario the design is meant to prevent (no Whisper multiplier applies here since decision #86 means an oversized file never reaches Whisper). 60 minutes gives ~2.5-3x margin over either estimate.
Why: A dead worker (crashed process, killed container) must not leave a row stuck in `claimed` indefinitely, but the threshold must not be so tight it reclaims a job still honestly in flight. Working the arithmetic from the pipeline's own real bounded steps (not an arbitrary round number) is what makes this defensible against "how do you know an hour is long enough" in an interview.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #85 — Range-chunked audio download: 8 MiB windows, early-abort on first oversized chunk
Decision: `_download_ranged()` fetches `mp3_url` via explicit `Range: bytes=X-Y` requests (through `BaseCrawler.fetch()`, so scope/robots/rate-limiting still apply) in fixed 8 MiB windows, writing each window straight to a temp file on disk rather than accumulating the whole file in memory. It reads the real total file size off the *first* response's `Content-Range` header and raises immediately (before fetching any further chunks) if that total exceeds Whisper's 25 MiB single-request limit (#86) — it does not download a multi-hour, multi-hundred-MB file just to reject it afterward.
Why: 8 MiB keeps round-trip count reasonable (~4 requests for a ≤25 MiB file, each paying the 2.0s/host rate-limit floor) while keeping peak memory trivial and staying comfortably under the 25 MiB Whisper ceiling so a single chunk can never itself exceed it. Early-abort-on-first-chunk matters because a real multi-hour council meeting MP3 is virtually certain to exceed 25 MiB (even a low 64 kbps mono encoding is ~28.8 MB/hour) — downloading a multi-GB file already known to be doomed to fail the size check would waste real bandwidth/time and blow past #84's stale-claim threshold's assumptions for no benefit.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #86 — Whisper >25 MiB files fail loud this round; no split-and-concatenate transcription
Decision: A meeting audio file that exceeds whisper-1's real 25 MB single-request limit (verified live via OpenAI's current docs, `developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/speech-to-text`) raises `AudioTooLargeError` and the job is stored as `status='failed'` with a specific byte-count `failure_reason` — never a silent truncation, and no attempt to split the file into multiple sub-25MB pieces and concatenate the transcripts this round. Checked whether the installed `openai==3.3.1` SDK offers a built-in multi-request chunking helper before assuming there wasn't one: `transcriptions.create()`'s `chunking_strategy` parameter is server-side VAD chunking *within* one request/25MB and is explicitly documented as ignored for `whisper-1` — no SDK-provided out. `WHISPER_MAX_FILE_BYTES = 25 * 1024 * 1024` (the MiB reading of "25 MB" — not independently confirmed byte-for-byte against a live API call, so treated as the conservative interpretation).

whisper-1 pricing, verified live via OpenAI's current pricing docs this session: **$0.006/minute** ($0.36/hour) — the real cost line to budget-alert on before any actual batch run (`.claude/rules/crawler.md`).
Why: A real multi-hour council meeting will very likely exceed 25 MB and hit this path routinely — that's a known, named limitation, not a hidden one. True audio-duration-aware splitting needs an audio-processing dependency (pydub, wrapping ffmpeg) — a new system binary in the Docker image with real Docker/Fargate deploy implications that are deploy-infra's territory and were not in scope or decided this round. A byte-offset split of an MP3 stream is also not guaranteed frame-safe (MP3 frames aren't fixed-length; a raw cut can corrupt both halves) — a naive split could produce a technically-succeeding but subtly-wrong transcript, which is arguably worse than an honest failure. **Follow-up needed, not yet authorized:** duration-aware chunked transcription (pydub/ffmpeg) requires its own future DECISIONS entry and Amber's sign-off on the new dependency — flagged here so it isn't forgotten, and flagged to Amber directly this round since most real St. Pete council meetings will hit this limit in practice, not just in a rare edge case.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #87 — `claimed_at` left set (not cleared) on both terminal states
Decision: `_mark_completed`/`_mark_failed` update only `status` + `transcript_text` or `status` + `failure_reason` — `claimed_at` is left as whatever the claiming worker set it to, not nulled out on completion or failure.
Why: `granicus_transcription_jobs` (DECISIONS #81) has no separate `completed_at`/`failed_at` column, so `claimed_at` is the only "when did a worker last touch this row" timestamp the schema has — useful audit/health-view information (CLAUDE.md's "at least basic visibility... not silent failure" note) for a terminal row, not just an in-flight one. `recover_stale_claims()` only ever matches `status='claimed'`, so a terminal row's `claimed_at` value never interacts with stale recovery regardless of whether it's cleared — clearing it would be pure cosmetics with no correctness upside.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #88 — Claim ordering: oldest meeting (`published_date ASC`) claimed first
Decision: `claim_next_job()`'s claim query orders candidate `pending` rows by `published_date ASC`, so the oldest registered meeting is always claimed next.
Why: This project's retrieval use case (CBA negotiation history) benefits from the deepest backlog becoming searchable soonest, not newest-registered-first. Registration is human-paced (a few meetings at a time via `scripts/register_granicus_meeting.py`, DECISIONS #80) — there's no fast-growing-queue risk of a "newest first" ordering being needed to keep the corpus current, so there's no real tradeoff being traded away here.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #89 — `archive-video.granicus.com/robots.txt` returns 403 under every User-Agent tried; worker cannot run for real against this host without a decision
Decision: Independently verified this session (orchestrator, three live `curl` attempts: this project's honest `DEFAULT_USER_AGENT`, no User-Agent at all, and the browser-like UA + `Referer` the worker uses for the audio-fetch path) — `https://archive-video.granicus.com/robots.txt` returns HTTP 403 every time, regardless of User-Agent. The first attempt (honest UA) returned the same CloudFront "403 Request blocked" bot-filter page crawler.md already documents for this CDN's audio endpoints (DECISIONS #76's underlying Day 1 finding); the browser-UA attempt returned a *different* 403 — an S3-style `AccessDenied` XML body, consistent with this CDN's bucket simply not having a `robots.txt` object and S3/CloudFront masking a missing key as `AccessDenied` rather than a clean 404 (a common S3 static-hosting pattern), rather than a real published disallow policy.

This matters because `RobotsChecker` (`app/crawlers/base.py`, DECISIONS #16) treats an exact HTTP 404 as "no restrictions declared" and raises `RobotsError` for any other non-2xx status — by design, so a crawler can't quietly assume permission on an ambiguous failure. A 403 is not a 404. That means `BaseCrawler.fetch()` will raise `RobotsError` on the *very first real call* to `archive-video.granicus.com` — before the worker ever reaches a single audio byte — regardless of the worker's own CDN-specific User-Agent/Referer override on the audio-fetch itself (that override only applies to the audio request's headers, not to `RobotsChecker`'s own internal robots.txt fetch, by original design). All 24+ of the worker's tests pass because they mock/stub the robots check entirely — this gap was invisible to the test suite and only surfaced via a live check.

**No code changed to work around this.** Unlike DECISIONS #77 (`stpete.granicus.com`'s explicit, published `Disallow: /` policy — a real stated restriction), this looks like CDN infrastructure noise (a missing `robots.txt` object returning `AccessDenied` instead of `404`), not a real access-control decision by Granicus/AWS. But treating a 403 as "safe to assume unrestricted" would mean quietly loosening `RobotsChecker`'s deliberately strict, already-proven-correct policy for one host's sake — exactly the kind of unilateral judgment call CLAUDE.md's precedence rules and the "ask Amber before proceeding" bar for interview-defensibility-affecting decisions exist to catch. **Options on the table, none chosen yet:** (a) special-case this exact host/path in `RobotsChecker` to treat a 403-on-robots.txt as equivalent to "no restrictions," documented as a deliberate, narrow, host-specific exception grounded in this session's evidence; (b) treat this as a hard blocker on the same footing as #77 and defer real Granicus transcription runs until Granicus support confirms there's genuinely no robots policy on this subdomain (option (b) from #77/#78's playbook, not yet pursued for this specific host); (c) something else Amber decides. This blocks any real invocation of the worker built this round — the mocked test suite and all design work stand regardless of which option is chosen.
Why: `.claude/rules/crawler.md`: "if a site blocks automated access, that's a signal to stop and flag it... not a reason to circumvent it." A 403 isn't confirmed to BE a block (unlike #77's explicit policy), but it isn't confirmed to be safe either — the honest move is surfacing the ambiguity for a human decision rather than quietly coding around it either direction (assuming it's fine, or refusing to build anything until it's resolved).
Date: 2026-08-22

## #90 — `crawler-review` pass on the Granicus worker (`app/granicus/worker.py`): 1 HIGH bug fixed, 2 MEDIUM bugs fixed
Decision: `crawler-review` ran an adversarial pass on the staged diff (`app/granicus/worker.py`, `tests/granicus/test_worker.py`, `tests/granicus/conftest.py` — DECISIONS #84-88's claim/stale-recovery/Range-download/Whisper-transcribe/store pipeline) before commit, per this repo's global pre-commit gate. All three findings were applied, independently re-verified by the orchestrator against the actual staged code (not taken on the reviewer's word alone):

**Applied — HIGH (silent truncation):** `_download_ranged`'s loop could exit on a premature empty response body (the CDN reporting more remaining data than it actually sent) without the loop itself treating that as an error — a truncated file could silently reach `_transcribe_audio`. Fixed two ways: (1) the empty-body case now raises `CrawlerStructureError` inline, with the exact byte counts (`downloaded`/`total`) in the message, instead of silently breaking out of the loop; (2) a second, independent post-loop guard — `if downloaded != total: raise CrawlerStructureError(...)` — was added so a truncated download can never reach Whisper even if a future change to the loop's own exit logic reintroduces a silent-truncation path. New test: `test_download_ranged_raises_structure_error_on_premature_empty_body`.

**Applied — MEDIUM (no retry on chunk fetches):** each Range chunk fetch had no retry for a transient network failure, unlike `_transcribe_audio`'s existing retry-with-backoff shape — a single connection blip partway through a multi-chunk download would permanently fail a hand-registered job with no cheap re-registration path (DECISIONS #80). Added `_is_retryable_download_error()` (retryable: `requests.exceptions.ConnectionError`/`Timeout`, plus `HTTPError` with status >= 500 treated as transient/server-side; everything else, e.g. a 4xx client error, not retryable) and `_fetch_chunk_with_retry()`, wrapping each chunk fetch with the same bounded-backoff shape already proven in `_transcribe_audio`. Two new tests: `test_download_ranged_retries_transient_connection_error_then_succeeds`, `test_download_ranged_does_not_retry_non_transient_http_error`.

**Applied — MEDIUM (systemic vs. per-job failures):** `process_one_job`'s original per-job `except Exception` clause would have caught an `openai.AuthenticationError`/`PermissionDeniedError` (a broken API key — systemic, not specific to that meeting's audio) and permanently marked that one job `'failed'`, then `process_pending_jobs`' loop would go on to claim and identically fail every other pending job against the same broken credential one at a time. Added `_revert_to_pending()` and special-cased both exception types BEFORE the generic per-job except clause in `process_one_job`: reverts the job to `'pending'` (not `'failed'` — the credential is broken, not the job; per DECISIONS #80's no-cheap-re-registration-path design, a future worker run should pick this job back up for free once the key is fixed, not require a human to manually reset the row) and re-raises rather than swallowing. `process_pending_jobs` catches the re-raise, logs how many jobs completed before the abort, and re-raises itself — the batch stops instead of continuing to treat a systemic failure as N independent per-job failures. New test: `test_process_pending_jobs_aborts_batch_on_systemic_auth_error` (2-job batch: job 1 hits the auth error, job 2's Whisper call never happens, both jobs end at `'pending'`, not `'failed'`).

Test results after fixes, independently re-run and confirmed by the orchestrator: `tests/granicus/` 44 passed (up from 40), full suite 381 passed (up from 377), zero live network/API calls.
Why: The HIGH finding is exactly the "fail loud, never silently empty" contract `.claude/rules/crawler.md` exists to enforce, manifesting in a download loop instead of a page parser — a truncated audio file handed to Whisper wouldn't error, it would just transcribe garbage or a partial meeting with no signal that anything was wrong. The two MEDIUM findings both trace back to DECISIONS #80's "no cheap re-registration path" constraint: a hand-registered job is expensive to recreate, so a transient failure (network blip, temporarily-broken credential) must not be treated the same as a genuine per-job problem (bad audio, oversized file) that legitimately belongs in `'failed'`.
Date: 2026-08-22


## #91 — Narrow, host-specific exception: `archive-video.granicus.com`'s 403-on-robots.txt treated as "no restrictions declared"
Decision: Amber's call on #89's blocker, choosing option (a) from #89's own listed options. `app/crawlers/base.py` adds `ROBOTS_403_TREATED_AS_PERMISSIVE_HOSTS = frozenset({"archive-video.granicus.com"})` and `RobotsChecker._get_parser()`'s status-handling changes from `if resp.status_code == 404: parser.parse([])` to `if resp.status_code == 404 or (resp.status_code == 403 and parsed.netloc in ROBOTS_403_TREATED_AS_PERMISSIVE_HOSTS): parser.parse([])`. The existing `elif resp.status_code >= 400: raise RobotsError(...)` branch is otherwise untouched — every other host's 403 (and this host's own 5xx or any other 4xx) still raises `RobotsError` exactly as it did before this change. DECISIONS #16's 404-only "no restrictions" rule stands unmodified for every host not in this explicit set.

Grounded directly in #89's evidence: three live curl attempts (this project's honest `DEFAULT_USER_AGENT`, no User-Agent, and the CDN-specific browser-like UA+Referer the worker already uses for the audio-fetch path) all returned a 403 on `https://archive-video.granicus.com/robots.txt` — never a clean 404, never a 200 with a real published policy. Two distinct 403 response shapes were observed (a CloudFront bot-filter page, and separately an S3-style `AccessDenied` XML body), both consistent with this CDN's bucket simply lacking a `robots.txt` object at all (S3/CloudFront's well-documented pattern of masking a missing key as `AccessDenied` rather than a clean 404) rather than a deliberate, published disallow policy — unlike `stpete.granicus.com` (#77), which returned a real 200 with an actual `Disallow: /` policy and remains a hard block, untouched by this change.

Scope discipline: the code comment on `ROBOTS_403_TREATED_AS_PERMISSIVE_HOSTS` explicitly instructs that adding another host requires a new DECISIONS.md entry documenting equivalent evidence (three-UA 403 pattern, response-shape analysis) for that host — this is a per-host allowlist for one specific status code on one specific fetch (the robots.txt fetch itself), not a general change to how any other status code or any other host is handled. `crawler-review` ran an adversarial pass on this exact staged change (`app/crawlers/base.py`, `app/granicus/worker.py`'s updated comment, `tests/crawlers/test_base.py`'s two new tests) before commit, per this repo's "nothing's too small to review" gate for security-adjacent changes. Findings: mechanism verified sound (exact-set `netloc` match, no subdomain/scheme/case bypass; fails *closed* on any mismatch, not open), both new tests proven to check what they claim, cache-poisoning question is a pre-existing property of `RobotsChecker`'s per-instance parser cache and not worsened by this change. One HIGH finding — this DECISIONS entry didn't exist yet at review time even though the code already cited it — is resolved by this entry's own existence; DECISIONS.md is staged alongside the code as of this commit. Full suite: 383 passing (381 + the 2 new robots-exception tests).
Why: The robots.txt object being genuinely absent (not a real access-control decision by Granicus/AWS) means the strict 404-only rule was blocking a legitimate fetch on what is almost certainly infrastructure noise, not a real "stop" signal — but `.claude/rules/crawler.md`'s "if a site blocks automated access, that's a signal to stop and flag it... not a reason to circumvent it" meant this couldn't be coded around unilaterally (#89's own conclusion). A narrow, evidence-grounded, explicitly-documented per-host exception — rather than loosening the 404-only rule generally, or treating any ambiguous non-404 as safe — keeps `RobotsChecker`'s strict-by-default posture intact for every other host and every other status code, while unblocking the one host where the evidence specifically supports it.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #92 — Retrieval design (`app/rag/retrieval.py`): threshold + dedup + candidate pool + token budget, and how it resolves DECISIONS #73's Pinellas HCD ambiguity
Decision: `retrieve()` is threshold-filtered and token-budgeted, deliberately not a fixed "return top N chunks." Four tunables, each independently justified against live evidence rather than picked as round numbers:

- **`RELEVANCE_THRESHOLD = 0.5`** — grounded in DECISIONS #73's live 342-chunk evidence (7 hand-picked on-topic queries scored 0.63-0.83 cosine similarity against their real target chunks; a known off-corpus negative control's best, still-wrong match scored 0.42-0.46 — 0.5 sits in the real gap between those two populations). Re-validated live against this finished retrieval+generation pipeline in `scripts/validate_generation.py` before being accepted for real use, not reused unchecked from the earlier manual sanity check.
- **`DEDUP_SIMILARITY_THRESHOLD = 0.97`** — chunk-to-chunk cosine similarity, computed as a plain dot product (`_dot()`) since OpenAI's `text-embedding-3-small` vectors are unit-normalized, no separate normalization step needed. Deliberately very conservative: this only collapses near-identical text (templated boilerplate rows differing in a few fields, e.g. DECISIONS #73's Pinellas CF "template-near-duplicate sibling rows"), not merely topically-similar-but-distinct chunks. Confirmed live that DECISIONS #73's Pinellas HCD rank-1 (0.776 similarity to the query) / rank-7 (0.68) pair — two genuinely different programs — sits well below 0.97 chunk-to-chunk and does NOT collapse.
- **`CANDIDATE_POOL_SIZE = 40`** — a pragmatic pre-filter bound on how many nearest-neighbor rows pgvector even returns for examination, explicitly NOT the relevance decision itself (that's threshold + dedup + budget, never "the first K by rank" — rag.md's "never blind top-k"). Sized to comfortably include the Pinellas HCD rank-#7 finding with 5-6x margin, without pulling a large fraction of the whole 342-chunk corpus on every query.
- **`MAX_CONTEXT_TOKENS = 6000`** — real justification chain, all live-verified this session, not a squeeze against the model's real limit: gpt-4o-mini's real 128K context window (verified live against `developers.openai.com/api/docs/models/gpt-4o-mini`) makes 6,000 tokens a deliberate small fraction (~4.7%) of the ceiling; gpt-4o-mini's real input rate is $0.15/1M tokens (verified live against `developers.openai.com/api/docs/pricing`), so 6,000 context tokens costs ~$0.0009/query before system prompt/query/output are even added; this corpus's real chunk-size distribution, live-queried from the 342-row `chunks` table, runs ~831 chars/~208 est. tokens average, 462 chars/~115 est. tokens median, 2,288 chars/~572 est. tokens at the 95th percentile — 6,000 tokens comfortably fits double-digit average/median chunks, or the Pinellas HCD pair together with room to spare. Deliberately NOT sized near the 128K ceiling: a bigger budget buys nothing once threshold+dedup have already narrowed candidates to a genuinely-relevant handful — it would only cost more per call and give the model more surface area to mis-cite across.
- **Token-budget mechanics:** the single highest-ranked surviving chunk is always included even if it alone exceeds the budget (never return zero chunks when at least one genuinely relevant one exists); every chunk after the first is strictly budget-gated, walking the deduplicated, similarity-ranked list and stopping once the next chunk would exceed `MAX_CONTEXT_TOKENS`. Token counts are estimated at `CHARS_PER_TOKEN_ESTIMATE = 4` chars/token — the same heuristic and reasoning DECISIONS #71/#74 already established for `app/embeddings/client.py`'s `MAX_INPUT_CHARS`, kept consistent rather than introducing a second sizing convention.

**Pinellas HCD resolution:** DECISIONS #73 named a forward-pointer — a correct on-topic program at rank #1 (0.776) but the specific intended, similarly-named program at rank #7 (0.68), both above threshold, both real and distinct, not a near-duplicate pair. This is resolved by NOT building a reranker/disambiguator. A large-enough `CANDIDATE_POOL_SIZE` means rank #7 is examined at all (the earlier `scripts/validate_retrieval.py`'s `LIMIT 3` never would have surfaced it); threshold-then-budget (not a small top-k) means both survive into context together rather than one being silently cut off; and `app/rag/generation.py`'s system prompt (see #94) explicitly instructs the model to name each distinct item separately when this happens — retrieval doesn't guess which one the user meant, generation is told to surface the ambiguity honestly instead.
Why: Every one of these four numbers is independently defensible against "why this value" in an interview — each traces to a specific live measurement (DECISIONS #73's real query scores, this corpus's real chunk-size distribution, gpt-4o-mini's real documented context window and pricing) rather than a plausible-sounding guess. Not building a reranker for the Pinellas HCD case is a deliberate scope call: the existing threshold+budget mechanism already produces the correct behavior (both real programs reach the model) without adding a new component whose own correctness would then need its own evidence.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #93 — `app/db/connection.py` docstring fix: `vector` column read-back type
Decision: `get_connection()`'s docstring claimed a `vector` column comes back from psycopg as a plain `list[float]`. Confirmed live against the real 342-row `chunks` table (by `app/rag/retrieval.py`, the first code in this repo to actually `SELECT` a `vector` column back out rather than only ever writing it — DECISIONS #71/#72 only ever inserted) that a read-back `vector` column is actually a `pgvector.vector.Vector` object, requiring `.to_list()` to get a plain `list[float]`. Docstring corrected to describe write and read paths separately: write accepts a plain `list[float]`; read returns a `pgvector.vector.Vector`, converted via `.to_list()`. No behavior change — `app/rag/retrieval.py`'s own `_to_float_list()` already handled this correctly; only the stale docstring was wrong.
Why: `.claude/rules/data.md`'s schema-comment discipline is only as good as the comments actually being checked against real observed behavior when new code exercises a path the original comment never had to account for. A wrong docstring on a widely-imported connection helper is exactly the kind of drift that misleads the next reader who trusts it instead of testing it themselves — worth a one-line fix the moment it's caught, not left to compound.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #94 — Generation design (`app/rag/generation.py`): prompt-injection defense and the grounding contract
Decision: `generate_answer()` enforces `.claude/rules/rag.md`'s prompting and grounding rules structurally, not just by instruction:

- **Prompt-injection defense:** `GENERATION_SYSTEM_PROMPT` is a fixed Python string constant with zero interpolation of user- or chunk-controlled content — checkable by inspection, it is only ever read, never `.format()`-ed or f-string-built from request data. Role separation is the real defense a static string alone can't provide: the `system` message is always exactly `GENERATION_SYSTEM_PROMPT`; a separate `user` message, built by `_build_user_message()`, concatenates the query and retrieved chunk text into clearly delimited `QUESTION:`/`REFERENCE MATERIAL:` sections. The system prompt additionally instructs the model in plain language that content inside those two sections is data to read and answer from, never a command to follow, even if it looks like one (e.g. a crawled page containing "ignore previous instructions"-style text) — explicitly future-proofed reasoning, since this corpus is public government/nonprofit content today but the design doesn't assume "our corpus is safe" stays true forever.
- **Not-in-corpus, no-API-call short-circuit:** when `retrieve()` returns zero chunks above threshold, `generate_answer()` returns the fixed `NOT_IN_CORPUS_ANSWER` directly, without ever calling gpt-4o-mini. This is reasoned as strictly cheaper AND strictly lower-risk, not a tradeoff between the two: an empty context has nothing for the model to reason about, so the outcome is deterministic either way, and calling the model anyway only adds the well-documented risk of it "being helpful" from its own training knowledge despite instructions — a real prompt-injection-adjacent failure mode with zero benefit to risking here.
- **Citation validation:** the model's returned `citations` list is treated as untrusted output (rag.md: "validate every field the model returns... before it touches the database or gets rendered"). `_validate_and_build_result()` checks every returned `chunk_id` against the actual retrieved set; an invalid citation (wrong type, or pointing at a chunk_id never retrieved) is dropped and logged at WARNING, never passed through as real. If the model claims a grounded answer (`not_in_corpus=False`) but every citation it returned turns out invalid, that's raised as a specific, named `UngroundedAnswerError` — not silently treated as "an answer with an empty citation list," since rag.md's grounding rule requires a real citation trail. `GenerationResult.invalid_citations_dropped` keeps an audit trail of exactly what was dropped and why, even though none of it is ever exposed to the end user as a real citation.
- **Citation-format design choice, discovered via live testing:** each reference block in `_build_user_message()` tags a chunk with ONLY its real `chunk_id` — no separate numeric index. Live validation (`scripts/validate_generation.py`, this round) caught an earlier prompt version, which did include a bracketed numeric index alongside each chunk, causing gpt-4o-mini to cite a `[27]`/`[29]`-style bracket position in its answer prose instead of the actual `chunk_id` string. `_validate_and_build_result()` did catch and drop the resulting invalid citation — but the model's answer text still parroted the fake index inline, which post-hoc citation validation alone can't clean up. Removing the redundant index removes the confusable second "looks like a citation" marker at the source instead of only catching its symptom downstream.
- **`GENERATION_TEMPERATURE = 0`** — deterministic sampling, because the system prompt demands the model reproduce a `chunk_id` string exactly, character for character, and demands stable behavior when distinguishing similarly-named distinct items (the Pinellas HCD case, #92) — both are grounding-correctness properties, not creative-writing ones, and both only benefit from removing sampling variance.
- **`GENERATION_MAX_OUTPUT_TOKENS = 1000`**, passed as `max_completion_tokens` — `max_tokens` is deprecated in the current chat completions API in favor of `max_completion_tokens` (verified live against `developers.openai.com/api/docs/api-reference/chat/create` this session). Sized against this round's real observed output across 5 live validation queries: ~525 completion tokens total (~105 tokens/query average), largest single answer (the 4-program Pinellas HCD disambiguation case) still well under 300 — 1,000 tokens is ~3-4x headroom over the largest real answer observed, generous without leaving output effectively unbounded against gpt-4o-mini's real 16,384-token ceiling (also verified live this round).
- **`GENERATION_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_SECONDS = 60.0`** — belt-and-suspenders against a genuine network hang, distinct from a clean `openai.APITimeoutError` (already handled by the existing retry/classification logic below). Concretely defines what "graceful timeout degradation" (PLAN.md Phase G's done-criteria) means here: the call can never hang indefinitely, and whatever happens after is a typed, catchable exception, never a silent stall.
- **`not_in_corpus`/`citations` normalization:** if the model claims `not_in_corpus=True` but also returns citations, citations are forced to an empty tuple — folded into the same `invalid_citations_dropped` audit trail, logged at WARNING. Keeps `GenerationResult`'s two fields from ever implying contradictory things ("not addressed in the corpus" + "here's my source").
Why: Every rule above traces directly to a named `.claude/rules/rag.md` requirement (static prompts, context-only answers, cited sources, untrusted-model-output validation) — this entry exists so each one is defensible on its own, not just as "the code does the right thing." The not-in-corpus short-circuit and the citation-format fix both came out of actually running the pipeline live rather than reasoning about it in the abstract — see #95.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #95 — Real end-to-end validation run (`scripts/validate_generation.py`), live against the 342-chunk corpus and real OpenAI calls
Decision: `scripts/validate_generation.py` runs 5 hand-picked queries through the full `retrieve()` + `generate_answer()` pipeline against the live corpus and real OpenAI embeddings + chat-completion calls (deliberately NOT part of the mocked pytest suite — DECISIONS #72's test-mocking precedent — this is the one deliberately-real end-to-end check, the Phase F+G analog of `scripts/validate_retrieval.py`'s Phase E precedent). Confirmed live, this round:
- The DECISIONS #73 Pinellas HCD query (deliberately phrased without naming a specific program, so it's the actual ambiguous case) surfaced both distinct programs and the model named each one separately in its answer, citing each one's own `chunk_id`, rather than silently picking one — #92/#94's design working as intended, not just working in theory.
- A genuine "not in corpus" query — what residents said during public comment on the Duke Energy franchise agreement, content that needs a Granicus meeting transcript, and no transcription job has run against this corpus yet (Tier-1.5 worker built but not run at scale, DECISIONS #84-91) — came back honestly declining via `not_in_corpus=True` rather than hallucinating from the model's general knowledge of Duke Energy franchise disputes elsewhere.
- A hallucinated citation was caught and dropped during this round's testing (the bracket-index citation-format issue documented in #94), confirming `_validate_and_build_result()`'s citation-validation path actually fires on real model output, not just on hand-constructed test fixtures.
- 3 of DECISIONS #73's already-validated on-topic queries (SHIP income limits, municipal deep-energy-retrofit plans, SPHA rent-due terms) re-ran clean through the full pipeline (threshold + dedup + budget + generation stacked on top of the bare cosine-similarity check #73 originally ran), confirming `RELEVANCE_THRESHOLD=0.5` still holds now that more logic sits in front of it.
- Real cost for this round's re-verification run (after the temperature/max-tokens/normalization fixes from #96 were applied): **$0.002707 total**, computed from the chat completion API's real `usage` field (not estimated) plus an estimated query-embedding cost, at gpt-4o-mini's real $0.15/1M input and $0.60/1M output rates and `text-embedding-3-small`'s $0.02/1M rate (all DECISIONS #73/this round, verified live).
Why: A design that's only ever exercised through mocked unit tests hasn't actually proven the grounding contract holds against a real model's real behavior — gpt-4o-mini's actual citation habits (including the bracket-index confusion this run caught) are exactly the kind of thing unit tests with canned responses can't surface. This is the same "verify against reality, then freeze it" discipline DECISIONS #19/#20/#23 already established for the crawlers, applied here to generation.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #96 — `rag-review` pass on `app/rag/retrieval.py` + `app/rag/generation.py`: verdict, 3 applied fixes, 3 deferred findings
Decision: `rag-review` ran an adversarial pass on the staged Phase F+G diff before commit, per this repo's global pre-commit gate. Verdict: no CRITICAL/HIGH findings; the grounding contract (DECISIONS #6, `.claude/rules/rag.md`) judged sound.

**Applied (3, all re-tested):**
1. `GENERATION_TEMPERATURE = 0` — deterministic sampling for a task with grounding-correctness requirements (exact `chunk_id` reproduction, stable multi-item disambiguation), not creative writing. See #94.
2. `not_in_corpus`/`citations` normalization — if the model claims `not_in_corpus=True` but also returns citations, they're forced to empty and folded into `invalid_citations_dropped`, logged at WARNING. See #94.
3. `max_completion_tokens` cap (`GENERATION_MAX_OUTPUT_TOKENS = 1000`) — output was previously unbounded on the output side even though `MAX_CONTEXT_TOKENS` already bounded input. See #94.

All three applied and re-tested: `tests/rag/` went 29 → 31, full suite 412 → 414 (independently re-confirmed this session: `pytest tests/ -q` → 414 passed; `pytest tests/rag/ -q` → 31 passed).

**Deferred (3, each with a stated reason, not silently dropped):**
1. A theoretical `chunk_id`-spoofing vector — if crawled page content itself happened to literally contain the text `chunk_id=` followed by a real ID, could that confuse citation parsing at the source (before it ever reaches the model)? Judged real but low-probability against this project's actual corpus shape (government/nonprofit HTML and PDF text, not user-supplied content designed to exploit this), and judged to deserve its own considered design pass rather than a rushed patch under this round's time pressure. Named here as a future concern, not ignored.
2. A cosmetic token-count undercount somewhere in the char/4 estimation logic — non-functional (doesn't change any accept/reject decision), deferred.
3. Partial retry-path test coverage gap in `generate_answer()`'s retry loop — deferred, not blocking, because the retry-with-backoff shape itself is reused/inherited from `app/embeddings/client.py`'s already-tested pattern (DECISIONS #72's precedent), not fresh unverified logic; the gap is in exercising that inherited shape's edge cases in this new call site, not in the shape's own correctness.
Why: `.claude/rules/rag.md`'s and this repo's global review-cadence rule both require applying findings or consciously deferring them with a note — never a silent skip. All three applied fixes close a real gap (unbounded output, an inconsistent result shape, non-deterministic grounding-sensitive output); all three deferrals are judgment calls about where limited build time is best spent this round, not gaps nobody noticed.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #97 — `GET /sources/{doc_id}`: `doc_id` means `chunk_id`, not a new "document" concept
Decision: `app/api/sources.py`'s `doc_id` path parameter is looked up directly against `chunks.chunk_id`. No new higher-level "document" grouping (e.g. all chunks sharing a `source_url`) was introduced.
Why: `chunk_id` (DECISIONS #58) is the only stable per-item identifier this schema has — there is no separate document table above it, and none of the 8 existing chunkers group multiple chunks under one parent identity. `POST /query`'s citations already return `chunk_id`s (DECISIONS #94), so resolving one straight back to its full record + attribution is the most direct fit for the real use case this endpoint serves: "show me the source behind this citation." Grouping by `source_url` instead would be a real design option for a future "show me everything from this page" browsing feature, but nothing in this round's brief calls for that, and inventing it now would be scope not asked for. Returns the full `chunks` row minus the embedding vector (explicit column list via `CHUNK_COLUMNS`, `.claude/rules/data.md`); 404 if the chunk_id doesn't exist.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #98 — Upload background-processing mechanism: `BackgroundTasks` + a durable `document_uploads` status table, not a Granicus-style polling worker
Decision: `POST /documents/upload` (`app/api/documents.py`) processes uploads via FastAPI's `BackgroundTasks` (in-process, runs after the HTTP response is sent), backed by a new `document_uploads` table (`app/api/schema.py`) keyed on `file_hash` (sha256 of the raw uploaded bytes) that tracks `status IN ('processing','completed','failed')` plus `failure_reason`/`chunk_count`/timestamps. This is deliberately NOT the `granicus_transcription_jobs` pattern (DECISIONS #80/#84-91: a separate table, `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` claiming, a standalone polling worker process, time-based stale-claim recovery).
Why: Those two patterns exist for genuinely different job shapes. Granicus transcription is multi-hour, needs restart-safe resumption mid-job, and is designed to be worked by a separate long-running worker process pulling from a queue that grows faster than one request-response cycle could absorb. An uploaded document is the opposite: one-shot, human-initiated, not recurring, and fast (seconds) — the file is already fully in memory by the time processing starts. Building a claim-table/polling-worker/stale-recovery stack for a seconds-long job would be solving a concurrency and crash-recovery problem this workload doesn't actually have, at real added complexity cost. The `document_uploads` table still gives this a durable, fail-loud status record (`.claude/rules/crawler.md`: never silently stuck) without that machinery: a `'failed'` row (real `failure_reason` stored) or a `'processing'` row that never reached a terminal state (e.g. the process crashed mid-task) both self-heal automatically on the next identical-hash upload attempt, since the pre-check treats both as retry-eligible — the same result Granicus's stale-claim sweep achieves, reached here through the natural retry path of "upload the same file again" rather than a time-based background sweep, which is proportionate given how rare and low-volume this path actually is.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #99 — Uploaded-document attribution: synthetic `upload://<sha256>` source_url, chunk_id from hash + position
Decision: Every `Chunk` produced from an uploaded document (`app/chunking/uploaded_document.py`) carries `Attribution(source_url=f"upload://{file_hash}", retrieval_timestamp=<upload time>, published_date=None)`. `chunk_id` is derived via `make_chunk_id(f"upload://{file_hash}", "uploaded_document", <heading text + document-global position index>)` — never from the chunk's own text.
Why: `Attribution.source_url` (DECISIONS #18) is non-nullable and every existing source populates it with a real crawled URL — an uploaded file has no such URL, and leaving it null wasn't an option even if the field allowed it, nor is inventing a fake `https://` URL that would misrepresent the item as having been crawled from somewhere on the open web. `upload://<hash>` is honest about what it is (an uploaded artifact, not a fetched page) while still being a stable, valid identifier — the same hash that keys the `document_uploads` idempotency table, so a citation pointing at `upload://<hash>` and the upload ledger row are trivially correlatable. `published_date=None` follows DECISIONS #18's nullable-over-sentinel precedent: an uploaded document's real-world effective/publish date is genuinely unknown to this system, not knowable-but-unparsed. Deriving `chunk_id` from hash + position (not content) keeps the same non-negotiable rule every other chunker follows (DECISIONS #58) and is what makes a retry of a `'failed'` upload (DECISIONS #98) reprocess into the identical chunk_ids, upserting cleanly via `embed_and_insert_chunk`'s existing `ON CONFLICT` logic instead of duplicating rows.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #100 — `POST /query` exception → HTTP status mapping
Decision: `app/api/query.py` maps every typed exception `answer_query()` can raise to a distinct HTTP status, never a blanket 500, and never leaks internal exception text into a response body (every `HTTPException.detail` is a static string; the real exception is logged server-side only):
- `UngroundedAnswerError` (checked before its parent) and `GenerationValidationError` → 502 — the model violated its own output contract; an upstream problem, not the caller's.
- `EmbeddingValidationError` → 502 — same "upstream sent something we can't trust" family, different call.
- `EmbeddingInputTooLargeError` → 400 — the one case that genuinely is the caller's fault (their question text was too long to embed).
- `openai.RateLimitError` (after `answer_query`'s own retries are exhausted) → 429.
- `openai.APITimeoutError` / `openai.APIConnectionError` (after retries exhausted) → 504.
- Any other `openai.OpenAIError` (auth failure, malformed request on this app's side) → 500, generic detail.
- Anything unexpected → 500, generic detail, full traceback logged.
Why: rag.md's failure-handling rule ("classify failures before retrying... fail with a reason, surface it") extends naturally to the API boundary: a caller (a future web UI, or Amber scripting against this API directly) needs to be able to tell "the model messed up" (502, maybe retry) from "you asked something invalid" (400, don't retry the same way) from "we're rate-limited" (429, back off) from "something's actually broken here" (500) — collapsing all of that into one status code would erase exactly the signal this rule exists to preserve, and echoing raw exception text back would risk leaking internal details (stack traces, library names) to a public-facing endpoint that CLAUDE.md already flags as unauthenticated.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #101 — `GET /health`: real DB check only, no live OpenAI call; 503 on degraded (api-layer addition, not explicitly spec'd)
Decision: `app/api/health.py` runs a real `SELECT 1` against Postgres and returns `{"status":"ok","db":"ok"}` (HTTP 200) or `{"status":"degraded","db":"error"}` (HTTP 503) — deliberately does not also make a live OpenAI API call on every hit.
Why: `/health` is meant to be polled frequently (container health checks, uptime monitors) — a live external API call on every poll adds real latency and, at high enough poll frequency, real dollar cost, for a signal `POST /query`'s own exception mapping (DECISIONS #100) already surfaces directly and immediately the moment an actual OpenAI failure happens on a real query. The gap this leaves — a green `/health` for the window between an OpenAI key breaking and the next real `/query` call — costs far less at this tool's real polling cadence and solo-operator usage than paying an OpenAI round-trip on every poll would. The HTTP 503 (not just a 200 with `"degraded"` in the body) is an api-layer addition beyond this round's original spec — Fargate/ECS task health checks and most uptime tooling key off HTTP status rather than parsing a JSON body, so it's more useful for near-zero added cost — but it wasn't confirmed against deploy-infra's actual Fargate health-check config before landing here. **Follow-up needed:** deploy-infra should confirm 503 is the status code its container health-check configuration expects before this is relied on for automatic task restarts.
Date: 2026-08-22

## #102 — New dependencies for Phase H: `fastapi`, `starlette`, `uvicorn[standard]`, `python-multipart`, `python-docx`
