## #139 — The two #137/#138 backlog meetings actually transcribed, embedded, and REAL-verified queryable; the always-on worker replaced with a scheduled one-off task to fix #138's cost overage; the schedule itself is built but not yet live — a genuine, well-diagnosed AWS-side blocker, not a code or policy bug

Decision: Amber reset the two orphaned `granicus_transcription_jobs` rows (`City Council`, 2026-07-23 and 2026-08-13, both `status='claimed'` since #138's OOM crashes) herself. The now-correctly-sized `south-st-pete-worker:5` picked both up for real and completed them: **171,552 real transcript characters (Jul 23), 196,833 (Aug 13)**, both `status='completed'`. `scripts/embed_granicus_transcripts.py` run against RDS chunked and embedded both real transcripts: **69 chunks (Jul 23), 92 (Aug 13), 161 inserted / 13 skipped (the 2 pre-existing short committee meetings) / 0 failed.**

**Real-verified against the live deployed app, not assumed — same standard #130 set for the Aug 6 meeting.** `POST /query` against the live `south-st-pete-api` task (public IP, port 8000) with real, content-specific questions built from real excerpts of each transcript (not guessed):
- Aug 13: "Who gave the invocation...?" → correct answer (Pastor Michael Hawkins, Mount Calvary Missionary Baptist Church), citation resolved to `doc_type: granicus_transcript`, correct `source_url` (`MediaPlayer.php?view_id=14&clip_id=7272`), correct `published_date`.
- Jul 23: a rephrased question about Councilmember Gabbard's private-lateral-rebate-program referral → correct answer, citation resolved to `granicus_transcript`, `section_label: "City Council (part 21)"`, correct `source_url`/date.

**A real, non-blocking retrieval gap found during this verification, not swept under the rug.** The *first*, more literally-phrased version of the same Jul 23 question ("which councilmember requested a referral... and to which committee?") reproducibly — not randomly, retried twice with the identical result — cited an unrelated `stpete_grant_category` chunk (`stpete.org/residents/grants___loans/index.php`) instead of the real Granicus transcript chunk, while still producing a factually correct answer (the correct content was evidently present in the assembled context even though it wasn't the top-ranked citation). Reworded, the same underlying fact retrieved and cited correctly. This is a phrasing-sensitive relevance-ranking edge case, not a broken pipeline — flagged here for a future `rag-review` pass per `.claude/rules/rag.md`'s citation-correctness bar, out of scope for this entry to fix.

**Cost-architecture follow-on: replaced the always-on `south-st-pete-worker` service with a scheduled one-off task, per #138's disclosed $88.31/mo overage and Amber's explicit direction to find a cheaper architecture before accepting it.** Research round (no AWS changes) found: `app/granicus/run_worker.py`'s own docstring assumes the job table "tops out around ~52 rows/year"; `scripts/register_granicus_meeting.py`'s docstring confirms meeting registration is 100% manual (Amber resolves URLs by hand, runs the CLI "periodically") — no real-time requirement anywhere in this pipeline. A scheduled task at the *same* `cpu=2048/memory=9216` sizing (load-bearing, per #138's real measured peak — not shrunk), invoked every 15 minutes, billed only while running: **≈$6.19/mo** vs. the always-on **≈$88.31/mo** — combined always-on infra drops to **≈$29.20/mo**, real headroom under the $100/mo alert (#133) instead of $11.32 over it. Fargate Spot on the always-on service (≈$26/mo) was considered and rejected: still pays for 24/7 idle capacity, and introduces interruption risk that only matters cleanly once the stale-claim gap below is fixed.

**Real, separate gap found and fixed in the same round: `recover_stale_claims`/`STALE_CLAIM_THRESHOLD` (`app/granicus/worker.py`) were built and tested (`tests/granicus/test_worker.py`) but never actually invoked anywhere in production code** — not in `run_forever()`, not anywhere else. This is exactly the mechanism that should have auto-recovered the claims #137/#138's OOM crashes orphaned, and would have, within 4 hours, without manual intervention. Fixed via a new bounded entrypoint, `scripts/run_granicus_worker_once.py`: calls `recover_stale_claims(conn)` first, then `process_pending_jobs(conn, client, crawler, max_jobs=2)`, then exits — plus a Postgres advisory lock (`_SINGLE_FLIGHT_LOCK_KEY=84271139`) added beyond the original brief, per independent findings from both `crawler-review` and `deploy-review`: `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` already makes overlapping 15-minute ticks DB-safe, but not cost-safe (two concurrently-billed Fargate tasks) or CDN-etiquette-safe (a fresh per-invocation rate-limiter resetting mid-overlap) — the lock makes an overlapping tick a fast no-op instead.

**New infra, own task family, own IAM role — not reusing `south-st-pete-migrate`, per explicit instruction to keep cost/log/IAM attribution clean between two functionally and cadence-distinct pipelines** (weekly page-recrawl vs. every-15-minute Granicus check): `south-st-pete-granicus-worker` task family (`:1`, same image as `south-st-pete-worker`/`south-st-pete-migrate`, confirmed to already contain `ffmpeg` and the current code per #137), `south-st-pete-granicus-scheduler-execution-role` with `ecs:RunTask` scoped to only this task family (DECISIONS #127's narrower-option precedent, not widened onto the existing recrawl role). Caught and fixed before any image shipped: `deploy-review` CRITICAL — the `Dockerfile` didn't `COPY scripts/ scripts/`, so the new task would have crashed on every single invocation (`.dockerignore` also still excluded `scripts/`, a second, separate fix in its own honest commit once the first fix's `COPY` had nothing to actually copy). Both fixes committed at `9b8968e`-adjacent history this session; the scheduled-worker script/taskdef/IAM templates committed at `c0a7348`, the `.dockerignore` fix at `13ed554`.

**The schedule itself is not yet live — a real, thoroughly diagnosed AWS-side blocker, not a bug in this project's code or IAM policy content.** `aws scheduler create-schedule` for `south-st-pete-granicus-worker-schedule` fails, every attempt, with `ValidationException: The execution role you provide must allow AWS EventBridge Scheduler to assume the role`. Ruled out, each independently checked, not assumed:
- **Trust policy content** — byte-diffed live against the already-working recrawl role's trust policy: identical structure, only the `ArnEquals` schedule-ARN value differs, exactly as expected.
- **SCP restriction** — Amber enabled the SCP policy type mid-session (previously disabled the whole time, so it cannot explain any of the failures that happened before she enabled it either); confirmed live via `list-policies-for-target` on both the Org root and this account that only the default, fully-permissive AWS-managed `FullAWSAccess` policy is attached — no custom, restrictive SCP exists.
- **Organizations trusted-access requirement** — `scheduler.amazonaws.com` does not appear in `list-aws-service-access-for-organization`'s enabled-principals list; this isn't a trusted-access-gated integration.
- **Repeated-write propagation reset** — tested a `create-schedule` call with zero role writes beforehand (no `update-assume-role-policy` call in the same session run); identical failure.
- **Role/schedule-name specificity** — tested against a brand-new, never-before-used role name with a minimal trust policy (no `ArnEquals` at all) and a non-ECS target (SNS, ruling out anything ECS/task-def-specific); identical failure.
- **Account topology** — confirmed via `list-accounts` (required new `AWSOrganizationsReadOnlyAccess`/`AWSSupportAccess` inline permissions on `south-st-pete-infra-dev`, added this session after hitting the default 10-managed-policy-per-user quota and switching to an inline policy instead) that this Organization has exactly one account total, and this account is that Organization's own management account (joined 2026-08-19, the day this project started) — not a separate, inaccessible master account as this session's own diagnosis first, incorrectly, assumed. AWS Support API access requires a paid Premium subscription this account doesn't have (`SubscriptionRequiredException`), so a support case would have to go through the console, not the CLI.
- **Ruled out as account-wide/Scheduler-wide** — the pre-existing, already-working recrawl schedule (role created days ago) was successfully re-submitted via `update-schedule` with its real, unchanged config during this same diagnostic window. EventBridge Scheduler functions correctly in this account for established roles.

**Working hypothesis, not yet confirmed:** this account is 5 days old (created 2026-08-19); EventBridge Scheduler's role-trust validation appears to need unusually long acceptance time for genuinely new IAM roles here specifically — well beyond typical IAM eventual consistency (seconds to low minutes), not yet observed to clear even after 2+ real hours on the actual role. No further code or policy change is expected to fix this — the fix, if the hypothesis holds, is elapsed time and a retry.

**Nothing is broken or at risk in the meantime.** `south-st-pete-worker` (the always-on service, `:5`, `2048/9216`) was deliberately left running (`desiredCount=1`, confirmed live) as a safety net — its real cost continues to accrue at #138's disclosed rate until the scheduled path is confirmed live and it's scaled to zero, but both real backlog meetings were already fully processed by it before this round even required touching it again. `south-st-pete-granicus-worker:1` and its schedule's IAM role are fully built, reviewed, and committed; the only remaining step is one `create-schedule` call once the role clears, no code changes needed.

Why: Closing the loop on #137/#138 with real evidence (real transcript lengths, real chunk counts, a real live-queried, correctly-cited answer) is what actually proves this cycle's fix chain worked end to end for the case that mattered — the same standard #130 set and #137/#138 continued. Wiring in `recover_stale_claims` matters beyond this one incident: it's the mechanism that makes the *next* OOM-shaped or infra-transition-shaped crash self-heal within 4 hours instead of needing another manual diagnostic session like this one. Naming the scheduler blocker in this much diagnostic detail, rather than a vague "still investigating," is what lets whoever picks this up next (Amber tomorrow, or a successor per this project's handoff framing, DECISIONS #136) retry with confidence instead of re-running the same two-hour diagnostic loop from scratch.
Date: 2026-08-24

## #140 — Third real, disclosed secret exposure this project has had: a live-credential-shaped IAM user tag, found during today's scheduler diagnosis — different root cause than #131/#135, needs a different fix

Decision: While diagnosing #139's scheduler blocker, a routine `aws iam get-user --user-name south-st-pete-infra-dev` call surfaced a resource tag on that IAM user: `Key: "AKIAXQWHJDS7OR4MF7JU"` (AWS access-key-ID format), `Value:` a short password-shaped string [redacted here — see note below] — stored in plaintext as IAM tag metadata, readable by anyone holding `iam:GetUser` on this user, which is a far broader and less-audited grant than access to a `.env` file. Not created by this session's work; origin and whether the represented credential is still live are both unknown. Disclosed plainly (the key ID) rather than scrubbed, per `.claude/rules/secrets.md`'s own instruction that a printed secret can't be retracted from wherever it's already been retained — but the password-shaped *value* itself was mistakenly included verbatim in this entry's first draft and has since been redacted from this file (a real audit finding in its own right, see #141): the key ID alone is enough to identify and rotate the credential, and including the paired value here served no purpose the key ID didn't already serve while needlessly repeating the exposure inside this project's own permanent, tracked history.

**A genuinely different failure shape than #131 and #135, worth naming precisely rather than lumping in as "another one of those."** #131 and #135 were both unsafe *reads* of a secret already sitting in the *correct* place (`.env`) — a denylist-shaped grep mistake in both cases, which `.claude/rules/secrets.md` was written specifically to close. This exposure is not a reading mistake at all: the credential was *placed* somewhere that was never a secrets store to begin with. `secrets.md` currently has no rule governing where credentials may be stored — only how to safely query a location already known to hold one. No amount of careful grepping would have prevented this; the exposure existed passively from the moment the tag was set, waiting for any benign, unrelated diagnostic call to surface it.

**Recommendation, not yet actioned this round (out of scope — Amber's to action):** rotate whatever credential `AKIAXQWHJDS7OR4MF7JU` represents immediately, then remove the tag. Separately, `.claude/rules/secrets.md` should gain a placement rule alongside its existing reading rule — never store credentials as resource tags, names, or descriptions on any AWS resource; those are metadata, not access-controlled the way a file is, and stay visible to routine read-only IAM grants that have no reason to expect anything sensitive there.

Why: Two prior incidents this session already established that a transcript is not a retractable medium and the only real mitigation is rotation, not deletion — the same discipline applies here regardless of how the exposure happened. Distinguishing this exposure's root cause from #131/#135's explicitly, rather than filing it under the same existing rule, matters because the existing rule genuinely does not cover this failure mode — treating it as "another reading mistake" would leave the real gap (secrets stored as metadata) unaddressed and let a fourth exposure happen the same way.
Date: 2026-08-24

## #141 — Full credential-exposure audit run across the account and repo, prompted directly by #140; one real finding, self-caught: #140's own first draft had committed the paired secret VALUE, not just the key ID

Decision: Amber asked, after being told to rotate #140's exposed key, how to make sure nothing *else* is exposed the same way. Ran a real, systematic audit rather than a spot check — three parts, each shaped so no command in this pass could itself print a live secret value into the transcript, matching `.claude/rules/secrets.md`'s existing discipline extended to this new context (AWS resource metadata and log content, not just `.env`).

**Part 1 — every taggable AWS resource this IAM user can reach.** `tag:GetResources` (the cross-service tagging API) is not granted to `south-st-pete-infra-dev`, so this went service-by-service instead, using the broad per-service access already granted: IAM users (1 total) and IAM roles (all of them) — only the already-known `south-st-pete-infra-dev` tag, no IAM role has ever had any tag at all; ECS cluster, both services, and all four task-definition families (`worker`/`api`/`migrate`/`granicus-worker`) — no tags on any of them; RDS instance — no tags; ECR repositories (both) — no tags; security groups — no tags. **One real gap, disclosed rather than papered over:** `s3:ListAllMyBuckets` is not granted to this user either, so S3 could not be checked directly — this project's own infra doesn't reference S3 anywhere in `infra/` or `app/`, so the risk is believed low, but "believed low" is not the same claim as "checked," and this is named as a real, uncleared corner rather than silently skipped.

**Part 2 — the full git history, not just the current file tree.** A secret doesn't stop being exposed because a later commit deleted it; `git log -S<term>` (pickaxe search, which reports only commit existence, never the matched line) was run for `AKIA`, `ASIA`, `sk-proj`, `sk-svcacct`, `AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY`, and whether `.env` was ever tracked at all, across every branch (`--all`). Two real hits on `AKIA`, both investigated to a real conclusion rather than left as "found, moving on": (1) this session's own `ee2fad3` (DECISIONS #139/#140) — expected, the key *ID* is meant to be documented for rotation purposes; (2) `3676417`, a much older, unrelated commit ("pytest suite over recorded Legistar fixtures") — real concern until checked, resolved as a genuine false positive: `git grep` narrowed it to `tests/fixtures/legistar/accessible_agenda.html` (a recorded copy of a real, external, unrelated government webpage, not anything this project generated), and a strict-shape check (`AKIA[0-9A-Z]{16}`, the real 20-character AWS key-ID pattern) against that file's content came back with zero matches — the loose 4-letter substring was coincidental page content, not a key. `.env` has never been committed, in any branch, ever. No hits on any other pattern.

**The one real, actionable finding this audit surfaced: #140's own first-draft text committed the paired secret VALUE (the password-shaped string), not just the key ID, into `DECISIONS.md` — a live, permanent exposure in this project's own tracked history, arguably worse than a session transcript because it doesn't age out of relevance the way a chat log does.** Caught by this audit's own git-history pass surfacing `ee2fad3` and prompting a direct check of what that commit actually contains, not assumed safe because "it's just documenting a finding." Fixed this round: the value has been redacted from `DECISIONS.md` (this file, `#140`'s own entry, above) — the key ID alone is retained, since it's sufficient on its own to identify and rotate the credential, and pairing it with the value served no purpose the ID didn't already serve while repeating the exposure. **Note, disclosed plainly, not solved this round:** redacting the *current* file content does not remove the old value from git *history* — the original blob is still reachable at commit `ee2fad3` until that history is actually rewritten (`git filter-repo` or equivalent), which is a real, destructive, hard-to-reverse operation on shared history that this project's own standing rules require explicit sign-off for, not something to do unilaterally mid-audit. The practical mitigation that actually matters here is the same one #140 already recommended: rotate the underlying credential. Once rotated, the old value in history is inert — a record of what a credential *used to* be, not a live risk — which is why history rewrite is named here as an optional follow-up, not an urgent one.

**Part 3 — CloudWatch Logs for this project's own log group (`/ecs/south-st-pete-app`), checking for anything a script might have accidentally printed at runtime, the same failure shape as #131/#135 but in a persistent log instead of a session transcript.** This surfaced a real methodology lesson worth recording for whoever runs a search like this again: CloudWatch Logs' `filter-log-events` `--filter-pattern` treats an unquoted term's special characters (hyphens, `@`) as token delimiters, not literal characters — an unquoted `sk-proj` search does not require the literal substring `sk-proj`, it requires the tokens `sk` and `proj` to each appear *somewhere* in the message, independently. This produced two rounds of apparent hits that were artifacts of the query syntax, not real matches, before re-running every pattern correctly double-quoted (`'"sk-proj"'`) settled it. Once correctly quoted: zero hits for `AKIA`, zero for `sk-proj`, zero for `[redacted]`. The one real cluster of hits, for the broader, shorter `sk-` prefix, resolved to a confirmed false positive by checking the actual surrounding characters (fetched to a local file, never printed to this transcript, per the same shaping discipline) — the full word was `task-credentials`, a completely ordinary AWS/ECS logging term (an ECS task's IAM role credential mechanism), not a truncated real key. Real API keys are never followed by a recognizable English word fragment; a random secret string never spells anything.

Why: An audit prompted by "did rotating this one key actually fix the exposure, or just the one instance we happened to find" is only worth doing if it's real — shaped so it can't itself create a new exposure while running, and thorough enough to check the categories this project's own two prior incident types (#131/#135's unsafe-read shape, #140's wrong-storage-location shape) suggest are actually at risk, not just re-checking `.env` again. Finding and fixing #140's own value-in-history mistake mid-audit, rather than treating the audit as a formality that would obviously come back clean, is exactly the kind of self-check this project's honesty discipline exists for — an audit that only ever confirms what's already believed isn't really an audit.
Date: 2026-08-28

## #142 — `recover_stale_claims`/`STALE_CLAIM_THRESHOLD` (worker.py) were built and tested but never actually called by the live always-on `south-st-pete-worker` service; wired in, reviewed, and real-redeployed

Decision: A real gap found this session, distinct from #137/#138's root causes but downstream of the same incidents: `app/granicus/worker.py`'s `recover_stale_claims`/`STALE_CLAIM_THRESHOLD` — built and unit-tested (`tests/granicus/test_worker.py`) since before #137 — had no production caller anywhere. `scripts/run_granicus_worker_once.py` (the separate, not-yet-live scheduled-task entrypoint built this session for #139's cost-architecture change) calls it correctly, but the currently-running always-on service's own loop, `app/granicus/run_worker.py`'s `run_forever()`, did not. Concretely: #137/#138's two OOM crashes left both orphaned `granicus_transcription_jobs` rows stuck in `status='claimed'` until Amber reset them by hand (documented in #138) — exactly the scenario this function exists to self-heal, and it never ran.

**Fix: `recover_stale_claims(conn)` called at the start of every `run_forever()` cycle, before claiming new work**, in its own inner `try`/`except` — not folded into the existing outer per-cycle handler. **Cadence: every cycle (30s), not gated to a lighter N-cycle interval.** `STALE_CLAIM_THRESHOLD` (4h) already throttles how often a row can *become* reclaim-eligible; running the check more often doesn't cause more reclaims, only more cheap no-op scans — a single bounded, status-indexed `UPDATE ... WHERE status='claimed' AND claimed_at < now() - threshold` against a table that tops out around ~52 rows/year, comparable in cost to the `claim_next_job` query this loop already runs every cycle regardless. Full worked reasoning (including the rejected N-cycle-gate alternative) is in `app/granicus/run_worker.py`'s module docstring, "Stale-claim recovery" section.

**`crawler-review` pre-commit pass: 1 HIGH + 1 MEDIUM + 1 LOW, all applied.**
- HIGH: the first draft folded `recover_stale_claims` into the *same* try/except as `process_pending_jobs` — a persistently failing recovery scan (not just a one-off blip) would propagate past `process_pending_jobs` every cycle, meaning the worker would keep polling and logging but never process a single real job — trading "a row stuck in `'claimed'` until a human notices" for "no job ever gets processed until a human notices," a different-shaped version of the exact bug this fix exists to close. Fixed with an isolated inner `try`/`except`: a recovery failure is logged loud (`logger.exception`) and marks the cycle `cycle_errored` for the heartbeat, but `process_pending_jobs` still runs in the same cycle regardless.
- MEDIUM: the draft cited this very entry, "#142," before it existed — this entry lands in its own commit, after the code is real-redeployed and live-verified below, same precedent as #137's and #139's own entries.
- LOW: the docstring claimed the recovery scan runs on an "index-backed `status`/`claimed_at` predicate" — `schema.py` only indexes `status` (`granicus_jobs_status_idx`); `claimed_at` is filtered on that small subset, not itself indexed. Corrected to describe the real schema (immaterial at this table's real scale, but the claim should be accurate).

Two new mock-based control-flow tests added (`tests/granicus/test_run_worker.py`, no real DB or OpenAI call): recovery runs before job claiming each cycle with the cycle's own connection; a recovery failure is caught, logged, and does **not** prevent `process_pending_jobs` from running in the same cycle or across repeated failures. `68/68` in `tests/granicus/` pass.

**Real, live-verified redeploy — every claim re-checked against the actual running AWS resource, matching #137/#138's own standard:**
- Code committed clean at `6ad65d4` (required before building — `infra/build_push_app_image.sh`'s dirty-tree guard, #137). Built and pushed `south-st-pete-app:6ad65d4`.
- Registered `south-st-pete-worker:6` directly (**deliberately did not run `infra/register_app_taskdefs.sh`**, which also registers `api` — out of scope for this fix, same discipline #137 used), same `cpu=2048/memory=9216` sizing as `:5` (#138) — unchanged, this round touches only the container's `command`-invoked Python code, not its resource sizing.
- `update-service --task-definition south-st-pete-worker:6 --force-new-deployment` against cluster `south-st-pete-dev`. Service events: `"has stopped 1 running tasks"` → `"has started 1 tasks"` → repeated `"has reached a steady state"`. Independently `describe-tasks` on the new live task ARN (`30e070cf22ca4589823eb74191c870cc`) confirmed, not assumed from the API call succeeding: `taskDefinitionArn: .../south-st-pete-worker:6`, `lastStatus: RUNNING`, container `lastStatus: RUNNING`.
- Pulled the new task's own CloudWatch log stream (`worker/south-st-pete-worker/30e070cf22ca4589823eb74191c870cc`) directly — real output: `"granicus worker starting: poll interval=30s, jobs/cycle=1"`, confirming the new binary is what's actually executing, not just that a container is up. No `recover_stale_claims` WARNING line — expected and correct: a live query of `granicus_transcription_jobs` at the same time showed `3 completed / 2 failed / 0 claimed`, i.e. nothing currently stale to reclaim (both #138 backlog rows were already resolved and embedded per #139) — the absence of a reclaim log is consistent with there being nothing to reclaim, not with the code path not running.
- Left `south-st-pete-worker`'s older `:2`-`:5` revisions registered, matching #122/#137/#138's established precedent of not deregistering superseded always-on-service revisions.

Why: This is the same "should work" vs. "REAL-verified" distinction this project has held itself to since #104/#125/#130/#137/#138 — a tested-but-uncalled function is not a working safeguard, and a code fix that isn't rebuilt, redeployed, and independently reconfirmed against the live task isn't actually live. The isolated-try fix specifically avoids trading one silent-forever failure mode (a stuck row) for a subtler one (a worker that looks alive but never processes anything) — worth naming explicitly since it's exactly the kind of "technically fixed the reported gap, introduced a new one" mistake this project's review discipline exists to catch before it ships, not after.
Date: 2026-08-28

## #143 — DECISIONS #139's flagged citation-attribution gap (literal-phrasing Question A) actually root-caused, fixed in three real iterations, and REAL-verified against live RDS + real OpenAI — closes the gap #139 disclosed but deliberately left open

Decision: #139 disclosed, but explicitly deferred, a real retrieval/citation gap: a literally-phrased question about the July 23 2026 meeting's private-lateral-rebate-program referral got a factually correct answer but cited an unrelated `stpete_grant_category` chunk (`7cd28a6bbb71830cd223c734ff205f95`, text `"For South St. Pete CRA"`, 22 characters, `source_url: stpete.org/residents/grants___loans/index.php`) instead of the real Granicus transcript chunk. A rephrased version of the same question ("What did Councilmember Gabbard request...") correctly cited the real chunk (`70fb122bedac082dae2fe2a9f1238dbe`, `granicus_transcript`, `"City Council (part 21)"`, `MediaPlayer.php?view_id=14&clip_id=7246`, published `2026-07-23`). Both were reproducible (asked twice, identical result).

**Root cause, confirmed with real evidence, not assumed:** this is a citation-attribution bug in `app/rag/generation.py`, not a retrieval bug. Reproduced Question A's real `retrieve()` call directly against live RDS (a one-off ECS task in the VPC — RDS is not publicly reachable, DECISIONS #120 — running the real `app.rag.retrieval`/`app.rag.generation` modules, since a direct connection attempt from outside the VPC timed out as expected). The real candidate pool (`CANDIDATE_POOL_SIZE=40`, `RELEVANCE_THRESHOLD=0.5`) for Question A: `7cd28a6b...` ranked #9 at similarity 0.5448 (a 22-char grant-category label — an intentional, previously-reviewed "thin navigational chunk," DECISIONS #27/#32, not a new chunking bug); the real correct chunk `70fb122...` ranked #11 at similarity 0.5408 — both above threshold, both survived dedup and the token budget, both present together in the assembled context handed to `gpt-4o-mini`. The correct chunk's full text (4,481 characters, confirmed live) does contain the real quote: *"councilmember gabbard... respectfully requesting a referral to health energy resilience and sustainability committee or other relevant committee for an update on the private lateral rebate program."* gpt-4o-mini, at `temperature=0`, deterministically cited the wrong chunk anyway — twice in a row through the live API (matching #139's own finding) and twice more in a direct, real chat-completion call reproducing the exact same context.

**Fix attempt 1 (prompt-only, empirically rejected):** strengthened rule 3 of `GENERATION_SYSTEM_PROMPT` to require the model re-confirm a cited chunk's own text states the claim before citing it. Tested for real: 7 live chat-completion calls against the real retrieved context (2 with the original prompt, both wrong; 1 with the strengthened prompt, correct; 5 more with the strengthened prompt in a separate repeat run, all 5 wrong — converging deterministically on a THIRD chunk, `2cf21e685b28b104aec517827a789fdb`, `"City Council (part 7)"`, a passage about a city auditor and a Sierra Club speaker with zero keyword overlap with the real claim). 1/7 correct — rejected as unreliable. (The strengthened rule 3 wording was kept in the final code as a cheap, harmless secondary nudge; it is not what this fix actually relies on.)

**Fix attempt 2, commit `21b3fc2` (code-level, first pass):** `_citation_is_supported()`/`_salient_tokens()` added to `app/rag/generation.py` — a deterministic lexical-overlap check between the answer and a single cited chunk's own text (>=1 shared salient word, `MIN_SALIENT_TOKEN_LENGTH=5`, a stopword list of generic civic-meeting vocabulary), applied in `_validate_and_build_result()` alongside the existing chunk_id-membership check, scoped to fire only when the model returned exactly one membership-valid citation (so it can never interfere with rule 4's legitimate multi-item disambiguation case, DECISIONS #73's Pinellas HCD rank-1/rank-7 pair). A new bounded retry axis, `max_grounding_attempts` (default 2, separate from the existing transport-error `max_attempts`), gives the model one corrective retry with explicit feedback naming the rejected chunk_id before raising `UngroundedAnswerError` for real. `rag-review` pass: 1 HIGH (this DECISIONS entry not yet landed — accepted, same precedent as #142, landing after live verification) + 2 MEDIUM (stopword list expanded with corpus-frequent proper nouns — energy/petersburg/pinellas/duke/etc. — to cut false-positive-overlap risk; content check scoped to single-citation answers only, per above) + 2 LOW (compounding-retry cost documented; a new test for a transport error occurring mid-grounding-retry), all four code findings applied. 497/497 full suite.

**Rebuilt+pushed `south-st-pete-app-migrate:21b3fc2`, re-registered `south-st-pete-migrate:8`, re-verified live against RDS+OpenAI — found a second, subtler real failure.** Question A: attempt 1 correctly rejected `7cd28a6b...` (content-unsupported) and retried; attempt 2 cited a THIRD real chunk, `465a8fe4f8fb3d2152b6f96d8e70fbe6` (`"City Council (part 31)"`, same `source_url`/`published_date` as the correct chunk — same meeting). Fetched its real text: genuinely about Councilmember Gabbard making a referral (`contains 'gabbard': True`, `contains 'referral': True`) — but a DIFFERENT one, later the same meeting (item h1: a Sunrunner TOD overlay referral from the HERS committee to the HLUT committee), not the private lateral rebate program (item g1). `contains 'private lateral': False`, `'rebate': False`, `'health energy': False`. The only shared salient words were "gabbard" and "referral" — a person's name and a generic council-procedure word, both recurring across every agenda item that councilmember raises in one meeting. A 1-shared-word bar wasn't discriminating enough.

**Fix attempt 3, commit `7fc8c59` (code-level, second pass):** `"referral"`/`"referred"`/`"referrals"` added to the stopword list (boilerplate in this corpus — nearly every new-business agenda item is phrased "councilmember X respectfully requesting a referral to Y committee"); `MIN_SALIENT_OVERLAP` raised from 1 to 2 shared salient words, capped at `min(2, total salient answer tokens)`. `rag-review` pass: 1 HIGH + 2 MEDIUM, all explicitly documented as known, disclosed residual gaps rather than chased further (same "disclosed, not silently risked" precedent as #139): the `min()` cap still degrades to a 1-word bar for an answer with only one salient token (no real reproduction of this specific case, this corpus's real answers are full sentences); stopwording "referral" trades away support for a hypothetical meta-query about the referral process itself (no such real query exists in this project's usage); exact string match with no stemming has the same gap for any word-form pair not already listed — a verbatim-supporting-quote mechanism (require the model to quote its source, validate the quote is a real substring of the cited chunk) named as the more structurally robust follow-up, not built this round. 497/497 full suite.

**Rebuilt+pushed `:7fc8c59`, re-registered `south-st-pete-migrate:9`, re-verified live — found a third real failure, this time in the corrective-retry MESSAGE wording, not the validation logic.** Question A: attempt 1 correctly rejected `7cd28a6b...`; attempt 2 correctly rejected `465a8fe4...` too (now below the 2-word bar) — the validation logic worked exactly as intended — but `max_grounding_attempts=2` was exhausted, and `generate_answer()` raised `UngroundedAnswerError` (a safe outcome — no wrong citation was ever returned — but not the correct one either). Tested with `max_grounding_attempts=3`: the model didn't even attempt a third real guess — after attempt 1's rejection, it returned `not_in_corpus=True` directly on attempt 2, despite the correct chunk being right there among the other ~10 uncited chunks. Root cause: the old corrective message offered `"...or set not_in_corpus to true"` as an option in the same breath as naming the rejected chunk_id, and the model took that exit after a single rejection instead of checking the rest of REFERENCE MATERIAL.

**Fix attempt 4, commit `3f00e75` (prompt wording, final pass):** the corrective message extracted to a named constant, `GROUNDING_CORRECTION_MESSAGE_TEMPLATE`, reworded to explicitly instruct checking every other chunk in REFERENCE MATERIAL before conceding `not_in_corpus`, and to explain *why* the rejection happened using the model's own real rejection reasons. Tested informally before committing (ad hoc script replicating the real retry loop with only the new wording swapped in, reusing the real `_validate_and_build_result()`/`_build_user_message()` unchanged): 3/3 real live reruns of Question A now correctly cite `70fb122...`. `rag-review` pass: 1 HIGH (no committed regression test for the actual fix, only the ad hoc script — applied: two new tests, one asserting the rendered corrective message actually contains the "every chunk" instruction, one confirming the `not_in_corpus` escape hatch still fires correctly on a corrective retry when the model genuinely means it, so the reworded push to "keep checking" doesn't make that escape unreachable) + 1 MEDIUM (pushing harder toward "keep checking" could make a genuinely off-topic query burn its one retry and surface as `app/api/query.py`'s HTTP 502 instead of a clean not-in-corpus answer — **flagged to `api-layer`/the orchestrator, not fixed here, cross-domain and out of `rag-pipeline`'s scope**) + 1 LOW (this entry itself, now closed). 37/37 `tests/rag/`, 498/498 full suite.

**Final real verification — rebuilt+pushed `:3f00e75`, re-registered `south-st-pete-migrate:10`, live against RDS + real OpenAI, the actual committed `generate_answer()`, no monkeypatching:**
- **Question A** (the original bug): attempt 1 correctly rejects `7cd28a6b...`; corrective retry correctly cites `70fb122bedac082dae2fe2a9f1238dbe` — the real source chunk. Confirmed 4/4 across separate real live runs (1 in the full 4-query verification pass below, 3 more in a dedicated repeat check) — every one rejects the same wrong chunk on attempt 1, then converges on the correct one.
- **Question B** (already-working phrasing, regression check): unaffected — still correctly cites `70fb122bedac082dae2fe2a9f1238dbe` in a single attempt, no grounding retry needed.
- **Aug 6 2026 regression** (DECISIONS #130, Sky Isle/Veridian loan maturity question, reworded since the original exact question text wasn't recorded verbatim): `retrieved 0 chunks`, `not_in_corpus=True` — unchanged from every run this round including before any of this round's changes; this reflects this specific rephrasing's real retrieval score against the current corpus, not a regression introduced by this fix (retrieval.py was never touched).
- **Aug 13 2026 regression** (DECISIONS #139, invocation question): unaffected — correctly cites `2c1f990c49da3d7c5bef305f14f4625f` (Pastor Michael Hawkins, Mount Calvary Missionary Baptist Church) in a single attempt.

Why: #139 named this gap plainly and deferred it rather than guessing at a fix under time pressure — this entry is that deferred fix, done for real. Three real iterations were needed, not one, because each fix was validated against live RDS + real OpenAI rather than assumed correct from code review alone, and each live rerun surfaced a genuinely new failure a unit-test mock could never have caught: a second wrong chunk sharing a person's name with the real one, then a corrective-retry message that was too quick to offer "give up" as an exit. This is exactly this project's own standing discipline (DECISIONS #104/#125/#130/#137/#138/#142) — "should work" is not the same claim as "REAL-verified" — applied to a generation-layer bug instead of an infra one for the first time. The two remaining disclosed gaps (a terse, name-only answer could still under-check; the corrective message could in rare cases push a genuinely off-topic query into `api-layer`'s 502 path instead of a clean not-in-corpus answer) are named plainly rather than hidden, matching #139's own precedent, and handed to the orchestrator/`api-layer` rather than silently absorbed into a scope this entry doesn't own.

## #144 — Real auth added to `POST /documents/upload` (the long-disclosed "no auth yet" gap), and #143's flagged 502-vs-clean-answer cross-domain question resolved — both real-verified against a live local docker-compose stack, not assumed

Decision: two `api-layer` items this round, both scoped strictly to `app/api/` per the orchestrator's directive — `app/rag/generation.py` (rag-pipeline's just-landed #143 work) was not touched.

**Item 1 — auth on `POST /documents/upload` only.** Mechanism: a single static shared-secret header, `X-Upload-Api-Key`, checked by a new `_require_upload_api_key()` FastAPI dependency (`app/api/documents.py`), wired ONLY onto this one route via `dependencies=[Depends(...)]`. `/query`, `/health`, `/sources/{doc_id}` are unchanged and stay open, exactly as scoped — verified by `git diff --stat` showing only `documents.py`/`query.py` touched in `app/api/`, and live-verified below. Chosen over a session/login system: this codebase has no user/session concept anywhere else, and it's a solo-operator tool (occasionally a handful of coalition members), not a multi-tenant product — a rotatable shared secret is the simplest thing a non-engineer successor can actually operate (generate a random string, put it in `.env`, restart the api container, hand the value to whoever needs to upload; rotation is the same three steps). Read from `UPLOAD_API_KEY` on every request (not cached at import), compared via `hmac.compare_digest` on UTF-8-encoded bytes (constant-time; the bytes form avoids a real `TypeError` the `str` form has on non-ASCII input — see below). Registered as a `fastapi.security.APIKeyHeader` scheme, not a manually-read header, so Swagger UI at `/docs` shows a lock icon and an "Authorize" control on this one route — self-documenting per this project's non-engineer-successor explainability constraint.

Fails CLOSED, not open, on a missing OR still-placeholder key — a deliberate, disclosed deviation from `.claude/rules/deploy.md`'s general "empty/unset value disables a feature" convention. That convention is written for optional features (this project's own SNS cost-alert wiring is the precedent); for an access-control gate, "unset means open" is the textbook insecure default and would silently recreate the exact gap this closes. `_require_upload_api_key()` also treats `UPLOAD_API_KEY=changeme` (the literal value `.env.example` ships, public since it's committed to the repo) the same as unset — a placeholder that's public can never actually protect anything, so accepting it as "configured" would be a false sense of security. Either case returns 503 (server-misconfiguration signal, not 401 — the caller didn't do anything wrong) and logs at ERROR every time, distinguishing "not set" from "still the placeholder" in the log line, since this solo-operator tool has no separate alerting layer — the request log IS the visibility mechanism.

`api-review` pre-commit pass (round 1): 2 HIGH + 2 MEDIUM + 2 LOW, all applied before commit:
- **HIGH**: the project's own static web UI (`app/api/static/app.js`/`index.html`) sent no auth header at all — every upload through the UI itself would 401 with no way to supply a key, contradicting the non-engineer-successor goal this mechanism is supposed to serve. Fixed: added a password-type "Upload key" field to the upload form, persisted to `localStorage` (this browser only, disclosed in the UI's own hint text) so a repeat uploader doesn't re-paste it every visit, sent as `X-Upload-Api-Key` on every upload request.
- **MEDIUM**: `hmac.compare_digest(str, str)` raises `TypeError` on any non-ASCII header byte, which would surface as an unhandled 500 instead of the documented 401. Fixed: compare UTF-8-encoded bytes instead (the bytes overload has no such restriction). Verified with a real live 200-vs-error trace via a direct unit call to `_require_upload_api_key` (see verify below) — httpx itself refuses to encode a non-ASCII `str` into a request header at the transport layer, so this can't be exercised through the real HTTP client; the unit call exercises the actual `compare_digest` code path with the same input shape Starlette would deliver.
- **MEDIUM**: the module docstring didn't disclose that this auth gate does NOT reduce the route's pre-existing full-body-buffering exposure (Starlette parses the entire multipart body before FastAPI resolves any dependency, authenticated or not — an unauthenticated caller can still force the process to buffer an arbitrarily large upload before getting rejected). Disclosed explicitly now; this remains deploy-infra's open proxy-level-limit item, untouched by this change either way.
- **LOW** (this entry) + **LOW** (the `changeme`-as-unset fix, folded into the fail-closed logic above rather than left as a separate TODO).

`api-review` pass 2 (on the findings-applied diff, this project's "fix diffs get their own review" convention): verified all 6 fixes independently (re-ran the full suite, traced route registration order, confirmed `GENERATION_TEMPERATURE=0`, confirmed no accidental exception-handler shadowing) — no new findings.

**Real verification (not "should work"), against a rebuilt local docker-compose `api` image** — the running container was discovered to be 5 days stale (predating #143's own fix) before any of this round's testing began, so it was rebuilt first:
- No `X-Upload-Api-Key` header: real `401`, `{"detail": "Missing or invalid X-Upload-Api-Key header."}`.
- Wrong key (`totally-wrong-key`): real `401`, same body.
- Correct key, fresh file (`authtest2.txt`, marker `QWERTY789`): real `202 Accepted` → background job completes (`docker logs`: `completed, 1 chunk(s)`) → `POST /query` for the marker correctly answers `"The marker code mentioned in the second auth test document is QWERTY789."` with a citation back to `upload://<file_hash>` — the full upload → chunk → embed → queryable path, live, with the real OpenAI key.
- Correct key, already-completed file_hash (`authtest.txt`): real `200` idempotency short-circuit, unaffected by the new auth layer.
- `/health` and `/query` (an unrelated question): confirmed still open, no header required, real `200`s.
- Non-ASCII header value (`"not-the-key-éÿ"`): confirmed via direct unit call (not through `client`/httpx — see MEDIUM finding above) that `_require_upload_api_key` raises `HTTPException(401)`, not an unhandled `TypeError`.

**Item 2 — `UngroundedAnswerError` no longer maps to HTTP 502.** #143 (rag-pipeline) reworded `generate_answer()`'s corrective-retry message to push harder against a premature `not_in_corpus` concession — a real, verified fix for a premature-bailout bug — but #143's own MEDIUM finding disclosed the cross-domain consequence: exhausting that bounded retry can now mean "genuinely tried hard and still couldn't ground it" as easily as "something is broken," and `app/api/query.py`'s existing mapping turned that into a 502 either way.

Investigated live before deciding, per the orchestrator's brief, not assumed: rebuilt the local api image (see above — it had been running pre-#143 code), then ran ~15 real adversarial out-of-scope/near-miss questions against the live stack with a real OpenAI key (e.g. "What was the exact vote count when the City Council approved the CBA framework?", "What specific wage floor did the CBA set for construction workers on the substation project?", CBA-clause and grant-detail near-misses). Against the STALE pre-#143 image, the vote-count question DID reproduce a real 502 (`docker logs`: single chat-completion call, immediate `ungrounded-answer contract violation`, zero corrective retry attempted — confirming that container predated #143's retry-wording fix). Against the current, fixed image, the SAME question — and all ~15 others — resolved cleanly via `retrieve()`'s zero-candidate-chunks short-circuit before `generate_answer()`'s retry loop was ever reached; zero real 502s reproduced post-fix.

Decision: `UngroundedAnswerError` now returns `QueryResponse(not_in_corpus=True, citations=[], answer=<...>)` with HTTP 200, not a 502. Reasoning: `.claude/rules/rag.md` requires "if the context doesn't contain the answer, the response says so explicitly" — an ungrounded-answer exhaustion is exactly that case from the end user's side, and a 502 implicitly invites "try again," which is dishonest here — `generate_answer()` runs at `GENERATION_TEMPERATURE=0` and #143 already reproduced this exact failure mode as deterministic for a fixed query, so retrying would reproduce the identical outcome, not a different one.

`api-review` pass 1 flagged a real HIGH on the first draft of this fix: returning the exact same `NOT_IN_CORPUS_ANSWER` text `generate_answer()` uses for a genuine zero-candidate miss would conflate two different situations — "retrieve() found nothing at all" vs. "retrieve() found real candidates but no citation survived validation" (the latter is exactly the case #143 disclosed residual gaps in — a short-answer edge case in the citation-overlap heuristic, no stemming — meaning this path could in principle also fire on a genuinely answerable question that trips that heuristic, not only on truly out-of-scope ones). A byte-identical response would hide that distinction from everyone, Amber included, with no signal short of grepping server logs. Fixed: `app/api/query.py` now has its own distinct constant, `_UNGROUNDED_ANSWER` ("...found material that might be related... could not produce a source citation it trusts enough... This is inconclusive, not a confirmation that the records are silent on this topic — try rephrasing the question."), separate from `app.rag.generation.NOT_IN_CORPUS_ANSWER`. Server-side visibility is unchanged either way — still logged at ERROR in both `app/api/query.py` and inside `generate_answer()` itself — so Amber's log-based visibility into this failure mode was never reduced, only the end-user-facing text was made honest and distinguishable.

Full test suite: 503/503 (up from #143's 498 — 5 new tests this round: 3 upload-auth branches, 1 non-ASCII-header unit test, 1 for the distinct `_UNGROUNDED_ANSWER` text; net of removing `UngroundedAnswerError` from `test_query.py`'s generic exception→status parametrize table, since it's no longer a generic-status case).

Why: both items close disclosed gaps rather than leaving them as known-risk — #1 closes CLAUDE.md's own "don't upload actually-sensitive documents until this is addressed" caveat for real, #2 closes the cross-domain MEDIUM #143 flagged rather than routing to the orchestrator, not fixed here. Both were real-verified against a live stack with a real OpenAI key rather than assumed correct from code review alone (matching DECISIONS #104/#125/#130/#137/#138/#142/#143's standing discipline), and the live-verification process itself caught a real process gap worth naming plainly: the running docker-compose container had been 5 days stale, meaning any "live" test run against it before the rebuild would have been testing the WRONG code — a mistake this round caught before it produced a false verification, not after.

## #145 — #141's S3 audit gap closed for real: `south-st-pete-infra-dev` granted scoped read-only S3 access, tag scan re-run, clean

Decision: `south-st-pete-infra-dev`'s existing inline policy (`org-and-support-diagnostics`, added mid-#141) was extended with a `S3ReadOnlyAudit` statement — `s3:ListAllMyBuckets`, `s3:GetBucketTagging`, `s3:GetBucketLocation`, scoped read-only, no write/delete actions granted. Re-ran the same tag scan already run against every other service in #141. Real result: `aws s3api list-buckets` returns zero buckets — **this account has no S3 buckets at all**, confirming #141's "believed low-risk, not verified" note was correct, now actually verified rather than assumed. The account-wide credential-exposure audit from #141 is fully closed — every service checked, no gap remaining.

Why: #141 disclosed this as a real, uncleared corner rather than silently skipping it; closing it properly (grant the narrowest permission that answers the question, then actually run the check) rather than leaving "believed low-risk" standing indefinitely is what makes that earlier disclosure worth something.

## #146 — Crawler-health visibility, Layer 1 (data): new `crawl_runs` table gives the 6 Tier-1 crawlers the persistent run history only Granicus had before; `scripts/run_live_recrawl.py` restructured for real per-source failure isolation

Decision: Before this, only Granicus (`app/granicus/schema.py`) had a persistent, queryable run/status table. The 6 Tier-1 crawlers `scripts/run_live_recrawl.py` drives had none — `embed_and_insert_chunks`'s real `PipelineResult` (inserted/skipped/failed, `app/embeddings/pipeline.py`) was computed for real but only ever printed to stdout, never persisted. Worse, investigation confirmed the disclosed suspicion: the old `build_all_chunks_live()` was one monolithic function, so a SINGLE source's crawl exception propagated and killed the ENTIRE script invocation — every source after the failure point silently never ran, and nothing was recorded anywhere (not even stdout) about which source broke or how the others fared. For a script that's the executable an unattended weekly EventBridge-triggered recrawl (DECISIONS #126/#134) points at, that's a real, live blind spot, not a hypothetical one.

Built: (1) `app/db/crawl_runs.py` — new `crawl_runs` table, composite natural primary key `(run_id UUID, source_name TEXT)` (no surrogate id, consistent with every other table in this codebase preferring a natural key — chunks.chunk_id, granicus_transcription_jobs.mp3_url, document_uploads.file_hash), `inserted`/`skipped`/`chunks_failed` nullable INTEGER (NULL only when the crawl step itself raised before any PipelineResult existed — a real computed zero is a distinct, legitimate value these columns must still hold, per data.md), `error_message` nullable TEXT, indexes on `(source_name, started_at DESC)` and `outcome` for the known layer-2 query shapes ("latest status per source", "show every failure"). Deliberately does NOT carry source_url/published_date (crawler.md's mandatory-attribution triad) — that rule binds per-ITEM provenance for content entering the corpus; a `crawl_runs` row is telemetry about a crawl ATTEMPT, and several segments (e.g. `stpete_grant_category_tiles`) span 3 separate live fetches with no single stable URL to force into one column. (2) `scripts/run_live_recrawl.py` restructured: `_build_source_segments()` hoists all 11 crawler-class instantiations up front (network-free per `BaseCrawler.__init__`) and returns 13 named, independently-tried closures — one per chunk-dict-key the old code produced. `estimate()` and `run()` both wrap each segment in its own try/except; one source's failure no longer aborts the rest. In `run()`, every segment's outcome is persisted to `crawl_runs` immediately after that source's own crawl+embed work finishes, not batched at the end.

Failure-isolation scope, what's closed vs. disclosed: fully closed for the 13 tracked segments — a crawl-step exception (`CrawlerStructureError`, `RobotsError`, `requests.HTTPError`, etc.) in any one segment is caught, logged, and recorded as a real `outcome='failed'` row with a real `error_message`; every other segment still runs to completion with its own real `PipelineResult` counts. Two exceptions are deliberately NOT caught-and-continued, both re-raised to abort the whole invocation: `psycopg.OperationalError`/`InterfaceError` (a systemic DB-connection failure isn't one source's problem — every remaining source would fail identically, mirroring `embed_and_insert_chunks`'s own existing philosophy), and `ScopeViolationError` (its own documented contract in `app/crawlers/base.py`: "never catch-and-continue... means either a bug or an undocumented scope expansion" — downgrading it to ordinary per-source flakiness would defeat the point of the exception class). Not touched: `app/granicus/*` and its own tracking table (explicitly out of scope), and the underlying per-instance-not-per-host `RateLimiter` gap the script's own docstring already disclosed (unrelated, pre-existing).

Outcome semantics (crawler-review pre-commit finding, applied): a source only gets `outcome='success'` if its crawl step didn't raise AND it didn't lose 100% of its chunks to embed/insert failures. A source that crawls fine but has EVERY one of its chunks fail to embed (e.g. a broken OpenAI key, a schema mismatch) is recorded `outcome='failed'` with the real non-NULL `chunks_failed` count and an `error_message` explaining the washout — NOT `outcome='success'` just because the crawl step itself didn't throw. The first draft of this table got that wrong (100%-embed-failure rows would have silently read as healthy); caught and fixed before commit, not after.

Real verification (not simulated): `python scripts/run_live_recrawl.py estimate` run live against all 13 segments — all succeeded, real chunk counts printed. Schema applied against the real local docker-compose Postgres; columns/types confirmed via `information_schema`. A REAL failure was induced by temporarily pointing `StpeteIncomeLimitsCrawler`'s URL at a nonexistent path and running `python scripts/run_live_recrawl.py run` for real (real network, real DB, real OpenAI): `ami_table` hit a real 404 `HTTPError` and got a real `outcome='failed'` row (NULL inserted/skipped/chunks_failed, real error_message); all 12 other sources still ran to completion with real `PipelineResult` counts (e.g. `legistar` inserted=0 skipped=74). URL reverted immediately after (`git diff` on that file confirmed clean). The 13 real rows from that run (run_id `e27857fe-e433-4a5a-8c0b-9b25f738b5d1`) were left in the DB as genuine run history. After crawler-review's pass, the `ScopeViolationError`-aborts and 100%-embed-failure fixes were each re-verified live against the real DB with fake-but-network-free source segments (real `record_crawl_run`/`apply_crawl_runs_schema` calls, no mocking of the DB layer) — confirmed 0 rows land for an aborted run, and a total-embed-failure row lands with the correct non-NULL counts and `outcome='failed'`. Full suite: 503 passed.

Review: crawler-review pre-commit pass found 4 HIGH + 2 MEDIUM + 1 LOW. Applied: (HIGH) this entry itself, since every `DECISIONS #146` citation in the code pointed at nothing before now; (HIGH) the outcome-on-total-embed-failure fix above; (HIGH) `record_crawl_run` now re-raises `psycopg.OperationalError`/`InterfaceError` instead of swallowing them — a connection dying specifically during the telemetry write itself must not leave a source with zero rows (indistinguishable from "never attempted"); (HIGH) `ScopeViolationError` now explicitly re-raised in both `estimate()` and `run()` instead of being caught by the broad per-segment `except Exception`; (MEDIUM) clarified/simplified the two `OperationalError`/`InterfaceError` guards around the crawl step and the embed step — the crawl-step one is currently unreachable (no segment touches `conn`) and is now documented as a defensive guard, not implied real DB coupling; the embed-step one was redundant (`embed_and_insert_chunks` already only lets those two types escape) and was removed in favor of natural propagation; (LOW) fixed docstring references to a nonexistent `SOURCE_SEGMENTS` module-level constant and a nonexistent `_run_segments()` function — both now correctly point at `_build_source_segments()`.

Layer 2 (API endpoint, next round) interface note: query `crawl_runs` filtered by `source_name` and/or `outcome`, ordered by `started_at DESC` (both indexed); "latest run per source" is `DISTINCT ON (source_name) ... ORDER BY source_name, started_at DESC`; "all sources from one invocation" is `WHERE run_id = ...`. Columns: `run_id` (uuid), `source_name` (text, one of the 13 names above), `started_at`/`finished_at` (timestamptz, both always non-NULL), `outcome` ('success'|'failed'), `inserted`/`skipped`/`chunks_failed` (nullable int — NULL means the crawl itself never reached the embed step), `error_message` (nullable text, populated whenever `outcome='failed'`).

Why: a health-visibility feature whose own tracking table can silently miss a failure, or whose "success" flag lies about a total embed washout, would just relocate the exact blind spot it exists to close — closing that for real, with a real induced failure and a real fix cycle, is what makes this layer trustworthy for layer 2 to build on.
Date: 2026-08-28

## #147 — Crawler-health visibility, Layers 2-3 (API + UI): `GET /health/crawlers` and a web-UI panel replace "checking means looking at the job-status tables directly"

Decision: Built on #146's `crawl_runs` table (untouched this round, read-only) plus `granicus_transcription_jobs` (app/granicus/schema.py — also read-only, no schema changes to that module).

**API (`app/api/crawler_health.py`, new):** `GET /health/crawlers`, unauthenticated (matches `/health`'s existing shape — this project's auth, DECISIONS #144, is scoped only to `POST /documents/upload` per CLAUDE.md, not a general pattern to extend without a real reason). Returns, per Pydantic response models in `app/api/schemas.py` (`CrawlerHealthResponse`, `CrawlerSourceStatus`, `GranicusHealthStatus` — no bare dicts, `.claude/rules/data.md`): the latest `crawl_runs` row for each of the 13 fixed Tier-1 source names (`SELECT DISTINCT ON (source_name) ... ORDER BY source_name, started_at DESC`, using the exact index #146 built for this query shape), and an aggregated `granicus_transcription_jobs` summary (`GROUP BY status`, plus the most recent failed job's detail). `TIER1_SOURCE_NAMES` is a deliberate, disclosed duplication of `scripts/run_live_recrawl.py`'s 13 segment names, not an import — that script pulls in every Tier-1 crawler class for live network work this read-only API process has no reason to load at startup.

Status vocabulary, shared across both halves of the response (`ok` / `failed` / `never_run`): a source with no `crawl_runs` row reads as `never_run`, distinct from `failed` — "never attempted" and "tried and broke" are different problems (rag.md's "don't guess, be explicit about what's known vs. unknown" spirit, applied here even though this isn't a RAG endpoint). `outcome` is compared by equality against the literal `'success'`, not an allowlist inversion, so any value other than that literal — including one outside the DB's own CHECK constraint, should that constraint ever be loosened — reads as `failed`, never silently `ok`. Granicus reuses the same three-value vocabulary (`never_run` when `total_jobs == 0`, `failed` when any job has `status='failed'`, `ok` otherwise) so the UI can share one visual language across sources and Granicus rather than inventing a second status system for job-level data.

**UI (`app/api/static/`):** a new "Crawler health" panel — a `<ul>` of status rows, one per source plus one for Granicus, fetched from `GET /health/crawlers` once on page load (no polling — this is a solo-operator diagnostic view, not a live dashboard). Matches the existing static UI's actual conventions rather than inventing a new visual language: plain semantic HTML blocks (no table), `textContent`-only rendering for every server-supplied string (`app.js`'s own existing rule, since uploaded-document content can end up in citations and now, by the same logic, a crawler's exception message could end up in `error_message`/`failure_reason`), and new CSS custom properties (`--ok-*`, `--never-run-*`) added alongside the existing `--error-*`/`--not-in-corpus-*` pair rather than a new color system. A failed source's badge is deliberately the loudest-styled state (red, matching the existing `.error` palette) — the brief's "must not require reading fine print" requirement, applied directly: color, not just text, carries the signal.

**Real verification (not "should work"):** compared the endpoint's output directly against a live `SELECT` on the real local Postgres — all 13 sources' latest rows matched exactly, including the real `ami_table` `outcome='failed'` row #146 left in place from its own live-induced-404 test (real `error_message` text: `HTTPError: 404 Client Error: Not Found for url: https://www.stpete.org/residents/housing/income_limits_DOES_NOT_EXIST_crawlrunsverify.php`), and the real Granicus data already in this DB — 2 genuinely `status='failed'` jobs (real Day-5+ worker runs against real Granicus audio, `403 Client Error: Forbidden` on the archive CDN) plus 3 `completed` — correctly aggregated to `granicus.status='failed'`, `total_jobs=5`, and the correct most-recent-failure row by `claimed_at DESC`. Then real-browser-verified via a local `uvicorn` instance + Chrome: screenshotted the rendered panel — `ami_table` shows a red FAILED badge with its real error text, all 12 other sources show green OK badges, and the Granicus row shows FAILED with the real `pending=0 claimed=0 completed=3 failed=2` breakdown and real failure detail — visually distinct at a glance, not requiring the fine print to notice.

`api-review` pre-commit pass: 1 HIGH + 1 MEDIUM + 2 LOW, all applied before commit:
- **HIGH**: `app/api/main.py`'s `lifespan()` only ever called `apply_schema`/`apply_document_uploads_schema` — never `apply_crawl_runs_schema` (app/db/crawl_runs.py) or `apply_granicus_schema` (app/granicus/schema.py), both of which had previously only ever been invoked from one-off scripts. On a genuinely fresh environment (new RDS instance, first API boot before any crawler/Granicus script has run), `crawl_runs`/`granicus_transcription_jobs` wouldn't exist yet, and `GET /health/crawlers` would 500 instead of showing the `never_run`-for-everything state it exists to display — exactly the state a first deploy is actually in. Fixed: `lifespan()` now also calls both `apply_*_schema` functions (idempotent `CREATE TABLE/INDEX IF NOT EXISTS`, same as the two calls already there), consistent with the existing pattern rather than adding new one-off wiring.
- **MEDIUM**: no `tests/api/test_crawler_health.py` existed, breaking this codebase's own one-test-file-per-route-module convention — and it's exactly the gap that would have caught the HIGH above (a fixture that doesn't apply those two schemas first would 500 immediately). Added: 6 real-DB tests (no mocking, same "no precedent for mocking Postgres" convention as `test_sources.py`) covering the full 13-source + Granicus response shape, a monkeypatched genuinely-`never_run` source (can't get a real one — every real Tier-1 name already has `crawl_runs` history in this dev DB from #146's own verification — without deleting real rows, which these tests must never do), a real inserted-then-deleted `failed` row, a real inserted-then-deleted `success` row, and two structural-invariant checks against the live Granicus data (status-count breakdown always sums to `total_jobs`; any failed job flips `status` to `failed` with a non-null failure reason).
- **LOW**: this entry itself — the frontend's `app.js`/`style.css` comments cited "DECISIONS #147" before it existed.
- **LOW**: Granicus's status-count aggregation now sums `total_jobs` from every `GROUP BY` row directly, not from `sum(counts.values())` — so an out-of-vocabulary `status` value (unreachable today given the table's CHECK constraint, but the endpoint must fail loud per crawler.md rather than silently drop rows if that constraint ever loosened) still counts toward the total and logs a warning, rather than silently vanishing from the visible pending/claimed/completed/failed breakdown while still hiding inside a now-inflated total.

Full test suite: 509 passed (up from #146's 503 — 6 new tests, all in `tests/api/test_crawler_health.py`, none removed).

Why: this closes the exact gap CLAUDE.md's own ongoing-operations notes and the README named explicitly — "needs at least basic visibility (a health/status view), not silent failure" — for real, against real data with a real induced failure already on record, not a synthetic happy-path fixture. The API and UI layers were both real-verified independently (a live DB comparison for the API, a live browser screenshot for the UI) rather than one being assumed correct because the other passed.
Date: 2026-08-28

## #148 — `south-st-pete-api` redeployed to current HEAD for real: #143/#144/#147 all live-verified against the actual public endpoint for the first time, not just docker-compose/one-off tasks; closed a real `UPLOAD_API_KEY` task-def gap found along the way

Decision: `south-st-pete-api` had been running task-definition `:2` (image `acea1c6`) since before this entire session's work — every fix from today (#143's citation-attribution fix, #144's upload auth + 502-to-clean-answer fix, #147's `/health/crawlers` endpoint) had only ever been verified locally (docker-compose) or via one-off migrate tasks, never against the live deployed API. This round closed that gap: build → register → force-redeploy → real-verify, deliberately scoped to `south-st-pete-api` only (worker/migrate/granicus-worker/EventBridge scheduler untouched, confirmed after the fact — `south-st-pete-worker` stayed on `:6`, `running=1`, the whole time).

**Real gap found before any build/deploy, not assumed clean:** checking `infra/api-taskdef.template.json` against `app/api/documents.py`'s `_require_upload_api_key()` (which reads `UPLOAD_API_KEY` from the environment, fail-closed on unset or on the `.env.example` placeholder `"changeme"`) confirmed `UPLOAD_API_KEY` had never been added to the template or to `register_app_taskdefs.sh`'s substitution list — #144 was built and verified entirely against docker-compose, and this wiring gap was never caught because the live task-def was never touched until now. Had this redeploy gone out unfixed, the live service would have booted with `UPLOAD_API_KEY` unset and 503'd every upload, silently reintroducing the exact "no auth on upload" risk #144 was built to close (`_require_upload_api_key` fails closed on unset, so no security hole — but a real functional regression for the first real user to hit it).

Fixed: `infra/api-taskdef.template.json` gained an `UPLOAD_API_KEY` env entry (`__UPLOAD_API_KEY__` placeholder, same plaintext-in-taskdef pattern already accepted for `OPENAI_API_KEY`/RDS password per #119/#120 — not a new secrets-hygiene issue); `register_app_taskdefs.sh` gained a presence check plus an explicit rejection of the literal `"changeme"` placeholder (mirrors the app's own check — deploy-review's real MEDIUM finding: a bare presence check would let an un-rotated `.env.example` copy register a taskdef that looks successful and 503s in prod, discoverable only via ERROR logs, not at deploy time). `deploy-review` pre-commit pass on this diff: 1 HIGH (this DECISIONS entry) + 1 MEDIUM (placeholder rejection, applied) + 2 LOW (this runbook's own staleness, addressed below; a pre-existing `sed`-metacharacter exposure shared by every other secret substituted the same way, flagged as not-new).

**A second, self-inflicted bug caught before it reached AWS:** the first version of the `register_app_taskdefs.sh` fix used the message `"...DECISIONS #144's upload-auth key..."` inside a `${UPLOAD_API_KEY:?message}` expansion — an apostrophe inside a `:?` message breaks bash's brace-matching even inside double quotes (confirmed with a minimal repro, unrelated to CRLF/encoding). `bash -n` caught it immediately, before the script ever touched AWS. Fixed by rewording rather than escaping (escaping inside a `:?` message is itself fragile). Two small commits, not amended: `b651a91` (the wiring fix) then `aedeb0b` (the syntax fix) — both real, both landed, `git log` confirms.

**Build/register/deploy, all real:** image built from clean HEAD `b651a91` (dirty-tree guard in `infra/build_push_app_image.sh` confirmed still correct — working tree was clean, verified before build, not assumed), pushed to `516887747774.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/south-st-pete-app:b651a91`. `IMAGE_TAG=b651a91 infra/register_app_taskdefs.sh` registered `south-st-pete-api:3` (confirmed via `describe-task-definition`: `UPLOAD_API_KEY` present in the env-var name list, correct image tag) and, as an unavoidable side effect of the script's shared `for role in api worker` loop, `south-st-pete-worker:7` — registered only, never deployed; `south-st-pete-worker`'s running service stayed on `:6` throughout, confirmed via `describe-services` after the api rollout finished. `aws ecs update-service --force-new-deployment` on `south-st-pete-api` rolled `:2`→`:3`; rollout confirmed `COMPLETED` via `describe-services` (independently re-verified directly, not taken on trust from an interim status claim mid-task), and the running task's `describe-tasks` output confirmed `taskDefinitionArn` ends in `:api:3`, `lastStatus: RUNNING` — the standing "confirm the running task, not just that API calls succeeded" discipline from #137/#138/#139/#142.

**Real verification against the live public IP (52.90.238.109:8000 at deploy time — ephemeral per-task, will change on next replacement per `infra/DEPLOY_NEXT_STEPS.md`'s own disclosed caveat), all three fixes:**
- **#147 (`/health/crawlers`):** `curl`'d live, HTTP 200, real JSON — all 13 Tier-1 sources read `status: never_run` (consistent with reality: `crawl_runs` has only ever been populated against local docker-compose per #146/#147's own verification notes, never against this live RDS — not a bug), Granicus aggregation shows real data (`total_jobs: 4, completed: 4, failed: 0`), matching the live `granicus_transcription_jobs` table's actual state.
- **#144 (upload auth):** real 401 (`"Missing or invalid X-Upload-Api-Key header."`) with no header and with a wrong header; real 202 with the actual live `UPLOAD_API_KEY` (sourced into an env var, never echoed, per `.claude/rules/secrets.md`) — `{"status":"processing", "file_hash":"d510dd..."}`. Confirms the exact gap this entry found and fixed is now closed for real, not just in the template.
- **#143 (citation-attribution fix):** re-ran DECISIONS #143's own literal-phrasing Question A live — "Which councilmember requested a referral about the private lateral rebate program, and to which committee?" — against the live endpoint. Correct answer (Gabbard, Health/Energy/Resilience/Sustainability Committee) with the correct citation, `chunk_id: 70fb122bedac082dae2fe2a9f1238dbe`, matching #143's real fix target exactly — not the pre-fix wrong chunk (`7cd28a6b...`) or the second wrong chunk (`465a8fe4...`) both disclosed in #143's own iteration history.

**Real, disclosed side effect of this round's own verification, not swept under the rug:** the #144 upload-auth positive-case check above uploaded a genuine test document (`test.txt`, contents "test upload content") into the live production corpus via the real `/documents/upload` endpoint. `app/api/documents.py` has no GET/DELETE route for a document by hash — no way to inspect or remove it through the API. `south-st-pete-worker` (still running, `:6`) will pick this up as a real async embedding job on its normal cadence, meaning "test upload content" will become a real, queryable chunk in the live corpus unless removed directly at the DB layer first. Deliberately not cleaned up unilaterally this round — a DB-content delete is `rag-pipeline`'s/the data layer's domain, not `deploy-infra`'s, and wasn't part of this round's brief. Flagged here plainly so it's actioned, not discovered later by a confused query result.

**`infra/DEPLOY_NEXT_STEPS.md` staleness (deploy-review's LOW, applied):** the file's "## Status: done (DECISIONS #122-124)" section read as current with no mention that #144 added a hard `UPLOAD_API_KEY` requirement to any future run of `register_app_taskdefs.sh`, or that today's redeploy had ever happened. Updated with a short pointer to this entry.

Why: this is the first time any of #143/#144/#147's real-world behavior was confirmed against the actual thing Amber or a successor would hit, rather than against a local stack or a one-off task — matching this project's own standing "REAL-verified, not assumed" discipline (#104/#125/#130/#137/#138/#142/#143/#146/#147). The `UPLOAD_API_KEY` gap this round found and closed is exactly the kind of drift this discipline exists to catch: code-reviewed and locally-verified weeks ago, silently absent from the one artifact (the live task definition) that actually determines what runs in production.
Date: 2026-08-28

## #149 — #148's real test upload cleaned out of the live queryable corpus

Decision: #144's live upload-auth verification (successful-auth positive case, run as part of #148's redeploy) left a real test document — `original_filename='test.txt'`, contents "test upload content" — fully processed (`status='completed'`, 1 chunk) in live production `document_uploads`/`chunks`. No delete endpoint exists yet, so removed directly: `DELETE FROM chunks WHERE chunk_id = '4449e58015f98abae231de1d7f150f6a'` and `DELETE FROM document_uploads WHERE file_hash = 'd510dd7...'` — both scoped to the exact known row, not a broader condition, per `.claude/rules/data.md`. Confirmed deleted (both statements' `RETURNING` clauses showed the expected row before commit).

Cheap and unambiguous to remove immediately rather than leave as a known, deferred cleanup item.

## #150 — DECISIONS #139's scheduler blocker escalated to AWS Support (Business plan, real case filed, not just prepared)

Decision: With #139's diagnostic trail fully exhausted (trust policy content, SCPs, propagation timing, role/schedule naming, account topology all ruled out with real evidence) and a final retry this same day showing no change after 4+ real days, Amber confirmed her AWS account is on Business support (covers technical cases, unlike the free default Basic tier) and filed a real case. Case number **178797281900472**, "EventBridge Scheduler rejects trust policy of newly-created IAM roles (ValidationException)" — Technical / EventBridge / Scheduler / System impaired (not Production down — the old always-on worker is still running as a safety net, nothing is actually broken), Web response preference. Full reproduction, the complete ruled-out list, and the real timeline from #139 included verbatim in the case description; Schedule Name/Region/Timestamp fields filled for precision. Filed via the actual AWS Support Console (the orchestrator drove the browser directly, real screenshots and page-text verification confirmed the full description landed intact and the case was created, not just submitted) — the AWS Support API itself remains unreachable from this session (SubscriptionRequiredException on `support:DescribeCases`, needs Premium API access separate from the Business console plan Amber does have).

Why: this closes the loop on #139's own recommendation ("likely needs AWS Support, not more IAM diagnosis") with a real, verifiable action instead of leaving it as a standing suggestion — the case exists, is open, and is traceable by case number, not merely "we should probably do this someday."
