## #160 — Ordered schema migrations and a limited runtime DB role (Phase B, layer B0)

Decision: the schema now changes only through numbered SQL files in `app/db/migrations/`, applied in order by `python -m app.db.migrate up` and recorded with checksums in `schema_migrations`. Editing an already-applied file fails loud. The API no longer runs any DDL at startup. It calls `assert_schema_current` and refuses to boot if a migration is pending.

The API connects as a new role, `app_runtime`, which gets `SELECT/INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE` on the application tables, read-only access to `schema_migrations` (its startup check reads it), and nothing else: no DDL, no ownership. Default privileges give tables created by later migrations the same row-level rights. A table that needs narrower rights (e.g. B3's insert-only audit log) must REVOKE explicitly, and a test sweeps every table against an explicit exceptions list. The password is SCRAM-hashed client-side before the `ALTER ROLE`, so plaintext never reaches a Postgres log. Until now the deployed API logged in as the RDS master user. Only the one-off `south-st-pete-migrate` task (`infra/run_migration.sh`) receives the master credentials. The runner creates the role and sets its password from `APP_RUNTIME_DB_PASSWORD`.

- `0001_baseline` is fully idempotent (`IF NOT EXISTS`, the #154 content_hash lock), so the live RDS and existing dev volumes adopt it unchanged.
- `0002_app_runtime_grants` grants the runtime role its rights. "No CREATE TABLE" relies on PostgreSQL 15+ not granting CREATE on `public` to PUBLIC. RDS is 16.4 (read 2026-10-19) and local is pg16, and a test asserts it.
- The #159 purge is **not** in this layer. `infra/run_migration.sh report` prints a read-only summary first (row counts, and each `document_uploads` filename/status/date) so Amber can confirm nothing real gets deleted. The purge migration lands as a separate commit after that.

Why: Phase C's row-level security and Phase B3's insert-only audit log both require that the API not own the tables (the design-plan review's HIGH #4), and superusers or table owners would read straight past per-org walls. Logging in as master was also simply excess privilege.

Migrations each run in one transaction (no CONCURRENTLY/VACUUM/COMMIT inside a file), with `lock_timeout = 5s` so `up` fails fast instead of stalling the live API.

Pre-commit review (deploy-review) found 1 HIGH: the API couldn't boot as `app_runtime` because it lacked SELECT on `schema_migrations`, and the tests passed only because they all connected as the superuser. It also found 6 MED and 6 LOW. All are applied except: the MED about 0001 not noticing a still-nullable prod `content_hash` (mitigated by running `report` before `up`); LOW about plain-env credentials (unchanged from #119/#120); LOW about `run_migration.sh` wait timeouts. 0002 was edited in place because it had never been committed or run outside local dev.

Verified: 135 tests pass (14 new in `tests/db/test_migrate.py`, including the API's startup check run as `app_runtime`, a SCRAM-stored password, an every-table grant sweep, and a real `app_runtime` login that can read/write rows while CREATE/ALTER/DROP and writes to `schema_migrations` are refused). Also ran `up` against a fresh throwaway database: both migrations applied, schema complete. Not yet run against AWS.
Date: 2026-10-19

## #161 — Keep the old civic-records data (snapshot + archive), superseding #159's purge

Decision: on 2026-10-19 Amber reversed #159 item 2: "I'd really like to keep all of it. I paid for it." The old St. Petersburg corpus represents real OpenAI spend (Whisper transcriptions and embeddings). Nothing is deleted. Instead:
1. **RDS manual snapshot** of `south-st-pete-dev-db`, taken before any migration runs against production. It's the untouchable backup, restorable to a new instance at any time. Amber runs the command.
2. **Archive migration (0003)** moves the old data out of the grant app's way rather than deleting it. The existing `chunks`, `document_uploads`, `crawl_runs` and `granicus_transcription_jobs` tables are **moved** into an `archive` schema with `ALTER TABLE ... SET SCHEMA` (`chunks` is renamed `civic_chunks`), and fresh empty application tables are created in `public`. `app_runtime` gets no access to `archive`, so the grant app can't retrieve, cite or leak any of it.

The first draft copied rows and then deleted the originals. Pre-commit review (deploy-review) found a HIGH, reproduced by running it: under READ COMMITTED, a row the still-running old API committed between the count check and the DELETE would be deleted without ever being archived, silently. Moving the tables is atomic under ACCESS EXCLUSIVE, so there is no copy window. Every column travels with the table, including any prod-only column the repo doesn't know about (a MED from the same review). A test proves byte-for-byte row preservation, including a vector column and an unknown extra column.

Rollout note: the old API image stays live during `up`. After 0003, its reads and writes go to the new empty `public` tables, so switch the service to the new image promptly. A restart of the old image would also recreate empty legacy tables in `public` (harmless to data).

Why: preserves paid-for work and keeps the CBA corpus usable if that tool is ever revived, while still giving Phase C an empty, org-scoped `public.chunks` to build on.
Date: 2026-10-19

#160/#161 production rollout log, 2026-10-19 (each step run by Amber; read-only checks by Claude):
- Baseline (`run_migration.sh report`, before any change): `chunks` 593 (174 `granicus_transcript`), `document_uploads` 0, `crawl_runs` 13, `granicus_transcription_jobs` 4.
- Manual RDS snapshot `south-st-pete-civic-archive-2026-10-19`: verified `available`, 100%, before `up`. *(Item 1 superseded by #167: this snapshot was deleted 2026-10-20. The corpus is kept in `applied-db`'s `archive` schema.)*
- Image `south-st-pete-app:1c3a804` pushed; registered `south-st-pete-api:6` (image 1c3a804, `LOCAL_DB_USER=app_runtime`) and `south-st-pete-migrate:14`.
- `run_migration.sh up`: applied 0001_baseline, 0002_app_runtime_grants, 0003_archive_civic_corpus, exit 0.
- `south-st-pete-api` updated to `:6`. The service reached a steady state, the old task was stopped, and the new task logged "Application startup complete" (the schema check passed as `app_runtime`). Live `GET /health` returned `{"status":"ok","db":"ok"}`, and `/health/crawlers` returns 404 (Phase A code is live).
- After `report`: public `chunks`/`document_uploads` are at 0; `archive.civic_chunks` 593, `archive.document_uploads` 0, `archive.crawl_runs` 13, `archive.granicus_transcription_jobs` 4. These match the baseline exactly, so nothing was lost.
- The deployed API no longer logs in as the RDS master user. Master credentials now live only in the `south-st-pete-migrate` task definition.

## #162 — Cognito settings verified against AWS docs (Phase B, layer B1); production login blocked on HTTPS

Decision: B1 creates the user pool with `infra/register_cognito.sh`. Each setting was checked against AWS documentation on 2026-10-19, which closes the design plan's `[UNVERIFIED]` tags for Cognito:
- **Tier: Essentials.** Managed login (the hosted sign-in pages) requires Essentials or Plus; Lite doesn't include it. Essentials is free under 10,000 monthly active users, so the cost at our scale is $0. [aws.amazon.com/cognito/pricing]
- **PKCE authorization-code grant for a public (no-secret) browser client: supported.** [docs.aws.amazon.com/cognito/latest/developerguide/using-pkce-in-authorization-code.html]
- **Admin-only account creation: supported.** Invited users start in FORCE_CHANGE_PASSWORD and must set a permanent password at first sign-in. [.../how-to-create-user-accounts.html]
- **MFA required, TOTP only: supported.** Managed login prompts for TOTP setup when MFA is required. Account recovery is by verified email, which doesn't conflict with a TOTP factor (the docs forbid recovery and MFA sharing the same email/SMS channel). [.../user-pool-settings-mfa.html]
- **Still ambiguous, and it could go either way.** For our exact case (an admin-invited user with only an email attribute, email as the recovery channel, no TOTP registered yet, TOTP the only MFA factor), the same docs page supports two readings:
  - (a) Cognito returns an MFA_SETUP challenge, and "managed login handles ... the setup of TOTP" before any tokens are issued. This is the intended behavior.
  - (b) Decision-tree step 7 says a user whose only attribute is the recovery channel "can't complete sign-in with MFA required and Amazon Cognito returns an error."
  - Separately, a note says Cognito "issues OAuth 2.0 tokens, even if your user pool requires MFA" on a first sign-in. That note is about self sign-up confirmation, which is disabled here.
  - The B1 acceptance test must distinguish these: a freshly invited user must see TOTP enrollment, with no redirect carrying a code before it. If (b) happens, the fallback is to set account recovery to `admin_only` so email stops being the recovery channel, and re-test. **Caution, per the AWS UpdateUserPool reference:** "If you don't provide a value for an attribute, Amazon Cognito sets it to its default value." A recovery-only update would silently reset MFA, admin-only sign-up and the password policy. Start from `describe-user-pool`, send the full edited configuration back, then re-run `register_cognito.sh` and confirm MFA is ON. Recovery would then go through an admin, which the questionnaire allows with "a designated backup administrator." If tokens arrive before TOTP enrollment, B1 fails and no B5 work proceeds on this pool configuration.
- **Client hardening (pre-commit review):** no direct password API flows (`ExplicitAuthFlows` is refresh only, so sign-in goes through managed login; the acceptance test confirms managed login works without them). The client reads only `email`/`email_verified` and writes only `email`. The sign-in session lasts 15 minutes, because the first sign-in chains a password change and TOTP enrollment and the 3-minute default is too short. `invite` makes the admin retype the address, since a typo would hand the temporary password and all recovery mail to a stranger.
- **Permissions:** the deploy user has no Cognito access. `infra/cognito-iam-policy.json` lists exactly what the script needs, for an admin to attach once. Cognito's built-in email sender has a low daily cap shared by invites and resets; move to SES before onboarding many people at once.

**Gap the design plan missed:** Cognito requires `https` callback URLs, with plain `http` allowed only for `http://localhost`. [.../user-pool-settings-client-apps.html] The deployed API is `http://<task public IP>:8000`: no TLS, and the address changes on every task restart. So:
- B1–B4 are built and tested against `http://localhost:8000/` (the only callback registered).
- **B5 (browser login) cannot work in production** until the app has HTTPS on a stable hostname, e.g. an ALB + ACM certificate + a domain, roughly $16–20/month (unverified estimate).
- Independently of Cognito, the live app already sends everything, including the upload key, unencrypted. Together with #158's Phase C gate, there should be **no real partner data on the deployed instance until HTTPS exists.**
- Amber chose "no domain for now" (2026-10-19). HTTPS gets its own DECISIONS entry when she picks a domain.

Why: verifying vendor behavior before building on it was a design-review requirement (#158 plan, review item 1), and it surfaced a real blocker before any B5 code was written.
Date: 2026-10-19

#162 B1 rollout and acceptance test, 2026-10-19/20 (each AWS step run by Amber; read-only checks by Claude):
- The deploy user had hit AWS's 10-managed-policies-per-user limit. Detached `AmazonEventBridgeSchedulerFullAccess`, which has been unused since the scheduler teardown (#159), then created and attached `coalition-grant-app-cognito` (v2 adds read-only verification actions).
- `register_cognito.sh` created pool `us-east-1_0KsvGD5bp`, domain `coalition-grants-747774`, client `5acluurhis281cmi3jq6136bm2`, and the managed-login branding. Verified afterward (read-only): Essentials tier, MFA ON with TOTP only (SMS/email off), admin-create only, deletion protection, verified-email recovery, a 12-character password minimum. The client has no secret, code flow only, refresh-only explicit flows, the localhost callback, read email/email_verified, write email, a 15-minute auth session, 60-minute tokens, a 12-hour refresh, and revocation on.
- **Acceptance test: PASS, outcome (a).** Amber invited herself. At first sign-in through managed login, she set a permanent password and was then required to enroll TOTP before the redirect to `localhost:8000` with a code. A second sign-in demanded the 6-digit TOTP code before redirecting, so MFA is enforced. Managed login works with refresh-only explicit flows, which resolves review MED1.
- Observed quirk: after enrollment, `admin-get-user` shows `UserStatus=CONFIRMED` but `UserMFASettingList` and `PreferredMfaSetting` are both null, even though TOTP is enforced at sign-in. Don't use those fields to judge whether a user has MFA. The second sign-in prompt is the reliable check.
- Amber's own Cognito account now exists (her real account, reused by the B4 bootstrap). Erica is invited at B4.

## #163 — Deploy user loses IAMFullAccess; separate MFA-protected admin login for rare IAM work

Decision: on 2026-10-20 Amber chose to tighten the deploy user. `south-st-pete-infra-dev` had `IAMFullAccess`, so anyone holding its access keys could grant themselves anything in the account. It was the only IAM user, and root has MFA.

What was done (each step by Amber; read-only checks by Claude):
1. Created a separate admin IAM user with `AdministratorAccess`, a console login (Amber set her own password after a temporary one generated in her terminal, never shown in the session), an authenticator MFA device, and **no access keys** (console only).
2. Signed in to the console as the admin IAM user and removed `IAMFullAccess` from the deploy user. Doing it this way proved the admin login can make IAM changes before the deploy user lost them.
3. Verified afterward: the deploy user is denied `iam:ListAttachedUserPolicies`, while ECS, ECR and Cognito reads still work.

Why it's safe for deploys: the only IAM action the deploy scripts need is `iam:PassRole` for the task execution role, and `AmazonECS_FullAccess` already grants PassRole to `ecs-tasks.amazonaws.com` (read from the policy document on 2026-10-20). **Not yet proven live**; the next `register_app_taskdefs.sh` run confirms it.

Going forward: IAM changes (new policies, attachments) are made as the admin IAM user in the console, or via a short-lived admin CLI session, never by re-granting IAM rights to the deploy user. Process note: a mutating `iam:CreateUser` call was used as a negative test during verification; it was correctly denied, but a read-only check alone would have sufficed, and future verifications stay read-only.

Unrelated, not investigated: two IAM roles in the account weren't created by this project; their origin and permissions are unreviewed.

Why: least privilege for the credential the deploy scripts use. A leaked deploy key can no longer escalate itself.
Date: 2026-10-20

## #164 — Per-request identity: Cognito access-token verification + the app's own `users` table (Phase B, layer B2)

Decision:
- `app/auth/tokens.py` verifies a Cognito access token. The checks are: RS256 only, against the pool's JWKS, matched by `kid`; issuer equal to the pool's issuer URL; `token_use == "access"`; `client_id` equal to our app client; unexpired; and `exp/iat/iss/sub/token_use/client_id` all present. `aud` is not checked, because Cognito access tokens don't have one (design review HIGH #1). ID tokens are rejected even though they're validly signed. `alg: none` and HS256 algorithm-confusion forgeries are rejected before any key lookup. Missing or placeholder `COGNITO_*` settings make verification fail closed.
- `app/auth/principal.py` provides `get_principal`, the FastAPI dependency protected routes will use. It verifies the token, then loads the person from `users` by `cognito_sub` **on every request**, and refuses anyone without an active row. Responses: 401 (no or invalid token, with `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer`), 403 (genuine token, no active account), 503 (auth not configured). Setting `users.disabled_at` cuts someone off on their next request even while their Cognito token is unexpired (design review HIGH #6).
- Migration `0004_users`: the `users` table (`user_id` UUID, `cognito_sub` unique, `email`, `display_name`, `is_platform_admin`, `disabled_at`, `created_at`). `is_platform_admin` grants platform administration only, never document access (questionnaire 2.2, 8.1).
- Layer split adjusted from the design plan: the `users` table moved from B3 into B2, because identity resolution needs it. B3 keeps organizations, memberships, assignments and the audit log.

Hardening from the pre-commit review (api-review: 3 HIGH, 3 MED, 6 LOW; all HIGH/MED applied):
- **Cognito outage → 503, not 401.** An unreachable or malformed key set raises `AuthUnavailableError` (503 plus an ERROR log), so an outage doesn't look like every user signing in wrong. Before the fix, some failures escaped as 500s.
- **The token is checked before any DB connection opens.** Anonymous or junk requests can't exhaust RDS connections. The user lookup uses its own short connection, so no idle transaction is left on the route's connection.
- **`app_runtime` can only SELECT from `users`** (explicit REVOKE in 0004, overriding 0002's default privileges). No API bug can create users, promote a platform admin, or re-enable a disabled account. Those go through the privileged migration/admin role.
- **One row per person:** emails are stored lowercase and trimmed (CHECK) and unique.
- **30 seconds of clock leeway** on `exp`/`iat`.
- **A database outage during the account lookup returns 503**, not a raw 500 (from the second review round).
- **Where the `users` restriction applies:** the deployed API connects as `app_runtime` (`south-st-pete-api:6`, `LOCAL_DB_USER=app_runtime`, verified at registration). Locally, the API and most tests connect as the compose superuser, so `tests/db/test_migrate.py` proves the restriction over a real `app_runtime` connection.
- **Stated limit:** verification is local, so a Cognito sign-out or token revocation does **not** end an access token before its `exp` (up to 60 minutes). The kill switch is `users.disabled_at`. A per-request Cognito check or a `jti` denylist can be added later if self-service revocation must be immediate.

Not done in B2, on purpose: no existing route requires sign-in yet; that's B4, which also passes `COGNITO_*` to the compose and ECS environments. New dependencies: `pyjwt[crypto]==2.15.1`, `cryptography==50.0.2`.

Verified: 185 tests pass. New ones cover real RS256-signed tokens against a local key standing in for Cognito's JWKS, every rejection case (expired, wrong issuer, ID token, other client, other key, unknown or missing kid, each missing claim, alg none, HS256 confusion, tampered payload, garbage, missing or placeholder config), and the dependency over real HTTP against the real `users` table (active, no header, wrong scheme, invalid token, no account, disabled, disabled mid-session, unconfigured).
Date: 2026-10-20

## #165 — Re-scope: a multi-tenant fundraising platform (subscription service); the FLFPC coalition is the first customer

Decision: on 2026-10-20 Amber decided ("This is happening") that the app becomes a subscription service for nonprofits. Each organization gets its own logins, data and history. A specialized agent finds grants matched to each org's profile, reports monthly, fills applications from the org's own data, packages documents, and prompts for uploads and deadlines. It also helps with funder relationships: funder profiles, next-step advice, drafted outreach, and stewardship reminders. The FLFPC coalition (#158 questionnaire) remains the first customer and its requirements still bind. Every org, FLFPC included, is a tenant like any other, with no special-casing in code.

Product rules carried forward and extended:
- **The app prepares; the org acts.** It never submits applications and never sends email to funders on an org's behalf. It drafts, packages, reminds and gives copy-ready answers. Portal filling may use the org's own browser tooling, but a person always clicks submit (questionnaire 7.1).
- **Facts trace to sources.** Advice about a funder cites public sources (e.g. 990-PF filings). Drafts cite the org's own documents. Unsupported claims are flagged, never filled in (#159).
- **"Learning" is per-org memory, never model training.** What the agent learns about a client (outcomes, funder feedback, what worked) is stored in that client's own isolated records and retrieved later. No model is trained or fine-tuned on client data (questionnaire 8.5). **Anything shared across clients comes from public data only.**

Data-model consequences, set in B3 before anything is built on them:
1. The **organization is the data tenant**. Every private row carries `org_id`, enforced by the database (Phase C row-level security).
2. **Billing is separate from tenancy.** A billing account (later) may pay for several orgs (a consultant's clients, a coalition's members). Orgs are not the billing unit.
3. **Public reference data vs private org data are separate tables.** Funder profiles from public filings are shared; an org's relationship with a funder (contacts, interactions, notes, outcomes) is private to that org.
4. **Every form of cross-org access is modeled explicitly:** membership (org staff), multi-org membership (consultants), explicit shares (coalitions), and **vendor support access** (time-limited, consented by the org, audited). There is no master key, and `is_platform_admin` still grants no content access.

Infrastructure gaps found by a read-only audit on 2026-10-20, all of which must be fixed **before any real customer data**:
- **RDS storage is not encrypted at rest** (`StorageEncrypted=false`). AWS can't enable it in place: snapshot, copy encrypted, restore, cut over. It's cheapest now, while the live tables are empty, and it doubles as the first real restore drill.
- **RDS deletion protection is off.** Turn it on.
- HTTPS on a stable domain (#162) becomes mandatory, not deferred.
- #140 (a credential in IAM tag metadata) must be closed.
- What's fine today: 7-day point-in-time backups (latest restorable point is current).

Not changed: Fargate, no K8s/IaC, OpenAI, the B0–B2 work, AWS resource names (renaming live resources is risk without benefit).

Why: Amber's direction. The decisions above are cheap now and expensive to retrofit once tables and customer data exist.
Date: 2026-10-20

## #166 — Tenancy foundation: organizations, memberships, consented support access, database-written audit (Phase B, layer B3)

Decision: migration `0005_tenancy_and_audit` implements #165's data-model rules, as designed and then adversarially reviewed (design review of the local plan §12, 2026-10-20: 3 HIGH, 6 MED, all folded in before code).
- **Tables:** `organizations` (the tenant; `billing_account_id` kept separate from tenancy and unused for now; status active ⇄ suspended → closed, closed being terminal), `org_memberships` (roles org_admin / point_person / contributor / budget_owner / authorized_approver; a consultant is a user with memberships in several orgs), `support_access_grants` (vendor access consented by the customer's org admin, at most 14 days, no backdating), `audit_log`.
- **The database knows who's acting.** `app/auth/context.py` (`get_auth_context`) verifies the token before any DB work, uses one connection per request, loads the active user, sets `app.user_id` with a bind parameter, and **commits immediately** so a route's rollback can't erase it. Triggers stamp `granted_by`/`consented_by`/`created_by`/`actor_user_id` from it and ignore client-supplied values.
- **Grant rules live in the database** (SECURITY DEFINER triggers, pinned search_path, a per-org `FOR UPDATE` lock against races):
  - Only a platform admin creates orgs or changes their status.
  - A platform admin may appoint an org admin only when the org has no active admin (first setup, or recovery when the only admin is disabled), never another platform admin, and can't revoke anyone. So two platform admins together are not a master key (design review H1).
  - Org admins grant any role; point people grant non-admin roles. Nobody grants themselves or a disabled person, and nobody grants in a non-active org.
  - Revocation only: org admins revoke, anyone may leave, there's no un-revoking, and the last active admin can't leave.
  - Support access is consented by the customer's org admin, goes only to an active platform admin, and is never self-granted. It counts only while the org is active and the support user is still an enabled platform admin.
- **Audit can't be skipped or forged by the API** (design review H2): `app_runtime` has no direct rights on `audit_log`. Rows come only from the triggers and `audit_write()`, which stamp the actor themselves and strip any client-supplied `system_job`. Organizations, memberships, support grants **and `users`** (platform-admin promotion, disabling) are all audited.
- **Privileged path:** login roles in the `app_privileged` allowlist (the migration/admin role) may do recovery or bootstrap only with a named `app.system_job` reason, which is recorded in the audit row. Unattributed changes are refused.
- **Phase C contract, defined now:** an active membership requires a non-revoked row, an enabled user and an active org; active support access is defined the same way. Both are exposed as `app_is_member()` / `app_active_support_grant()` SECURITY DEFINER helpers that RLS policies will reuse (avoiding policy self-recursion).
- **Stated limit:** these rules stop application LOGIC bugs. Anyone able to run arbitrary SQL as `app_runtime` (e.g. via SQL injection) could set `app.user_id` to anyone. SQL stays parameterized everywhere.
- **Connection pooling is not allowed** without first switching to per-transaction `set_config(..., true)` (noted in `app/api/dependencies.py`).
- Layer split: `assignments` (per-application, time-limited) moved to D2, because it references `applications`.
- **Vendor staff never become members of a customer org** (Amber, 2026-10-20). Platform admins (the vendor: Amber and future staff) reach customer data only through consented, at-most-14-day, audited support grants, whoever tries to add them as members. Consequence: **Erica is FLFPC's org admin, not a platform admin**, which revises the "both at bootstrap" plan. Anyone with both roles uses two logins.
- Hardening from the pre-commit review (general-purpose; 1 HIGH, 4 MED, 7 LOW, all applied):
  - The HIGH was exactly the vendor-membership path above.
  - "Privileged" is an allowlist role (`app_privileged`, granted to the migration role by 0005), not "anything that isn't app_runtime". Org rules too now require a named `app.system_job` reason; a privileged session without one gets no bypass.
  - The API can't write database-reserved audit actions (`org.*`, `membership.*`, `support_access.*`, `user.*`) or log into an org it doesn't belong to. Every audit row records its source (`database` / `api` / `system` / `operator`).
  - Grant rules refuse to run outside READ COMMITTED, because a stale snapshot under REPEATABLE READ let two simultaneous revocations remove an org's last admin.
  - New orgs always start `active` with no billing.
  - The first-admin and last-admin checks now agree that disabled admins don't count, so a platform admin can appoint a recovery admin when an org's only admin is disabled.
  - `audit_log` gives the API no rights at all, and the privileged role can't update, delete or truncate it by accident (a named reason is required). It is **not** tamper-proof against the database owner, who can disable triggers. True immutability needs export to an external store, a later item.
  - A DB error while setting the acting user returns 503.
  - Every test denial asserts its specific reason.
- Second review round (0 HIGH; 1 MED, 4 LOW, all applied): membership checks (`app_is_member`) ignore platform admins entirely, so a member later promoted to platform admin loses member powers and Phase C RLS inherits the rule; API audit actions must match a strict lowercase `area.event` format; docs no longer overclaim immutability; a test proves the `app_privileged` allowlist contains the migration role and not `app_runtime`.
- Privileged CLI contract: changes that record a person (granted_by, revoked_by, created_by) need `app.user_id` set to the real operator plus an `app.system_job` reason. Audit-only changes (users, org status/billing) accept a reason alone.
- Amber's decision (2026-10-20): **customer org admins may invite their own staff** (self-service). That's built in B4 as a guarded SECURITY DEFINER function plus the API calling Cognito with a narrowly scoped task role.

Verified: 225 tests pass. 38 new ones in `tests/db/test_tenancy.py` run through a real `app_runtime` login acting as specific people and cover every rule above, including forged `granted_by`/`created_by`, the platform-admin master-key attempt, last-admin protection, support-grant consent/backdating/closed-org cases, direct audit reads and writes denied, audit rows with the real actor, users changes needing a reason, and identity surviving a rollback.
Date: 2026-10-20

## #167 — Database moved to an encrypted instance (`applied-db`); product named "Applied"; HTTPS will use a subdomain

Decision (Amber, 2026-10-20):
- **The product is named "Applied."** New infrastructure uses the name (`applied-db`). Existing `south-st-pete-*` resources keep their names (#165: renaming live resources is risk without benefit).
- **Domain:** Applied will use a **subdomain** of Amber's existing domain (one DNS record when HTTPS is set up), so the existing site is untouched. The domain itself isn't named here.
- **Encryption at rest (#165 gap closed).** Done by Amber as the admin IAM user (CloudShell, then the console), with read-only checks by Claude after each step:
  1. Snapshot `applied-pre-encryption-2026-10-20` of `south-st-pete-dev-db` (unencrypted).
  2. Encrypted copy `applied-pre-encryption-2026-10-20-encrypted` (KMS `aws/rds`), verified `Encrypted=true`. This needed the admin IAM user, since the deploy user has no KMS rights.
  3. Restored as **`applied-db`**: `StorageEncrypted=true`, **`DeletionProtection=true`**, 7-day backups, and the same class, subnet group, security group, parameter group, AZ and storage type as before.
  4. Cutover: `.env` `RDS_HOST` set to the new endpoint; registered `south-st-pete-api:7` / `south-st-pete-migrate:15` (both confirmed pointing at `applied-db`, image `1c3a804`); `run_migration.sh status` showed 0001–0003 applied on the new instance; the api service was updated to `:7`, reached a steady state, and the live `/health` returned `{"status":"ok","db":"ok"}`.
  5. `run_migration.sh report` on the new instance: archive 593 / 0 / 13 / 4, which **matches the pre-move baseline exactly**.
- **#163 confirmed live:** `register_app_taskdefs.sh` ran successfully without `IAMFullAccess`, so `AmazonECS_FullAccess`'s PassRole grant covers deploys.
- Writes during the move: none. The live tables are empty and no workers run, so no data could be lost between the snapshot and the cutover.
- **Old instance retired (Amber's explicit choice, 2026-10-20).** Amber ran the deletes as the admin IAM user (console, then CloudShell). Claude checked the result read-only after each step.
  - Deleted: instance `south-st-pete-dev-db` and its retained automated backups (8 dailies, 2026-10-13 to 2026-10-20, as observed in a read-only listing before the delete).
  - Deleted: the console's final snapshot `south-st-pete-dev-db-snapshot`. The console delete created it because the final-snapshot box was left checked.
  - Deleted: the unencrypted snapshots `applied-pre-encryption-2026-10-20` and `south-st-pete-civic-archive-2026-10-19`.
  - **This supersedes #161 item 1** ("untouchable" snapshot). The civic corpus is still kept: it's in `applied-db`'s `archive` schema (counts verified above) and in the encrypted snapshot. Lost: the only copy of the database as it was before migration 0003.
  - Remaining RDS footprint, all encrypted: instance `applied-db` (deletion protection on), manual snapshot `applied-pre-encryption-2026-10-20-encrypted`, and `applied-db`'s automated backups. The automated backups expire after 7 days, which makes the encrypted manual snapshot the only long-lived backup. Restoring it needs the admin IAM user (KMS, item 2).
Date: 2026-10-20

## #168 — #140 closed: the tagged key is dead, the tag is gone, and the deploy user's console login (no MFA) is disabled

Decision (Amber, 2026-10-20). Console checks were done by Amber as the admin IAM user. Claude ran the read-only CLI checks. The deploy user can't read IAM (#163), so the IAM-side facts below are as Amber reported them.
- **Tag:** `south-st-pete-infra-dev` has no tags. The credential-shaped tag from #140 is gone.
- **Key `…F7JU`:** no longer exists. It's not on `south-st-pete-infra-dev`, which has a single access key (a different one, matching the local `south-st-pete` CLI profile; Claude compared last 4 only), and it's not on any other IAM user. Root has no access keys, and root MFA is on. So no IAM user in this account holds `…F7JU`. Whether it was ever this account's key is unknown, and nobody searched CloudTrail for API calls made with it. There's nothing left to rotate.
- **Console login:** `south-st-pete-infra-dev` had console sign-in **enabled without MFA**. The tag's password-shaped value may have been that password. **Disabled.** The deploy user needs only its access key.
- **Use check:** CloudTrail event history (`us-east-1`, about 90 days back to 2026-07-22) shows no `ConsoleLogin` by `south-st-pete-infra-dev`; the only sign-ins were root and the admin IAM user. Limits: only one region was checked, and the window starts about a month before the tag was found (2026-08-24), so it says nothing about earlier use. Strong evidence, not proof.
- **Remaining risk:** the password-shaped value is still in git history (#141, commit `ee2fad3`), which is on `origin/master` of a **public** GitHub repo. Disabling this user's console login makes it useless here. If that value was used as a password anywhere else, change it there. Re-enabling console access for this user requires a new password plus MFA.
- **Prevention:** `.claude/rules/secrets.md` gains a placement rule: never store credentials in tags, names or descriptions, and report credential-shaped metadata by key ID's last 4 characters, with values shown as `[redacted]`.
Date: 2026-10-20

## #169 — Phase B, layer B4: every API route needs sign-in; the shared upload key is retired; bootstrap takes the Cognito sub
Decision (Amber, 2026-10-20), plus the working choices made while building it:
- **Every API route except `GET /health` requires a signed-in user** (`get_auth_context`, #164/#166): `/query`, `/sources/{doc_id}`, `POST /documents/upload`, and the admin routes the rest of B4 adds. `UPLOAD_API_KEY` and its `X-Upload-Api-Key` header are removed everywhere: the routes, the UI's key box, docker-compose, the api task-definition template, `register_app_taskdefs.sh` and `.env.example` (supersedes #144). The old key header is ignored. README.md and CLAUDE.md now describe the new posture.
- **Accepted consequence:** the deployed instance can't upload or query until browser sign-in (B5) ships, and B5 is blocked on HTTPS on a stable hostname (#162). The Cognito app client only allows refresh tokens plus the browser flow (`ExplicitAuthFlows`), so there is no command-line way to get a token. Locally the page can't upload or query either, since it has no sign-in; try the routes with Swagger's Authorize and a token, or through the tests (which exercise real signed tokens). If the Cognito settings aren't configured, every protected route answers 503, locally too. This matches CLAUDE.md, which already bars real partner documents on a deployed instance until Phase C.
- The sign-in check runs before request-body validation, so an anonymous caller gets 401 rather than a validation error for a well-formed but incomplete body. Starlette still reads an upload's multipart body first (the pre-existing buffering exposure, unchanged; limit it at the proxy).
- **Authenticated `/query` still searches every org's documents** until Phase C adds per-organization scoping. Sign-in decides who may ask, not which documents they may see. Any signed-in user's upload joins the one shared corpus until then.
- **"Every route" means API routes.** The static page mounted at `/` stays public, because the future login page has to be reachable before anyone is signed in; it holds no data. The static page carries no secrets.
- **`/docs`, `/redoc` and `/openapi.json` stay enabled.** They list the API's shape (already public in this repo) and contain no data. `get_auth_context` now declares a bearer scheme so Swagger's Authorize button can send a token, which gives a successor a way to try protected routes without a browser login.
- **Bootstrap CLI (B4f) takes `--cognito-sub` and touches only the database.** It gets no Cognito permissions: the migrate-style Fargate task has no task role, and Amber looks up each person's sub herself. This keeps the privileged DB role and the Cognito role in separate places.
- **B4c admin routes** (`app/api/admin.py`): list/create organizations, list/revoke memberships (the route that added a member was removed in layer B4d, per #171), list/grant/revoke support access. They are thin over the 0005 triggers; the database decides who may do what. Choices made while building (api-review 2 HIGH, 6 MED, applied):
  - A platform admin can list every organization's legal name and status (never its content), because creating an org and appointing its first admin needs it. Members list only their own active orgs.
  - Writes that name a person by email check the caller's right to act in the org first and refuse with one uniform 403, so a signed-in stranger can't learn which emails have accounts, who is vendor staff, or which orgs exist.
  - Only trigger-written sentences reach the caller; Postgres's own messages never do.
  - A support grant's expiry is checked on the way in (future, at most 14 days) as well as by the database.
  - Known gaps, deferred: no route to rename or suspend/close an org; no "my memberships" or "my grants" listing (so a contributor leaving, or a support user revoking their own grant, needs an admin to look up the id); members of a suspended or closed org lose list access because the membership helper requires an active org, so an org admin of a suspended org can't revoke vendor access through the API until reactivated.
- Layers: B4a routes + retiring the key; B4b config plumbing; B4c admin endpoints; B4d `invite_user()`; B4e invite endpoint + Cognito + task role; B4f bootstrap CLI; B4g deploy runbook. B4d-f get a design review before code.
Date: 2026-10-20

## #170 — Invitations need acceptance; invite caps (Phase B, design for layers B4d–B4e; superseded in part by #171: N1)
Decision (Amber, 2026-10-20), as refined by a design review of the plan (3 HIGH, 6 MED, all folded in; local plan sections 13.10 and 13.11):
- **Inviting someone never grants access.** An org admin or point person creates a pending invitation; the person gets a role only when they accept. This holds for people who already have an account and for brand-new ones. There is no auto-accept at first sign-in: the API has no first-sign-in event, and one explicit step with one audit trail is simpler. Acceptance protects people who are unaware or never sign in; it does not stop the owner of a mistyped mailbox from accepting, which is why the admin's pending list shows the address and can revoke. Invitations last 14 days; they can be declined, or revoked by the sender or an org admin.
- **The database enforces it.** Migration 0006 removes org admins' and point people's right to insert memberships directly. (Superseded in part by #171: the platform-admin route for appointing a first admin is also removed; the only ways in are acceptance by the invitee and the privileged command-line path.)
- **The response is made uniform, not secret.** Inviting a new person, an existing one, a current member, a disabled account or vendor staff all give the same 202 and each creates a row that is counted against the caps; the unactionable ones simply can never be accepted. Differences that remain: 429 at the cap, 502 if Cognito is down, 403 for non-members, 422 for bad input, and a small response-time difference where a new account is created in Cognito.
- **Caps:** 20 invitations per organization and 10 per inviting admin in any rolling 24 hours, counted from the invitation table; over the cap is a 429.
- **Stated limits:** a platform admin who uses the privileged CLI can disable an org's only admin and appoint a replacement without that person accepting (audited with its reason; confirmed in #171); unaccepted invitations leave a user and a Cognito account behind until a later cleanup (N4); until Phase C row-level security, any signed-in user could read the invitations table directly at the database level, though the API exposes only a person's own invitations and an org admin's list.
- **Plan impact:** B4d (migration 0006) adds the `org_invitations` table, four guarded database functions, a changed membership rule and a reserved audit area; B4e adds the invitation and "my invitations" endpoints and, per #171, removes `POST /orgs/{org_id}/memberships` (#169's description of that route no longer holds). No B4d-f code is written yet.
Open for Amber: N1-N4, answered in #171.
Date: 2026-10-20

## #171 — Answers on #170's open questions: CLI-only first/recovery admin, explicit acceptance for all, notices and cleanup later
Decision (Amber, 2026-10-21):
- **N1:** a platform admin may appoint an organization's first or recovery admin without that person accepting, through the privileged command-line tool only, never through the web API, and always audited (named reason plus the acting operator). Consequences: migration 0006 drops the database rule that let a platform admin insert an `org_admin` membership as `app_runtime`, so the only direct membership paths left are the privileged session with a named reason and acceptance of an invitation. The web route `POST /orgs/{org_id}/memberships` (B4c) is removed in B4e, and the `grant-org-admin` command in B4f is the replacement. This supersedes #170's "database enforces it" bullet and its B4e plan-impact line (route restricted to platform admins), and #169's description of that route. Bootstrap and recovery keep working through the early privileged return in `trg_org_memberships_guard` (a session with a named `app.system_job` reason), which 0006 leaves unchanged; the removal is permanent, so the guard has one fewer branch.
- **Limits:** until B4f ships there is no way to appoint a first admin once the web route is gone, so B4e must not be deployed before B4f. B4c's tests for that route are deleted in B4e and the CLI gets its own. "Audited" means the named reason is always recorded and the operator is recorded as the actor wherever the command sets `app.user_id` (B4f does for grants); a user row created by the command carries the reason only.
- **N2:** brand-new people accept explicitly too, exactly like existing users.
- **N3:** an email notice to an existing user that an invitation is waiting comes later (Phase F). Until then they see it at next sign-in.
- **N4:** invitations nobody accepts leave a user row and a Cognito account behind; a cleanup job comes later. Their pending invitation expires after 14 days either way.
Date: 2026-10-21

## #172 — Invitations built: migration 0006 (Phase B, layer B4d)
Decision: migration `0006_org_invitations` implements #170/#171 as designed (local plan sections 13.10-13.11), with these build choices.
- **Tables and functions.** `org_invitations` (14-day expiry, three mutually exclusive closed states, one open invitation per org/person/role) and four guarded functions the API calls: `invite_user`, `accept_invitation`, `decline_invitation`, `revoke_invitation`. `app_runtime` can only read the table.
- **Membership rule replaced.** A membership is created only by (1) the invitee accepting a matching pending invitation, or (2) the privileged session with a named `app.system_job` reason. Org admins, point people and platform admins can no longer insert one directly. A trigger closes the matching invitation in the same statement, keyed on the inserted row (its `granted_by` is the inviter) rather than on the session setting, so a membership never appears beside an open invitation even in a multi-row insert. The `granted_by` column is the inviter; the audit row's actor is the invitee, and its detail carries the invitation id.
- **Uniform answers.** `invite_user` creates a row for every kind of target (new, existing, member, disabled, vendor staff); the unactionable ones can never be accepted. Authorization is checked first (before any lock is taken), with one refusal for "no such org" and "not yours"; the caps run next and before any lookup of the target. A read-only `invite_precheck(org, role)` applies exactly that check so the API can confirm it before touching Cognito; the API then always supplies the account id, so the only unmetered probe left (an unknown address with no account id, which writes nothing) is reachable only by someone calling the database function directly. Accept, decline and revoke answer "no such invitation" (P0A03) for every failure.
- **Caps.** 20 per organization and 10 per sender in any rolling 24 hours (#170), plus 30 across all organizations in 24 hours, because the whole AWS account shares 50 Cognito emails a day (checked in the AWS docs on 2026-10-21; resets 09:00 UTC) with password resets. The global figure is mine, not Amber's; it is a constant in the migration and moves to SES later. Known cost: three busy customers could use it up and block every tenant's invitations for a day, and it is not serialized, so concurrent invites can overshoot it slightly; Amber to decide whether to count only invitations that send email. Cap errors use SQLSTATE P0A02, "account needed" P0A01.
- **Renewal.** An invitation that expired unaccepted is renewed in place by a new `invite_user` call (new sender and dates, audited as `invitation.create` with `renewed: true`).
- **Email rule.** ASCII-only, lowercased and trimmed in the database, at most 254 characters, so Postgres, Python and Cognito agree on case. Account ids (Cognito `sub`) must be UUIDs.
- **Lock order** is the organization row first, then the invitation, everywhere (a deadlock between accepting and renewing was found in review and fixed).
- **Route removed.** `POST /orgs/{org_id}/memberships` (B4c) is gone, along with its tests; the privileged bootstrap command (B4f) is the only way to seat a first or recovery admin.
- **Stated limits (unchanged from #170):** invitation rows are readable by any signed-in session at the database level until row-level security; the cap and the per-sender lock do not defend against someone running arbitrary SQL as `app_runtime`.
Verified: 289 tests pass, including 30 new in `tests/db/test_invitations.py` and the tenancy tests rewritten to go through invitations.
Date: 2026-10-21
